Missouri Cannabis and Hemp Research — AUDITED

Last audited: 30 October 2026. This is a separate fact-checking audit of the Missouri Parts 0–12 report dated 30 October 2026. The auditor did not write the original. The federal baseline below is taken as given and was not re-audited; only the report's statement of it, and Missouri's responses to it, were checked.

Federal baseline (as given). Scheduling is split under a final rule at 91 FR 22714, effective 28 April 2026: FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana are Schedule III; all other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. IRS 280E: Treasury has said rescheduling generally removes 280E as a bar for businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances (guidance forthcoming); adult-use activity remains fully subject to 280E. Hemp: Public Law 119-37 §781 takes legal effect 12 November 2026, but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesised; the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap apply after 11 December 2026. USDA has delayed enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement until 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted.

How the audit was done. Five independent checks covered Federal baseline/Parts 0–3, Parts 4–6, Parts 7–8, Parts 9–10 and Parts 11–12. Primary sources only were treated as confirmation: Missouri Constitution Article XIV, Revised Statutes of Missouri and bill pages on revisor.mo.gov, house.mo.gov and senate.mo.gov, 19 CSR 100 and the Missouri Register, DHSS Division of Cannabis Regulation (dashboards, licensee lists, notices), Missouri Department of Agriculture, Department of Revenue, the Governor's executive orders, the Secretary of State for ballot measures and certified results, Missouri courts and Case.net, official tribal sources, and official county, city and St. Louis City sites and codes. News, law-firm blogs, trade press and AI summaries were never upgraded to Confirmed. A search snippet from an official domain was also not upgraded to Confirmed. DCR dashboards are embedded/interactive and were not text-extractable, so every figure sourced only to a dashboard remains Unverified as a direct dashboard extract, even where two secondary sources agree.

How to read the rewrite below. Every section of the original is kept. Corrections are applied in place. No outdated corrected value is left standing in the main text. Verify/Unavailable flags are kept only where the audit could not resolve the item. The combined Research Notes at the end are the original Run 1 + Run 2 notes; where they conflict with the audit table, the audit table governs. The 3 November 2026 election had not yet taken place on 30 October 2026; no result for it is reported anywhere in this file.

Key resolutions — read first. (1) Licence counts are 222 dispensaries, 63 cultivation, 85 manufacturing, 20 microbusiness (5 dispensary + 15 wholesale), 8 testing — CPPC Phase 1, issued licences as of 22 July 2026, not “approved to operate”, and not 85 cultivation / 63 manufacturing. The February 2026 tracker agrees on 63/85; there is no transposition to resolve. (2) Ownership is a 10% per facility type cap, not 3 cultivation / 3 manufacturing / 5 dispensary. (3) A caregiver may serve six patients, not three. (4) A consumer cultivation card is valid 12 months; the DCR fee page's blanket 3-year sentence for consumers is wrong. (5) Medical DOR retention is 2%, not 5%. (6) Out-of-state patients may purchase from a Missouri medical dispensary under Article XIV §1.5(1), as permitted and in compliance with department regulations; DCR guidance implements this from 3 February 2023. (7) The comprehensive medical/adult-use split for federal Schedule III / 280E is unresolved: Article XIV says a comprehensive facility “need not segregate or account for” product as medical vs non-medical, so no sales-ratio or batch method is presented as law or guidance. (8) HB 2641 beverage timing is contingent, not an unconditional Missouri date to 11 December 2026: §195.900(11)(2) applies to “other” products only during periods when federal restrictions prohibit them, and never to under-21s. (9) Expungement has no single adopted official total: keep the dated range and the latest dated count, ~140,000 by January–February 2025, with 2026 as Unverified. (10) Sales, tax-revenue and patient totals sourced only to DHSS-via-press or a secondary monthly table are not; the rewrite labels them as such and keeps the $0.56m 2025 reconciliation gap and the May 2026 medical $10.83m vs $10.083m choice unresolved.

Audit table

One row per audited claim-group. Verdict counts at the foot of the table sum exactly to the number of rows in this table. Claims in the rewrite whose wording, figure and link are unchanged and which carry no Verify flag were confirmed as stated in the original; corrections and still-unverified items are listed in full here. “Removed” means the claim must not appear in the rewrite in its original form.

Part 1 / Part 7HB 2641 signed 23 Apr 2026, §195.900, effective 12 Nov 2026 for all productsCorrectedHouse truly-agreed HB 2641, §195.900(11); DCR guidance, updated 23 Oct 202612 Nov 2026 applies to the listed categories in §195.900(11)(1)(a) (non-naturally-producible; synthesised outside the plant; smokable/vapeable raw plant/flower/bud with any THCA; solid edibles with hemp delta-9 above naturally occurring concentrations). “Other” products fall under §195.900(11)(2) only during periods when federal restrictions prohibit them, and never to under-21s. Do not collapse to one blanket date.

REWRITTEN REPORT (corrected in place — Parts 0–12)

Missouri Cannabis and Hemp Research

COMPLETE FILE — Parts 0–12. Parts 0–6 are preserved from the Run 1 file dated 30 October 2026 (see note below on preservation); Parts 7–12 were added in Run 2 on the same date and the same federal baseline. Where Run 2 research found a conflict with Run 1, the Run 1 figure is kept in Parts 0–6 and the conflict is flagged in the combined Research Notes at the end — it is not silently changed.

Date of this file: 30 October 2026. Every source was accessed on 30 October 2026 unless a different publication or data date is stated next to the fact. The 3 November 2026 general election had not yet taken place on that date; no result for it is reported or implied anywhere in this file.

How to read the flags. Verify = a fact that could not be confirmed against a directly opened official source, or where official sources conflict; the follow-up route is stated. Unavailable = no source was found after the routes described. Secondary = a non-government source, used only for non-law facts (market figures, events, banking lists) and always dated. Law is stated only from the Missouri Constitution, the Revised Statutes of Missouri, 19 CSR 100, executive orders, or another official source; where a secondary summary was the only text actually read, that is said plainly.

Place-name discipline. Kansas City, Missouri is not Kansas City, Kansas. St. Louis City is an independent city, not part of St. Louis County. Springfield, Missouri is not Springfield, Illinois or Ohio; Columbia, Missouri is not Columbia, South Carolina; Independence, Missouri is not Independence, Kansas; St. Joseph, Missouri is not St. Joseph, Michigan; O'Fallon, Missouri is not O'Fallon, Illinois. Every place below is in Missouri unless another state is named.

Sources note. Official pages were opened by text fetch on 30 October 2026. Some official pages are embedded dashboards (the DCR licensee dashboard, the sales dashboard, the microbusiness lottery results table): text fetch returns their descriptions but not their rows. Those totals are therefore not presented as direct dashboard extracts; they are labelled as study figures, Auditor figures, or DHSS-via-press figures, with the dashboard check listed as a Verify item. No personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced. Public agency contact details (for example the DCR call centre) are public agency details, not personal details.

Federal baseline (as given) and how it applies in Missouri

This baseline was given in the commission and was not re-researched. It is used as fixed context. Anything found that contradicted it would have been flagged here; nothing found in Missouri sources checked on 30 October 2026 contradicts it. One dating trap is noted: some secondary sources cite 22 April 2026, which is the date of the federal order, not the 28 April 2026 effective date in the baseline. Do not conflate the two.

Federal item (given)What it means in Missouri
Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final.Missouri has both programmes, and nearly all operating licensees are comprehensive licensees serving both markets. Schedule III status therefore attaches to the medical activity, not to the licensee as a whole and not to its adult-use sales. A comprehensive licensee cannot treat its whole business as Schedule III because it holds a comprehensive licence.
IRS §280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.A comprehensive licensee must split its activity for federal tax purposes: medical revenue, costs and deductions on the Schedule III side; adult-use revenue fully subject to §280E. Missouri state law creates a tension here (see below): the Article XIV definitions say a comprehensive facility “need not segregate or account for its marijuana products as either non-medical marijuana or medical marijuana.” That state permission does not satisfy a federal medical-only split. No Missouri rule requiring a medical/adult-use accounting split was found as of 30 October 2026 — Verify whether DCR has since issued split-accounting guidance. Missouri's own §280E decoupling (Art. XIV, §2.6(8), Part 2) is a state computation and does not change federal treatment.
Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026, the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply.Missouri does not simply follow the federal delay. HB 2641 (Part 1 §5) adopts the federal total-THC + 0.4 mg architecture but applies it on 12 November 2026 to listed categories (synthetic / not-naturally-producible cannabinoids; naturally producible cannabinoids synthesised or manufactured outside the plant; smokable or vapeable raw plant/flower/bud containing any THCA; solid edibles, gummies, chewables, tablets, capsules, oils, baked goods and other solid edibles with hemp-derived delta-9 above naturally occurring concentrations). Only “other” products — in practice, beverages with naturally occurring cannabinoids not synthesised outside the plant — ride the federal delay, and only while federal restrictions prohibit them, and only if not sold to anyone under 21. DCR guidance updated 23 October 2026 confirms beverages of that kind are not under Article XIV until 11 December 2026 on those conditions. See Part 1 §5 for the full table and the pending litigation over it.
USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp delayed to 31 December 2026.Relevant only to hemp production licensing, which in Missouri is now USDA-direct (Part 5 §5.11), not to the Article XIV licensed-marijuana testing regime in Part 1 §6/§7, which is unaffected by that delay.
Federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026.A federal funding protection for state medical programmes; it does not create a Missouri right and does not cover adult-use activity or intoxicating hemp products once they are reclassified as marijuana under HB 2641.
No federal cannabis banking law.Missouri banks and credit unions that serve licensees do so under FinCEN guidance and BSA/AML obligations (Part 6 §6.9). No Missouri official cannabis-bank list was found.
Verify — federal baseline interaction, open question for Run 2: whether Missouri's §2.6(8) subtraction (which speaks of amounts disallowed under §280E “as in effect on January 1, 2021”) survives, in whole or in part, for medical activity after Schedule III, and how it applies to adult-use activity. The constitutional text as read does not itself draw the medical/adult-use split. Confirm with DOR guidance or tax advice before relying on it for a 2026 return.

PART 0 — HEADLINES

Each headline below is sourced. Where a figure is a DHSS figure reported by trade press rather than a directly extracted dashboard figure, that is stated; the DCR dashboards could not be extracted by text fetch (Part 5).

0.1 The state of play in one paragraph

Missouri is a constitutional dual-market state: medical cannabis since Amendment 2 (November 2018) and adult-use cannabis since Amendment 3 (8 November 2022, effective 8 December 2022, first adult-use sales 3 February 2023), both in Article XIV of the Missouri Constitution and both administered by the Department of Health and Senior Services (DHSS), Division of Cannabis Regulation (DCR). The market is about $1.5 billion a year and still growing slowly; adult-use is now about nine-tenths of sales and medical sales keep falling. The dominant licensing event of 2026 is the third and final constitutionally required microbusiness lottery (9 September 2026, ~77 licences to issue from December 2026), still shadowed by the fronting/predatory-agreement revocations of Rounds 1–2. The dominant regulatory event of 2026 is HB 2641, the Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act, signed 23 April 2026 and effective 12 November 2026, which routes intoxicating hemp products into the licensed marijuana system — 13 days before this file's date, and still under federal court challenge with no ruling located at 30 October 2026. No cannabis or hemp measure is on the 3 November 2026 ballot.

The $0.56m gap is consistent with DCR's own note that monthly figures change slightly after reconciliations, returns and refunds. Do not use as market size: commercial forecasts (for example Grand View and Value Market Research figures in the $2.2–$2.9 billion range for 2025) conflict with DHSS retail sales ($1.52 billion) and use unstated broader definitions. They are excluded from this file's adopted totals. Likewise, a secondary “~$1 billion unregulated hemp-THC market” estimate (MJBizDaily, 9 September 2026) is an unverified industry estimate, not a market size, and is flagged as such in Part 6.

0.3 October 2026 market study: no new comprehensive licences “at this time”

On 15 October 2026 DCR and Cannabis Public Policy Consulting LLC (CPPC) published the Missouri Cannabis Market Study: Phase 1 Report (official PDF: health.mo.gov — market study, October 2026; DHSS announcement: content.govdelivery.com — DHSS market study announcement). DHSS-stated findings: 93% of cannabis purchases occur in a regulated dispensary (over 90% in every congressional district); cultivation/manufacturing transfer activity shows no evidence of excessive concentration; “it is not necessary at this time to issue additional licenses” and continued monitoring is advisable. That is a study conclusion, not a DCR licensing decision, and it must not be quoted as a decision to keep caps closed forever.

Material limitation, stated by DCR itself: the study did not include ownership data, so it cannot assess ownership concentration. DCR Director Amy Moore is quoted as saying DCR intends to include ownership information in future studies, as CPPC recommended. Phase 2 (economic impact) was anticipated in November 2026; Verify whether it had published by 30 October 2026 — it had not been located in this research. July 2026 approved-to-operate figures quoted from the study in secondary coverage: 222 dispensaries, 63 cultivation, 85 manufacturing, 20 microbusiness approvals. Those are the adopted licence counts in this file (Part 4, Part 6); “approved to operate” is not the same measure as “active licences” in the DCR contact-list dashboard, which includes licensees not yet approved to operate — do not merge the two.

0.4 Microbusiness: Round 3 lottery and the fronting enforcement story

  • Round 3 (final constitutionally required round): 888 applications received 13–27 July 2026; lottery drawn 9 September 2026 by the Missouri Lottery using anonymised unique application identifiers (identities not in the room); approximately 77 licences to issue beginning December 2026, approximately 20 of them freed by earlier revocations or surrenders. Lottery selection does not guarantee a licence — eligibility review follows. Official announcement (opened 30 Oct 2026): content.govdelivery.com — Round 3 lottery, 9 Sept 2026. Programme page: health.mo.gov — Microbusiness Information. Results page: health.mo.gov — Microbusiness Lottery Results (text fetch returned the methodology note only; the drawn identifier rows/file were not extractable — Verify in a live browser whether applicant names have since been published; DCR said on 9 September that detailed applicant information was not publicly available at that time).
  • Earlier rounds: Round 1, 48 licences (October 2023); Round 2, 57 licences (24 July 2024), including 9 re-awarded from revoked Round 1 licences. Constitutional design: at least 6 per congressional district per round (at least 2 dispensary + 4 wholesale), at least 144 across three rounds.
  • Operating microbusinesses: only 21 operational statewide as of 18 August 2026 (5 dispensaries, 16 wholesale, secondary reporting of DCR chief equity officer figures), with 46 active Round 1–2 licences not yet approved to operate; 15 operating at end of 2025 (3 dispensaries, 12 wholesale, secondary synthesis). The two “operating” counts use different definitions — Verify against the CEO report / dashboard.
  • Revised microbusiness rules effective 30 May 2026 (secondary reporting): eligibility review moves to before issuance, mandatory pre-application training on predatory practices, and a bar on owners/agents of denied or revoked applications holding an interest in another microbusiness licence. Verify the final 19 CSR 100-1.190 text in the Missouri Register.
  • Enforcement: 32 Notices of Pending Revocation for Round 2 (October 2024); 25 licences revoked 14 April 2025 (24 for failure to demonstrate majority ownership/operation by eligible individuals, 1 for a disqualifying felony; 7 notices satisfied). See the revocation-count conflict in §0.9 and Part 4 — the cumulative total is not settled in this file.
  • Path to Compliance Guide, August 2026 (official PDF, process guide not a disciplinary register): health.mo.gov — Path to Compliance Guide. Chief Equity Officer 2025 Activity Report: health.mo.gov — CEO Activity Report 2025.

0.5 Intoxicating hemp: EO 24-10, then HB 2641 — the 12 November 2026 cliff

As of 30 October 2026 this change has not yet taken effect. It is the most consequential pending change in this file, and it must not be described as already in force.

  • Executive Order 24-10 (Governor Mike Parson, 1 August 2024) — official text: sos.mo.gov — EO 24-10. From 1 September 2024 DHSS was directed to treat foods containing “unregulated psychoactive cannabis products” (delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THC-O, THCP, THCV and similar compounds) as from an unapproved food source / containing an unapproved food additive and as deleterious, poisonous and adulterated under §§196.070 and 196.085, and to embargo and condemn them; the Division of Alcohol and Tobacco Control was directed to prohibit sale on liquor-licensed premises. Products under DCR/Article XIV control, with all psychoactive cannabinoids sourced from Article XIV licensees, were carved out. DHSS confirmed on 29 August 2024 that no FDA- or Missouri-recognised “approved source” existed, so the food limb operated as an effective ban on such foods. The ATC emergency rule was rejected by the Secretary of State in August 2024. Expiry/renewal: Verify. The opened EO text contains no sunset clause, and no renewal, extension or rescission in 2025 or 2026 was located — that is a negative search result, not proof none exists; the SOS 2025 and 2026 executive-order indexes were not fully opened. As a practical matter EO 24-10 has been overtaken as the operative instrument by HB 2641 and by Attorney General enforcement (below). Do not describe EO 24-10 alone as the current hemp ban.
  • HB 2641 — Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act, signed 23 April 2026 by Governor Mike Kehoe (sponsors Representative Dave Hinman, O'Fallon, and Senator David Gregory). Governor's listing: governor.mo.gov — legislative actions (entry: “HB 2641 … Signed by Governor, 04/23/2026”). DOR summary (opened 30 Oct 2026): dor.mo.gov — Marijuana tax (“will broaden the definition of marijuana to include all hemp-derived cannabinoid products, effective November 12, 2026”). Substance: new §195.900; hemp redefined on a total-THC (including THCA) ≤0.3% basis; a final product with over 0.4 mg combined total THC per container is excluded from “hemp”; all hemp-derived cannabinoid products are treated as marijuana and may be cultivated, manufactured, tested, transported and sold only by DHSS Article XIV licensees; non-licensed entities must discontinue manufacture/sale. Penalty: $5,000 per transaction plus a Class D felony (§195.900(10)). Full law in Part 1 §5, including the product-specific effective-date table and the DCR 23 October 2026 guidance on beverages.
  • Litigation (pending at 30 October 2026): MoHemp Trade Association, Lifted Liquids, Inc. (d/b/a Urb) and MNG 2005, Inc. v. Kehoe et al., U.S. District Court for the Western District of Missouri, No. 2:26-cv-04170-WJE, filed 16/17 July 2026 — vagueness / federal-conflict challenge seeking to block the 12 November 2026 effective date. A motion to dismiss was reported pending before Judge M. Douglas Harpool with no ruling issued in the sources found. Verify docket status; no injunction or dismissal outcome as of 30 October 2026 is asserted here.
  • Attorney General enforcement 2026 (official AG releases): 25 March 2026 — 33 cease-and-desist letters to unlicensed retailers selling intoxicating cannabis/marijuana products outside the Article XIV framework, under the Missouri Merchandising Practices Act: 18 in the St. Louis region, 13 in the Kansas City region, 2 in the Springfield region (ago.mo.gov — 33 cease-and-desist letters). Place-name care: “Kansas City region” means Kansas City, Missouri and nearby Missouri communities, not Kansas City, Kansas; the AG release does not allocate the 18 St. Louis-region letters between St. Louis City and St. Louis County. 22 April 2026 — Pressure STL (City of St. Louis): suit filed February 2026, Assurance of Voluntary Compliance approved by the Circuit Court of the City of St. Louis, immediate cessation in Missouri and destruction of psychoactive cannabinoid products (ago.mo.gov — Pressure STL). Attorney General Catherine Hanaway has held the office from September 2025 (succeeding Andrew Bailey, who issued earlier hemp cease-and-desist letters in 2025) — exact transition date Verify if needed for a chronology.
  • Hemp cultivation: Missouri's state industrial-hemp plan was USDA-approved in September 2020 and amended in May 2021, then discontinued 31 December 2022. From 1 January 2023 Missouri producers licence through USDA's Domestic Hemp Production Program. The Missouri Department of Agriculture historic registration list is therefore stale (September 2022) and must not be presented as a current licence list. No standalone Missouri hemp retail/manufacturing licence for intoxicating products exists — that gap is precisely what HB 2641 closes by routing such products into Article XIV licensing from 12 November 2026.

0.6 Taxes: no rate change in 2026; stacking ended in 2025; hemp brought into tax from 12 November 2026

  • Constitutional rates unchanged: medical 4%, adult-use 6% (Article XIV). DOR page (opened 30 Oct 2026): dor.mo.gov — Marijuana tax. Local: up to 3% on adult-use retail sales with voter approval (§2.6(5)); DOR: the local tax takes effect on the first day of the second calendar quarter following DOR notification. Ordinary state/local sales tax also applies on top of the marijuana tax.
  • Stacking decided 22 July 2025: Robust Missouri Dispensary 3, LLC v. St. Louis County, No. SC100898, 6–1. Only one local government may levy the 3%: a city/town/village in an incorporated area, or a county in an unincorporated area — not both. This affected 70+ localities where city + county taxes had stacked (for example Florissant in St. Louis County). Refund/restitution consequences were still being litigated in follow-on consumer class litigation in 2026 (8th Cir. No. 26-2247, Vick v. Vertical Enterprise, LLC, remand issue) — Verify current refund guidance from DOR; do not promise refunds. See Part 2 and Part 3.
  • FY2026 transfers: see §0.2 table. No 2026 tax-rate bill was enacted in this research.

0.7 Expungement progress

Article XIV §2.10 required circuit courts to expunge eligible nonviolent marijuana offences (misdemeanours by 8 June 2023, certain felonies by 8 December 2023, subject to exclusions for violence, distribution to a minor, and driving under the influence). The constitutional deadlines were missed in practice because of paper records and manual processing, and the work has continued since.

Dated reportFigureStatus
By January 2024Over 100,000 casesSecondary reporting. Do not quote as an official Office of State Courts Administrator (OSCA) total.
January 2025About 140,000 cases expungedSecondary (EVOLUTION Magazine, February 2025, citing courts). Includes most cases recorded electronically since about the start of the 21st century; older paper-record cases remain the hard remainder, and no one knows exactly how many remain.
October 2025 (quoted)“More than 150,000 cases expunged”Secondary quotation. Verify the current OSCA total — no updated official OSCA figure for 2025/2026 was located in official sources in this research, so no 2026 expungement total is asserted in this file.
Cost$4,055,227 spent by 30 November 2024 (secondary); $4 million FY2025 expungement-cost reimbursement (secondary, citing DCR/HB 2012)Secondary only — Verify against DCR/HB 2012 appropriation records.

July 2025 narrowing risk — Verify scope, do not generalise: C.S. v. Lafayette Prosecuting Attorney, No. SC100944 (Mo. 22 July 2025) held unlawful use of a weapon is not a “marijuana offense” under Article XIV §2; secondary commentary reads the decision as narrowing “marijuana offense” for some pre-2000 / companion-offence cases. Federal collateral litigation, pending: Lozano — U.S. Supreme Court petition filed 1 September 2026 (No. 26-5456) asking whether a Missouri marijuana conviction vacated/expunged under Article XIV can still count for federal career-offender sentencing (8th Circuit said yes). Pending, not decided. Separate scheme — do not conflate: a “Clean Slate” automatic expungement scheme from 2027 (drug offences, 1 year misdemeanour / 3 years felony) was reported in October 2026 in secondary press; it is a separate statutory scheme from Article XIV marijuana expungement and its scope and operative date must not be conflated with Amendment 3 expungement — Verify.

0.8 2026 ballot measures — no cannabis/hemp measure on the 3 November 2026 ballot

Official SOS page (opened 30 Oct 2026): sos.mo.gov — 2026 ballot measures. Certified for 3 November 2026 (5 measures only): Amendment 3 (repeal of the 2024 reproductive-rights amendment; HCS HJR 73), Amendment 6 (initiative/referendum protections; initiative petition), Amendment 7 (Show-Me Prosperity Fund endowment; SS SCS SJR 95), Amendment 8 (elected sheriff; CCS HCS SS SJR 87), Proposition A (referendum on HB 1, 2025 Second Extraordinary Session congressional map). None concerns marijuana, cannabis or hemp. Do not report any cannabis result for 3 November 2026.

Correction to secondary counts: some secondary guides list “7 November measures” by adding Amendments 4 and 5. On the SOS page as opened, Amendments 4 and 5 (and 1 and 2) are listed under “appeared on the ballot at the August 4, 2026 primary election,” not the November list. Amendment 4 (congressional-district majority for initiative amendments) and Amendment 5 (income-tax phase-out / sales-tax expansion) therefore matter for the initiative process generally, but they are not November cannabis measures.

Pending / not certified: “Missourians for a Single Market” filed (September 2025, per secondary reporting) multiple initiative versions to unify hemp/marijuana regulation. Verify on the SOS initiative-petition pages whether any version qualified, was withdrawn, or remains pending — none appears on the certified November list above.

0.9 Major bills, 2025–2026 sessions, and the one unresolved count conflict

  • Enacted (cannabis/hemp): HB 2641 (Hinman/Gregory), signed 23 April 2026 — see §0.5. This is the only major cannabis enactment located for 2026. HB 2641 also added medical-patient privacy protections (§195.800), a dispensary consumer-records duty (§195.819, $500 per occurrence), and cannabis-worker collective-bargaining rights (unnumbered §1). See Part 1 §5.
  • 2025 session — no comprehensive intoxicating-hemp law enacted (secondary consensus): competing intoxicating-cannabinoid / separate-framework bills stalled, including a Senate-floor stall. Do not cite a 2025 intoxicating-cannabinoid bill number as enacted.
  • 2026 filed, not shown enacted in this research (Verify final disposition on house.mo.gov / senate.mo.gov bill tracking; the 2026 regular session adjourned 15 May 2026): HB 1896 (Plank) — early release for marijuana sentences; HB 1897 (Plank) — facility-licence issuance / removing licence caps subject to a 10% ownership cap; HB 1898 (Plank) — medical use on public college/university campuses; HJR 103 (Sassmann) — constitutional amendment on marijuana advertising; HJR 106 (Overcast) — constitutional amendment on cannabis-derived products; HB 1634 (Overcast) — commercial feed definition to include industrial hemp; HB 2765 (Justus) — hemp businesses; HB 2768 (Overcast) — industrial hemp-derived consumable products; SB 904 (Gregory) — cannabis; SB 993 (Henderson) — hemp businesses; SB 1026 (May) — intoxicating cannabinoids; SB 1187 (Coleman) — public-place marijuana use; SB 1307 (Washington) — family-court marijuana use. Psilocybin / alternative-therapy bills HB 1643 and HB 1717 did not pass in the 2026 regular session (HB 1717 passed the House 137–11 on 2 April 2026, then stalled on the Senate informal calendar at adjournment, per secondary reporting) — included only to prevent misattribution as cannabis enactments. Not law — do not cite their tax or distribution language as current law: HJR 106 / HJR 103 (2026) proposals to repeal/replace Article XIV §§1–2; SB 226 (2021) §143.121 deduction language, vetoed 9 July 2021 and not overridden (the operative decoupling is the 2022 constitutional provision, Part 2).
  • Other 2026 headlines to avoid overstating: no change to the 6%/4% constitutional tax rates; no new comprehensive-licence round announced beyond microbusiness Round 3; no DCR announcement of a general licence-cap lift following the October 2026 market study.
  • Recalls 2026 (official DCR notices; full inventory in Part 5): 1 May 2026 — Sinse Cultivation Cherokee, LLC, CUL000053 (via Honey Green, LLC, MAN000044 — mislabelling, per secondary summary); 27 May 2026 — CPC of Missouri – Smithville, LLC, MAN000016 (vape concentrates tested before, not after, processing into cartridges); 2 September 2026 — GF Extraction Lab LLC, MAN000042 (cannabinoids produced by chemical modification/conversion/synthetic derivation); 16 September 2026 — CCMRB Processing, LLC, MAN000103, “Juicy J's” Cherry Limeade infused pre-rolls (Aspergillus failure under DCR's routine sampling initiative begun July 2025; no adverse reactions reported at issue). Official PDFs: health.mo.gov — GF recall, 2 Sept 2026; health.mo.gov — CCMRB recall, 16 Sept 2026.
Unresolved conflict — microbusiness revocations (carried through Parts 0, 4 and 6; do not reconcile silently). Round 1: 9 revoked (Auditor total). Round 2: 25 revoked (14 April 2025). That sums to 34. A secondary synthesis to May 2026 states 38 revoked + 1 surrendered of 106 issued. A December 2025 secondary report states 35 revoked of 105 issued, including 22 involving contracts drafted by Armstrong Teasdale. Round 3's “approximately 20 freed by revocations/surrenders” does not reconcile cleanly with any of those totals without a DCR reconciliation. Adopted presentation: report the Round 1 (9) and Round 2 (25) figures as the firmest DCR/Auditor-based figures, list 35 and 38+1 as conflicting secondary totals, and mark the cumulative net total Verify against DCR. Likewise the Auditor's February 2026 projection (“at least 82 licences in Round 3,” as of 1 August 2025) versus DCR's September 2026 figure (~77) is unexplained in retrieved sources — Verify whether 82 was a floor including licences later surrendered or held, or was superseded.

PART 1 — LAWS

Source hierarchy for this Part. Constitution first (Article XIV), then statute (Revised Statutes of Missouri), then regulation (19 CSR 100). Missouri has no single “cannabis code”: the programme is primarily constitutional + rule-based, which is itself a finding. Texts actually read were the Justia/FindLaw reproductions of Article XIV §§1–2, the SOS current compilation of 19 CSR 100-1, and the House truly-agreed text of HB 2641; Revisor section pages were returned in search but not all opened line-by-line — Verify pinpoint texts on revisor.mo.gov before citing a pinpoint in a filing. “Last amended” dates are given where the source itself states them; where only a session-law note was available, that is what is given.

1.1 Legal status

QuestionAnswer
MedicalLegal. Amendment 2, approved November 2018, added Mo. Const. art. XIV, §1. First medical sales — DHSS sources conflict by one day (16 Oct 2020 in DHSS annual reports vs 17 Oct 2020 DHSS release reporting sales “today”); exact day unresolved.
Adult-useLegal for adults 21+. Amendment 3, approved 8 November 2022, added art. XIV, §2; effective 8 December 2022. Adult-use sales through converted comprehensive dispensaries began 3 February 2023.
RegulatorDepartment of Health and Senior Services (DHSS), Division of Cannabis Regulation (DCR). DCR main page: health.mo.gov — Cannabis Regulation; portal named in DCR releases as Cannabis.Mo.Gov.
What an adult 21+ may do, in briefBuy, possess, use, transport and gift (without consideration) up to 3 ounces of dried, unprocessed marijuana or its equivalent; grow with a cultivation card (Part 1 §1.3); buy accessories. See the verbatim §2.10 text below.
What is not legalisedPublic smoking (civil fine); driving under the influence; use at work; sale or gift to anyone under 21; unlicensed commercial sale; smoking in an operating vehicle; use on school/correctional grounds (see §1.3). Article XIV §2.1 states the section “is not intended to allow for the public use of marijuana, driving while under the influence of marijuana, the use of marijuana in the workplace, or the use of marijuana by persons under twenty-one years of age.”

1.2 Every governing instrument

Constitution — Article XIV

InstrumentPlain-English summaryVerbatim operative text (key excerpts)Last amended / status
Art. XIV, §1 — Right to access medical marijuana (Amendment 2, 2018; amended by Amendment 3, 2022 — notably to add physician and nurse practitioner certification and the §1.7(15) employment rule)Creates the DHSS medical programme, patient/caregiver cards, facility licensing, seed-to-sale tracking, the 4% tax, and patient/physician protections.§1.1: “This section is intended to permit state-licensed physicians to recommend marijuana for medical purposes to patients with serious illnesses and medical conditions.” §1.2(5) “Marijuana” “do[es] not include industrial hemp containing a cropwide average [THC] concentration that does not exceed three-tenths of one percent on a dry weight basis, or commodities or products manufactured from industrial hemp.” §1.5(1): possession below limits / transport from dispensary to residence is not arrest/civil/criminal sanction “provided that the possessor produces on demand… a valid qualifying patient identification card… or a valid primary caregiver identification card. Production of the respective equivalent identification card or authorization issued by another state… shall also meet the requirements of this subdivision.”Constitutional; the legislature may enact laws “consistent with” §1 but “shall not enact laws that hinder” patient access (§1.6). No ordinary statute can repeal it. Text read: Justia — Art. XIV §1.
Art. XIV, §2 — Marijuana legalization, regulation, and taxation (Amendment 3, 2022)Legalises 21+ personal use; creates comprehensive + microbusiness licences, the lottery, local-government rules, the 6% tax, expungement, and personal-use penalties.§2.1: purpose “to make marijuana legal under state and local law for adults twenty-one years of age or older” and “to prevent arrest and penalty for personal possession and cultivation of limited amounts.” §2.10(1) — see §1.3 for the full quote.Constitutional; in force 8 December 2022. Text read: FindLaw — Art. XIV §2; Revisor page: revisor.mo.gov — Art. XIV §2 (Verify pinpoint).

Implementing statutes — there is no single cannabis code

CitationSubjectSummary / verbatimLink / last-amended note
§§195.010, 195.017, 195.203, 195.740–195.773Controlled-substances definitions; industrial hemp (pre-HB 2641)§195.010: “Marijuana”, all parts of genus Cannabis “except industrial hemp”; “Illegal industrial hemp”: “All nonseed parts and varieties of the Cannabis sativa L. plant, growing or not, that contain an average delta-9 [THC] concentration exceeding three-tenths of one percent on a dry weight basis” — to be destroyed, destruction verified by the Missouri State Highway Patrol. §195.017 lists marijuana (except industrial hemp) and naturally occurring tetrahydrocannabinols (except industrial hemp) in Schedule I. §§195.740–195.773 were the Department of Agriculture industrial-hemp pilot/registration scheme (see §1.5 — now largely superseded in practice by USDA administration).revisor.mo.gov — §195.010 (pre-HB 2641 version as returned; Verify post-HB 2641 Revisor text, since HB 2641 repeals and reenacts §195.010 effective 12 November 2026).
§195.805 (L. 2020, HB 1682 merged with HB 1896)Edible design / universal symbol“No edible marijuana-infused product, packaging, or logo… shall be designed in the shape of a human, animal, or fruit, including realistic, artistic, caricature, or cartoon renderings. However, geometric shapes… shall be permitted.” Each package of an edible with ≥10 mg THC “shall be stamped with a universal symbol” — diamond containing “THC”, “M” under it, mg THC in package — “on the front of the package in red and white print and shall measure one-half inch by one-half inch.” §195.805(4) requires DHSS rules barring edibles designed to appeal to anyone under 18.revisor.mo.gov — §195.805. Session law 2020 as noted.
Chapter 579, esp. §§579.015, 579.020, 579.068Criminal drug offences — residual law beyond Article XIV protection§579.015: “A person commits the offense of possession of a controlled substance if he or she knowingly possesses a controlled substance, except as authorized by this chapter or chapter 195.” (2) Possession of any controlled substance except ≤35 g marijuana or any synthetic cannabinoid = Class D felony. (3) Over 10 g but ≤35 g marijuana/synthetic cannabinoid = Class A misdemeanour. (4) ≤10 g = Class D misdemeanour; Class A if a prior controlled-substance finding of guilt. (5) Burden of proving an exception/exemption is on the defendant. Trafficking thresholds in §579.068 include over 30 kg marijuana (2nd degree) and ≥100 kg / over 500 plants (1st-degree tier in the same section).revisor.mo.gov — Chapter 579. §579.015 effective 1 January 2017 as transferred from former §195.211 — confirm current text on the Revisor before citing an amendment date.
§§191.1145, 191.1146General telemedicine (not cannabis-specific)Cited by DHSS guidance for certification appointments: a physician/nurse practitioner may certify by telemedicine only if the standard of care does not require an in-person encounter and the clinician can truthfully answer all certification attestations.Verify exact §191.1146 text on revisor.mo.gov — not opened directly in this research.
§577.010Driving while intoxicatedOperating a vehicle “while in an intoxicated condition” — covers alcohol, drugs, or a combination. No per-se THC blood threshold was found in Missouri law (see §1.3).Verify current §577.010 text/penalty tiers on revisor.mo.gov — not opened directly.
