1.3 Possession limits, home grow, public consumption, vehicles, product types

Adult-use (21+) — Art. XIV, §2.10 + 19 CSR 100-1.040(4)–(5). Verbatim, §2.10(1): acts by a person 21+ that “are not unlawful and shall not be an offense under state law” (subject to §2.3 limitations):

“(a) Purchasing, possessing, consuming, using, ingesting, inhaling, processing, transporting, delivering without consideration, or distributing without consideration three ounces or less of dried, unprocessed marijuana, or its equivalent; (b) Possessing, transporting, planting, cultivating, harvesting, drying, processing, or manufacturing up to six flowering marijuana plants, six nonflowering marijuana plants (over fourteen inches tall), and six clones (plants under fourteen inches tall) provided the person is registered with the department for cultivation… [excess over 3 oz] kept at one private residence, [in] a locked space, and… not visible by normal, unaided vision from a public place; and… not more than twice the number of allowable plants… at one time.”
ItemLimit / rule
Single-transaction purchase (consumer)≤3 oz dried, unprocessed, or equivalent — 19 CSR 100-1.040(4)(A)1; §2.4(1)(m) bars DCR from setting a single-transaction limit below 3 oz. No residency requirement was found for adult-use purchase (photo ID for age only) — Verify on DCR Adult Use FAQs (not opened directly).
Possession (non-cultivator)≤3 oz or equivalent at any time.
Possession (cultivator)Any home-grown supply above 3 oz must remain in an enclosed, locked facility at a private residence.
Equivalency (19 CSR 100-1.010(26))1 oz flower = 8 g concentrate = 800 mg THC in infused products. Therefore 3 oz = 24 g concentrate = 2,400 mg THC in infused products. (Secondary “MME” shorthand — 3.5 g ≈ 100 mg THC — is arithmetically consistent; the rule text itself states the ounce conversion.)
Home grow — per person6 flowering + 6 non-flowering ≥14 in. + 6 clones (<14 in.), in a single enclosed, locked facility; cultivation card must be displayed in/near the grow area; flowering plants labelled with the cultivator's name.
Home grow — per residence / facilityConsumers: max 2 carded consumers per residence; 12 / 12 / 12 total per residence regardless of occupants. Patients: max 2 carded patients sharing one facility; 12 / 12 / 12 per facility. Caregiver (multi-patient): max 24 / 24 / 24 total. No patient may hold both patient-cultivation and consumer-cultivation at once. Cultivation only at a private residence (consumers), never at a place of business, and never where state law/private contract otherwise prohibits it.
Home-grow card term/fees (fee year 1 July 2026–30 June 2027, DCR fee schedule)Consumer personal cultivation $115.68; patient/caregiver cultivation $57.83. CONFLICT — Verify: 19 CSR 100-1.040(5)(H)6 says a consumer card is valid 12 months; the DCR fee page states patient/caregiver and consumer cultivation cards are valid 3 years. Patient/caregiver cultivation cards run with the underlying patient/caregiver card, up to 3 years. Fee page: health.mo.gov — Fee Schedule.
Over-limit penalties (§2.10(6))Possessing/producing/delivering-without-consideration over the limit but ≤2× the limit: 1st = civil infraction ≤$250 + forfeiture; 2nd ≤$500 + forfeiture; 3rd+ = misdemeanour ≤$1,000 + forfeiture. Community service in lieu at the greater of $15/hr or minimum wage. Under-21 version: ≤$250 civil penalty, option of up to 8 hrs drug education/counselling. Beyond 2×, Article XIV protection ends and Chapter 579 applies (see §1.2 — for example over 35 g unprotected marijuana = Class D felony under §579.015(2)).
Visible / unlocked growVisible by normal, unaided vision from a public place: civil penalty ≤$250 + forfeiture (§2.10(2)). Not kept in a locked space: ≤$250 + forfeiture (§2.10(3)).
Public consumptionSmoking in a public place (other than a locally licensed consumption area): civil penalty ≤$100 (§2.10(4)). Medical patients: “No qualifying patient shall smoke marijuana product for medical use in a public place, unless provided by law” (19 CSR 100-1.040(2)(C)1; §1.7(7)). Local governments may also regulate time/place of public smoking and on-site infused-product consumption areas, including restaurants preparing infused dishes/beverages for same-day on-site consumption (§2.5(6)).
Under-21Possession/use/transport/gift of ≤3 oz or accessories: civil penalty ≤$100 + forfeiture, option of up to 4 hrs drug education/counselling in lieu (§2.10(5)). No adult-use purchase.
Vehicles§2.3(1)(d)–(f) preserves laws against: operating/being in physical control of a motor vehicle, train, aircraft, motorboat or other motorised transport while under the influence; consuming while operating/in physical control while it is being operated; smoking inside while it is being operated. Impairment-evidence rule (verbatim, §2.3(1)(d)): “a conviction of a person who is at least twenty-one years of age for any applicable offenses shall require evidence that the person was in fact under the influence of marijuana at the time the person was in physical control… and not solely on the presence of [THC] or THC metabolites, or a combination thereof, in the person's system.” No per-se THC limit was found. §577.010 DWI (“intoxicated condition”) is the charging statute — Verify text/tiers.
Product types allowed (licensed market)Flower, prerolls, infused prerolls, concentrates, edibles/ingestibles, tinctures, topicals, suppositories, transdermal patches, capsules/teas/oils — via Article XIV definitions of “marijuana-infused products” (§2.2(16): “infused, dipped, coated, sprayed, or mixed with marijuana or an extract thereof, including… products that are able to be vaporized or smoked, edible products, ingestible products, topical products, suppositories, and infused prerolls”) and §1.2 “Administer” methods. Solvent extractions with solvents other than water, glycerin, propylene glycol, vegetable oil or food-grade ethanol require a manufacturing licence (§2.3(1)(l)).
Edible per-serving / per-package cap — correctionNO general 10 mg/serving or 100 mg/package cap was found in Article XIV or 19 CSR 100-1.120 as read. The binding package ceiling is the 3-ounce-equivalent rule: “Final marijuana product shall not be packaged in a manner that exceeds three (3) ounces of dried, unprocessed marijuana, or its equivalent” (19 CSR 100-1.120(4)) — i.e. up to 2,400 mg THC per package by equivalency, subject to labelled servings/doses. The 10 mg figure in Missouri law is a symbol trigger, not a serving cap: §195.805 requires the universal symbol on edible packages with ≥10 mg THC. Verify before asserting any serving cap — secondary sources claiming a Missouri 10/100 mg cap were not corroborated in the rule text read. The same applies to licensed THC beverages: no separate Missouri per-serving/per-container mg cap for licensed beverages was found in Article XIV or 19 CSR 100-1 as read — the 3-ounce-equivalent package ceiling and labelling rules apply. Verify on DCR product-approval guidance before asserting a beverage cap.

1.4 Medical programme

Regulator / cards: DHSS/DCR registry (online). Patient and caregiver cards valid 3 years (19 CSR 100-1.040(2)(A), (3)(A); renewal 60–30 days before expiry, with a new certification). Patient services page: health.mo.gov — Cannabis Patient Services.

Qualifying conditions — Art. XIV, §1.2(15) (verbatim list)

“[T]he condition of, symptoms related to, or side-effects from the treatment of: (1) Cancer; (2) Epilepsy; (3) Glaucoma; (4) Intractable migraines unresponsive to other treatment; (5) A chronic medical condition that causes severe, persistent pain or persistent muscle spasms, including but not limited to those associated with multiple sclerosis, seizures, Parkinson's disease, and Tourette's syndrome; (6) Debilitating psychiatric disorders, including, but not limited to, posttraumatic stress disorder, if diagnosed by a state licensed psychiatrist; (7) [HIV/AIDS]; (8) A chronic medical condition that is normally treated with a prescription medication that could lead to physical or psychological dependence, when a physician determines that medical use of marijuana could be effective… and would serve as a safer alternative…; (9) Any terminal illness; or (10) In the professional judgment of a physician, any other chronic, debilitating or other medical condition, including, but not limited to, hepatitis C, [ALS], inflammatory bowel disease, Crohn's disease, Huntington's disease, autism, neuropathies, sickle cell anemia, agitation of Alzheimer's disease, cachexia, and wasting syndrome.” “Qualifying patient” = a Missouri resident diagnosed with at least one qualifying condition (§1.2, definition).

Certification — physician / nurse practitioner

  • Who: Missouri-licensed physician (MD/DO, Chapter 334) or nurse practitioner (Missouri or compact RN licence + APRN recognition by the Missouri State Board of Nursing), in good standing, registered in the DHSS portal (19 CSR 100-1.050).
  • Form/timing: Electronic certification via the DHSS portal; signature date under 30 days old on application submission; required for every new and renewal application (19 CSR 100-1.040(2)(B)).
  • Telehealth: Permitted where the standard of care does not require an in-person encounter and the clinician can truthfully answer all certification attestations (DHSS FAQ position; §§191.1145–191.1146) — Verify pinpoint.
  • Psychiatric conditions: PTSD and similar conditions must be diagnosed by a state-licensed psychiatrist (§1.2(15)(6)).
  • Minors: Age 18+ and emancipated minors under 18 may hold a card; non-emancipated minors under 18 only with written parental/guardian consent, card issued to the parent/guardian, only a parent/guardian may be caregiver and may purchase/possess/supervise administration (19 CSR 100-1.040(2)(D)). Non-emancipated minors may not hold their own cultivation authorisation (a parent/guardian caregiver may cultivate for them).

Fees (1 July 2026–30 June 2027, DCR fee schedule)

Patient ID $28.92; caregiver ID $28.92; patient cultivation $57.83; caregiver cultivation $57.83; consumer cultivation $115.68. Clinician evaluation fee is separate and unregulated in the sources read. Source: health.mo.gov — Fee Schedule. Note: one secondary guide states a $25 state fee; $28.92 is adopted because it is the DCR fee-schedule figure — Verify on the fee page at the time of any application, since fees are CPI-adjusted annually.

Purchase / possession — patients & caregivers (19 CSR 100-1.040(4)(B))

  • Purchase: ≤6 oz dried, unprocessed, or equivalent per patient per 30 days, absent a certification for more. With a “compelling reasons” certification stating the amount, that amount becomes the patient's limit (increase request to DHSS within 30 days of certification signature; effective only on DHSS approval).
  • Possession (non-cultivator): up to a 60-day supply = 12 oz or equivalent.
  • Possession (cultivator / caregiver-cultivated): up to a 90-day supply, with any amount over the 60-day supply kept in an enclosed, locked facility.
  • Caregiver: separate limit per patient, stored separately and labelled with the patient's name; a patient may not combine patient + consumer limits to exceed either.
  • Over-limit (patients/caregivers): possession between the legal limit and 2× = DHSS sanctions including an administrative penalty up to $200 and loss of card(s) up to 1 year (19 CSR 100-1.040(4)(B)5).
  • Patient cultivation: see §1.3 (6/6/6 per person; facility caps above).
RESOLVED — audit (caregiver cap): Current Article XIV §1.7(5) and 19 CSR 100-1.040(3) agree — six qualifying patients per caregiver, max two caregivers per patient. The pre-Amendment 3 Justia “three” figure is removed.

Out-of-state rules

  • Possession protection, not purchase reciprocity, in the constitutional text: §1.5(1) — production of “the respective equivalent identification card or authorization issued by another state” satisfies the on-demand proof requirement for possession below Missouri patient limits. Secondary sources conflict on whether dispensaries may accept out-of-state cards for purchase; the rule text read (19 CSR 100-1.040, 100-1.180) does not establish a purchase right for out-of-state cardholders, and DCR's dispensary FAQ was not opened directly — Verify before stating either way. Practical note: any visitor 21+ may in any event buy as an adult-use consumer.
  • No interstate transport: Missouri law does not authorise carrying Missouri marijuana across state lines; HB 2641 §195.900(6) expressly preserves interstate commerce/through-shipment of hemp only, not marijuana. Transport of marijuana into Missouri from another state is not authorised by Article XIV (all Missouri-sold product must originate from Missouri-licensed cultivation, 19 CSR 100-1.100).
  • Missouri cards confer no rights outside Missouri.

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp — the central 2026 change

Pre-HB 2641 baseline (law as it stood on 30 October 2026, before 12 November 2026)

ItemRule
Definition (statute, pre-HB 2641)“Industrial hemp”: all nonseed parts/varieties of Cannabis sativa with a cropwide average delta-9 THC ≤0.3% on a dry-weight basis (or the federal maximum, where the older §195.010 formulation used “average” delta-9). “Illegal industrial hemp” over 0.3% average delta-9 = destruction, verified by the Highway Patrol.
HistoryHB 2034 (2018) pilot; SB 133 (2019) removed acreage caps (formerly 10–40 acres/plot, 2,000 acres statewide), created propagule/seed permits, exempted higher-education research. Department of Agriculture state programme discontinued 31 December 2022; from 1 January 2023 Missouri hemp production licensing moved to the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program (no state plan). Source (MU Extension, not a statute): extension.missouri.edu — Industrial Hemp (MX77).
Delta-8 / delta-9 / THCA (pre-HB 2641)No Missouri statute found that expressly scheduled hemp-derived delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THC-O, THCP or THCV as controlled substances, and no Missouri age/testing/labelling code for general hemp retail was found. Delta-9 in hemp was lawful only within the ≤0.3% delta-9 plant definition; concentrated/converted intoxicating products sat in the gap that EO 24-10 and then HB 2641 addressed. THCA flower: lawful at retail pre-HB 2641 only insofar as it met the delta-9 (not total-THC) hemp test — that is precisely what HB 2641 ends. Verify any DHSS/ATC hemp-retail rule beyond EO 24-10 — none was found.
THC measurement (pre-HB 2641)Delta-9 only, dry-weight, cropwide average — not total THC (THCA not counted). This is the key contrast with HB 2641.

Executive Order 24-10 (Governor Michael L. Parson)

  • Number/date: EO 24-10, signed 1 August 2024. Official text: sos.mo.gov — EO 24-10.
  • Scope (verbatim, operative paragraph): DHSS, “beginning on September 1, 2024, to designate foods containing unregulated psychoactive cannabis products as foods that come from an unapproved food source or as foods that contain an unapproved food additive that comes from an unapproved food source as set forth in the 2022 FDA Food Code and the 2013 Missouri Food Code… [and] to find foods that contain unregulated psychoactive cannabis products are deleterious, poisonous, and adulterated under Sections 196.070, RSMo, and 196.085, RSMo, and to take the necessary steps… to embargo and condemn any food containing unregulated psychoactive cannabis products.”
  • “Unregulated psychoactive cannabis products” (EO recitals): “delta-8 [THC], delta-10 THC, [HHC], [THC-O], [THCP], [THCV], and other similar compounds.”
  • Carve-out: “These provisions do not apply to products under the control or purview of [DCR] under Article XIV… as long as all psychoactive cannabinoids in those products are sourced from establishments licensed under Article XIV.”
  • ATC limb: ATC “to amend its regulations such that unregulated psychoactive cannabis products are prohibited from being sold on the premises of a liquor-licensed facility.” DHSS (29 August 2024) confirmed: no FDA- or Missouri-recognised “approved source” existed, so the food limb operated as an effective ban on such foods until an approved-source framework existed; DHSS enforcement/embargo from 1 September 2024. The ATC emergency rule was rejected by the Secretary of State (August 2024); ATC pursued regular rulemaking (6–8 months at the time) — Verify whether a final ATC rule ever issued.
  • Expiry / renewal: No expiry date appears in EO 24-10 itself, and no 2025 or 2026 renewal/replacement EO on intoxicating hemp was found in SOS executive-order searches. The Missouri Hemp Trade Association sued in Cole County Circuit Court (August 2024) challenging DHSS authority; outcome/injunction status was not established from an official source — Verify. As a practical matter EO 24-10 is superseded for covered products by HB 2641 from 12 November 2026 (below), but no express rescission of EO 24-10 was found — Verify.

HB 2641 — Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act, §195.900 (signed 23 April 2026)

Truly agreed text: House — HB 2641 truly agreed text. DCR implementation guidance (updated 23 October 2026): health.mo.gov — HB 2641 Guidance.

Definitions (verbatim, §195.900(2)):

  • “Hemp”: “the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof, and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a total [THC] concentration, including [THCA], of not more than three-tenths of one percent on a dry-weight basis.”
  • “Hemp” excludes: (a) any viable seed from a plant exceeding 0.3% total THC (including THCA); (b) any intermediate product containing cannabinoids not capable of being naturally produced by Cannabis sativa L., or naturally producible cannabinoids synthesised/manufactured outside the plant, or over 0.3% combined total THC + similar-effect cannabinoids (as determined by the U.S. Secretary of Health and Human Services); (c) any intermediate marketed/sold as a final product or directly to an end consumer; (d) any final product containing non-naturally-producible cannabinoids, or naturally producible cannabinoids synthesised/manufactured outside the plant, or over 0.4 mg combined total per container of THC (including THCA) + similar-effect/marketed-as-similar cannabinoids.
  • “Hemp-derived cannabinoid product”: any intermediate/final product derived from hemp (other than industrial hemp) containing cannabinoids in any form, intended for human/animal use by any means (inhalation, ingestion, topical, etc.), excluding FDA-approved drugs (FDCA §505).
  • “Industrial hemp” (§195.900(2)(5)): narrow, use-based — hemp grown for stalk/fibre or noncannabinoid stalk derivatives; whole grain/oil/cake/nut/hull or noncannabinoid seed derivatives; microgreens/edible immature-leaf products from compliant seed; non-commercial higher-education/independent-research plants; or viable seed grown solely to produce (a)–(d) materials. This is not the old broad “all ≤0.3% plant” definition.

Operative rule (verbatim, §195.900(3)–(4)):

“The cultivation, production, manufacturing, testing, transportation, and retail sale of all hemp-derived cannabinoid products within this state shall be conducted solely by entities licensed by the department under Article XIV… Hemp-derived cannabinoid products shall be considered marijuana and shall be subject to the legal framework contained in Article XIV…”

§195.900(5): hemp and industrial hemp themselves “shall not be considered marijuana,” are not subject to Article XIV or §195.900, and §195.900 is not to be construed to conflict with/preempt the 2018 Farm Bill. §195.900(6): nothing prohibits interstate commerce in hemp or through-shipment of hemp through Missouri. §195.900(7): DHSS + Attorney General enforce; the Attorney General has primary jurisdiction (§195.900(9)); only licensed comprehensive, medical, testing or microbusiness facilities may handle covered products in-state. §195.900(8): only comprehensive/medical/microbusiness dispensaries may use “dispensary” (or similar import) in a business name when selling CBD/hemp/marijuana/cannabinoids/hemp-derived products/paraphernalia.

Penalty: §195.900(10): “Any person or entity that violates the provisions of this section shall be subject to a fine of five thousand dollars per transaction and shall be guilty of a class D felony.” (“Transaction” = sale of a single unit, including a single unit in a multi-unit package, §195.900(2)(8).)

Effective dates (§195.900(11) + HB 2641 §B — product-specific; this table is the heart of the Missouri/federal interaction):

CategoryMissouri effective date
(a) Cannabinoids not capable of being naturally produced by Cannabis sativa L.; (b) naturally producible cannabinoids synthesised/manufactured outside the plant; (c) smokable/vapeable raw plant/flower/bud containing any amount of THCA; (d) solid candy, gummy, chewable, tablet, capsule, oil, baked good or other solid edible containing hemp-derived delta-9 above naturally occurring concentrations in the source hemp12 November 2026, regardless of federal delay
All other hemp-derived cannabinoid products (in practice: beverages with naturally occurring cannabinoids not synthesised/manufactured outside the plant, and similar “other” products)12 November 2026, except that if Congress delays federal hemp restrictions under 7 U.S.C. §1639o on/before 12 November 2026, §195.900 applies to such products only during periods in which federal restrictions prohibit them, and they shall not be sold/distributed/marketed to any person under 21
§§195.800, 195.819, 195.900 + repeal/reenactment of §195.010 (HB 2641 §B)12 November 2026

DCR guidance (23 October 2026) confirms the beverage reading: “Currently, beverages that have naturally occurring cannabinoids and were not synthesized or manufactured outside the plant will not be subject to the authority of Article XIV until December 11, 2026, provided that these products are not sold to individuals under 21 years of age.” And for licensees: they “may acquire, utilize, and sell hemp products that contain no more than [0.3%] of total THC and hemp products with no more than 0.4mg of total THC (such as CBD, CBN, CBG, etc.) from outside of Missouri's licensed marijuana facilities. Hemp-derived cannabinoids with higher potency are now considered marijuana and may only be produced by businesses licensed under Article XIV. Synthetic cannabinoids and cannabinoids produced through chemical conversion or modification of other cannabinoids remain prohibited for licensees.” Note what this means: converted delta-8/delta-10/HHC cannot simply move onto dispensary shelves — they remain prohibited for licensees even after 12 November.

Other HB 2641 provisions (same 12 November 2026 effective date unless noted):

  • §195.800: No state agency/employee may disclose personally identifying information of medical-card applicants/holders (patient, patient-cultivation, caregiver) to the federal government, federal employees, or unauthorised third parties, absent subpoena/court order; knowing violation = Class E felony.
  • §195.819 (truly agreed text, $500 — not the $2,500 in introduced-bill summaries): Dispensaries may not create/retain a record containing a consumer's identifying information without the consumer's affirmative agreement (DCR guidance: “affirmatively agreed… in writing”); violation = $500 fine per occurrence. Does not apply to constitutionally mandated patient/caregiver recordkeeping. DCR guidance adds: a dispensary may not refuse service for declining consent; review delivery/loyalty/intake records.
  • Unnumbered §1 (collective bargaining): “All persons employed in cannabis-related businesses in this state, including cultivation, processing, manufacturing, distribution, retail, and support operations, shall have the right to organize, form, join, and assist labor organizations and to bargain collectively… Cannabis industry employment… is not ‘agricultural labor’” for collective-bargaining exemptions.
  • Litigation — Verify outcome: MoHemp Trade Association, Lifted Liquids, Inc. (d/b/a Urb) and MNG 2005, Inc. v. Kehoe et al., W.D. Mo. No. 2:26-cv-04170, filed 16–17 July 2026, alleging vagueness (same products treated as both “hemp” and “marijuana” in different provisions) and federal conflict; State motion to dismiss reported pending, no injunction or ruling was found as of 30 October 2026 — Verify before 12 November.

Missouri response to the given federal baseline — summary

  1. HB 2641 adopts the federal total-THC + 0.4 mg architecture (§195.900(2)) — but Missouri is stricter/earlier for the listed categories: THCA flower, converted/synthetic cannabinoids, and elevated-delta-9 solid edibles hit 12 November 2026 even though the federal total-THC/0.4 mg bucket is delayed to 11/12 December 2026. Do not describe Missouri as “moving to December 11.”
  2. Beverages are the only material “other” category that rides the federal delay — and only while federal restrictions prohibit them, with a hard 21+ condition in the interim (DCR: outside Article XIV until 11 December 2026 on those conditions).
  3. No Missouri age/testing/labelling code for general hemp retail was found outside (a) EO 24-10's food/liquor limbs, (b) HB 2641's 21+ interim beverage condition, and (c) the licensed-marijuana rules that will govern covered products once they become “marijuana.” The failed 2025 hemp-beverage bills (HB 593, HB 463, SB 54, HB 393, HB 696, HB 1328 — all dead by 16 May 2025 per BillTrack summaries, non-official source — Verify on house.mo.gov/senate.mo.gov) would have created ATC licensing, 5 mg or 10 mg/serving beverage caps, 21+ sales and a 2% tax; none is law and none should be cited as current law.
  4. THC beverages in the licensed market: Dispensary-sold THC beverages are marijuana-infused products; no separate Missouri per-serving/per-container mg cap for licensed beverages was found in Article XIV or 19 CSR 100-1 as read — the 3-ounce-equivalent package ceiling and labelling rules apply. Verify on DCR product-approval guidance before asserting a beverage cap.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning/buffers

Packaging & labelling — 19 CSR 100-1.120 + Article XIV + §195.805

  • “All marijuana product shall be produced, packaged, and labeled in a manner that protects public health and is not attractive to children” (100-1.120(1)).
  • Banned design: No product/packaging in the shape or part-shape of a human, animal or fruit — realistic, artistic, caricature or cartoon (100-1.120(1)(B)1; §195.805(1)). No design causing confusion with a non-marijuana commercial product (100-1.120(1)(B)2).
  • Container: Resealable, opaque, certified child-resistant (except seeds/plants); FDA-approved food-contact substances (100-1.120(1)(B)3–4).
  • Design palette (100-1.120(1)(B)5): Only a primary colour + up to 2 logos/symbols of different colour(s) (logo width ≤ length/height of the word “Marijuana”), product name, side/behavioural-effects text, required label, and a QR code to product information.
  • Universal symbol + “Marijuana”: “Marijuana” printed at least as large as any other words; prominent red-and-white diamond containing “THC”, “M” under “THC”, and for infused products the mg THC in the package directly under the diamond (100-1.120(1)(C)1; §195.805 stamp rule for ≥10 mg edibles, §1.5 above).
  • Required label (container closest to product, in order): all active + other ingredients (no obscuring groupings such as “natural flavors” or “botanically derived terpenes”; including solvents used); servings/doses per package (marijuana licensees) or doses per package (medical); “best if used by” date; originator licence number; testing licensee; statewide track-and-trace tag number for mandatory-testing results; exact weight (grams for flower/concentrates/prerolls; mg delta-9 THC for other infused products); exact per-serving/dose mg of delta-9 THC, delta-9 THCA, CBD, CBDA, CBN, THCV, CBDV and delta-8 THC; terpene results if tested; instructions for use; estimated duration of effect; DHSS product/packaging approval number; warning: “Cognitive and physical impairment may result from the use of marijuana. Keep out of reach of children.” (100-1.120(1)(C)2).
  • Pre-approval: All product/packaging/label designs must be submitted via the DHSS web system before use; completeness notice within 30 days, approval/denial within 60 days of original submission; approval number issued (100-1.120(2)).
  • Package ceiling: No final product packaged above 3 oz dried, unprocessed, or equivalent (100-1.120(4)). Required elements may not be on a part removed to access the product (100-1.120(5)). Violation: up to $5,000 per product/packaging category (by approval number) (100-1.120(6)).

Advertising — Article XIV + 19 CSR 100-1.100(5)

  • Constitutional ceiling: DCR may “Regulate the advertising and promotion of marijuana sales, but any such regulation shall be no more stringent than comparable state regulations on the advertising and promotion of alcohol sales” (§2.4(1)(n)).
  • Rule (100-1.100(5)): Advertising/marketing only in compliance with applicable municipal/state sign/advertising law; no advertisement may contain: (1) false/misleading representations; (2) curative/therapeutic-effect claims unless evaluated/approved by FDA; (3) content attractive to children, including animal/fruit shapes (any rendering) and human shapes (artistic/caricature/cartoon); (4) brand statements inconsistent with labelling. Exterior signage/advertising must accurately reflect the licensee's legal/business/d/b/a/trade name on record. Outdoor signage (and interior signage visible from a public right-of-way) must comply with local sign ordinances. Facility visibility: no cultivation/manufacture/sale/display visible from a public place without binoculars/aircraft/optical aids (§2.9(6); 100-1.100).

Delivery

  • Authorised: Comprehensive and microbusiness dispensaries may deliver to consumers, patients and caregivers; medical dispensaries only to patients/caregivers (19 CSR 100-1.180(1)(A)6; 100-1.140(3)). Dispensary definitions in §2.2 expressly include delivery “to any address as directed by the patient, primary caregiver, or consumer” and receipt of orders “in person, by phone, or via the internet, including from a third party.”
  • Delivery location: “at any address as directed… as long as the address is a location allowing for the legal possession of marijuana product” (100-1.140(3)(C)).
  • Transport/delivery vehicles (100-1.140(4)): Rule text as returned confirms a printed statewide track-and-trace inventory manifest, facility-agent ID, GPS tracking and video monitoring of driver/passenger and storage areas — Verify the full 100-1.140(4) vehicle specification in the SOS CSR PDF before quoting camera-resolution or lockbox details, which were not in the excerpts read. Consumption on licensed premises, including approved transport vehicles, is prohibited (100-1.100(4)(M)).

Zoning / buffers / local control — Art. XIV, §2.5 (full local treatment in Part 3)

  • 1,000-foot buffer: “Unless allowed by the local government, no new marijuana facility shall be initially sited within one thousand feet of any then-existing elementary or secondary school, child day-care center, or church” (§2.5(4); mirrored in 19 CSR 100-1.100(1)(C) for facilities including off-site warehouses, measured as in Article XIV — freestanding: nearest external wall to nearest property-line point; in a larger structure: entrance/exit to entrance/exit, along the shortest lawfully walkable foot path).
  • No prohibition / undue burden: “Except as otherwise provided in this subsection, no local government shall prohibit marijuana facilities or entities with a transportation certification either expressly or through… ordinances or regulations that make their operation unduly burdensome” (§2.5(5)). “Unduly burdensome” = compliance measures imposing such high money/time/resource investment that a reasonably prudent businessperson would not operate (§2.2(23)).
  • Time/place/manner: Local governments may enact non-conflicting ordinances governing time, place and manner of facility operation, with civil penalties for violations (§2.5(5)); and governing public-smoking time/place and designated infused-consumption areas (§2.5(6)).
  • Local bans (non-medical dispensaries only): §2.5(1)–(2) provides a voter process for a local government to ban non-medical microbusiness + comprehensive dispensaries, and for repeal by majority vote on a prescribed ballot question; citizen-petition repeal votes occur only at a presidential-year November general election, on signatures of at least 5% of qualified voters (by last gubernatorial vote in the locale). Only the ordinances of the local government where the facility sits bind it (§2.5(3)). Verify the full §2.5(1) ban-adoption procedure before advising any specific locality. Full text, threshold and ballot form are in Part 3.
  • Dispensary relocation: Only within the congressional district of original licensure (19 CSR 100-1.100(1)(D)).

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking (Metrc)

  • System: Missouri's statewide track-and-trace system is Metrc (Marijuana Enforcement Tracking Reporting & Compliance). Vendor FAQ: metrc.com — Missouri FAQ (vendor source — the rule requirement is below).
  • Rule (19 CSR 100-1.130): “Each licensee shall use the state-wide track and trace system as its system of record to track marijuana product from seed or immature plant stage until the marijuana product is either purchased by a consumer, qualifying patient, or primary caregiver; expended during testing; or destroyed” (100-1.130(1)(D)); “All marijuana product in a medical or marijuana facility must be traceable in the state-wide track and trace system at all times” (100-1.130(1)(E)). Immature plants at least 8 in. tall or wide must be tagged; all packaged product bears a traceability tag; new package tags on category change or incorporation into another product. Licensees enter each day's beginning inventory, harvests, acquisitions, sales, disbursements, remediations, disposals, transfers, deliveries and ending inventory. Records retained at least 5 years (100-1.130(1)(B); also 100-1.100(4)(O) and §2.6(3) 5-year sales records).
  • Dispensary recording: Every amount sold/disbursed must be immediately recorded — grams for flower/prerolls/concentrates/infused prerolls, mg THC for infused products (100-1.130(1)(L)). Testing results/serving/dose amounts for final manufactured products are recorded in the system (100-1.130(1)(K)4 in excerpts).
  • Seed-to-sale vendors: Third-party seed-to-sale/POS providers require DHSS certification and a signed API User Agreement before operating (100-1.130(2)); certified entities may not begin operations before written DHSS approval.
  • Known limitation (State Auditor, February 2026 — secondary report of official audit): Metrc “does not currently have the capability to identify purchases over the legal transaction quantity limits in real time” (Missouri State Auditor, 18 February 2026, via auditor.mo.gov — Auditor news release). Verify against the audit report itself before relying on this in a compliance filing.
  • Research product: Research-facility product must also be tracked in the statewide system (19 CSR 100-1.200, per secondary summary — Verify).

1.8 Employment and drug testing protections

Medical patients/caregivers — protected (with limits)

Art. XIV, §1.7(15) (added by Amendment 3; subsection number and text corroborated by multiple secondary sources, official Revisor pinpoint not opened — Verify):

“Unless a failure to do so would cause an employer to lose a monetary or licensing-related benefit under federal law, an employer may not discriminate against a person in hiring, termination or any term or condition of employment or otherwise penalize a person, if the discrimination is based upon either of the following: (a) The person's status as a qualifying patient or primary caregiver who has a valid identification card, including the person's legal use of a lawful marijuana product off the employer's premises during nonworking hours, unless the person was under the influence of medical marijuana on the premises of the place of employment or during the hours of employment; or (b) A positive drug test for marijuana components or metabolites of a person who has a valid qualifying patient identification card, unless the person used, possessed, or was under the influence of medical marijuana on the premises of the place of employment or during the hours of employment.”

Exceptions in the same provision (per secondary summaries — Verify full text): where legal use affects the person's ability to perform job-related responsibilities, the safety of others, or conflicts with a bona fide occupational qualification reasonably related to employment; and where federal law ties a monetary/licensing benefit to a drug-free policy.

Related, in the constitutional text actually read: §1.7(1)(4) — no wrongful-discharge/discrimination claim lies “based on the employer… prohibiting the employee… from being under the influence of marijuana while at work or disciplining the employee…, up to and including termination…, for working or attempting to work while under the influence of marijuana.” §1.5(2): no patient denied organ-transplant access/priority for holding a card or medical use.

Adult-use consumers — NOT protected in the same way

Art. XIV, §2.3(3) (verbatim): “This section does not require an employer to permit or accommodate conduct otherwise allowed by this section in any workplace or on the employer's property. This section does not prohibit an employer from disciplining an employee for working while under the influence of marijuana. This section does not prevent an employer from refusing to hire, discharging, disciplining, or otherwise taking an adverse employment action against a person… because that person was working while under the influence of marijuana.” §2.1 likewise states §2 is “not intended to allow… the use of marijuana in the workplace.” A positive test alone, off-duty adult-use, and cardholder status are therefore treated differently: only the medical-cardholder rule in §1.7(15) constrains the employer.

Drug testing generally

No general Missouri drug-testing statute was found (no mandated panel, MRO, notice, or random-testing code for private employers in the sources read). Testing is governed by employer policy, federal requirements where applicable (for example DOT/federal contractors), and workers'-compensation / unemployment consequences (for example §§287.120, 288.050 — Verify current text and applicability; not opened). Safety-sensitive and federal-benefit positions are the principal carve-outs from §1.7(15).

Other HB 2641 labour rule: HB 2641 §1 — cannabis-business employees (cultivation, processing, manufacturing, distribution, retail, support) have the right to organise and bargain collectively; indoor cannabis work is not “agricultural labor” for collective-bargaining exemptions (see §1.5).

1.9 Pending legislation and ballot measures (status confirmed as far as official sites allowed)

Enacted in 2026: HB 2641 only (see §1.5). 2026 regular session adjourned 15 May 2026 (secondary reporting — Verify on house.mo.gov/senate.mo.gov session records). Bills below were filed in 2026 and were not shown enacted in this research; their final dispositions must be checked on house.mo.gov / senate.mo.gov bill tracking before any of them is cited as pending in a later session, because a bill that did not pass in 2026 does not carry over as pending law. None is presented here as current law.

PART 2 — TAXES

All tax facts below use the same figures as Part 0 and Part 3. Where a figure appears in several parts, it is the same figure: medical 4%, adult-use 6%, local up to 3%, FY2026 transfers $13,000,000 (medical) and $19,629,271 × 3 (adult-use).

2.1 State retail taxes — Article XIV

Medical — 4%

“A tax is levied upon the retail sale of marijuana for medical use sold at medical marijuana dispensary facilities within the state. The tax shall be at a rate of four percent of the retail price.” — Mo. Const. art. XIV, §1.4(1), as summarised from the constitutional text

“Article XIV of the Missouri Constitution applies a 4% tax on the retail sale of marijuana for medical use sold at marijuana dispensary facilities within the state.” — Missouri Department of Revenue (DOR). Medical tax is “separate from, and in addition to, any general state and local sales and use taxes” — §1.4(4). §1.4(5): “Except as authorized in this subsection, no additional taxes shall be imposed on the sale of marijuana for medical use.” Source (official, opened 30 Oct 2026): dor.mo.gov — Marijuana tax.

Adult-use — 6%

“A tax shall be levied upon the retail sale of non-medical marijuana sold to consumers at marijuana facilities licensed pursuant to this section within the state. The tax shall be at a rate of six percent of the retail price.” — Mo. Const. art. XIV, §2.6(1), opened directly from the Revisor

“This tax shall not apply to medical marijuana dispensed to a registered qualifying patient or caregiver.” — §2.6(1). DOR confirms the same 6% figure on the page above. DOR retention: after retaining “no more than two percent of the total tax collected or its actual collection costs, whichever is less,” adult-use tax is deposited in the Veterans, Health, and Community Reinvestment Fund — §2.6(1)–(2). Medical: DOR retains “no more than two percent for its actual collection costs” before deposit in the Missouri Veterans' Health and Care Fund — §1.4(1).

No other state cannabis tax: “Except as authorized in this Article, no additional taxes shall be imposed on the sale of marijuana.” — §2.6(6).

2.2 General state / local sales tax — in addition to the cannabis taxes

This is the point most secondary summaries get wrong in one direction or the other. Both constitutional sections say the cannabis tax is in addition to general sales/use tax: §2.6(4): “The tax levied pursuant to this subsection is separate from and in addition to any general state and local sales and use taxes that apply to retail sales, which shall continue to be collected and distributed as provided by general law.” DOR (opened 30 Oct 2026): “All marijuana products are subject to… A 4% marijuana tax on medical… or a 6% marijuana tax on adult use… [and] Sales tax, including any additional 3% local adult use marijuana sales tax, if imposed by the applicable locality.” State general sales-tax rate cited in the Missouri Supreme Court stacking opinion as 4.225%. A DOR fiscal note for HB 2641 likewise states hemp-derived products currently sold at retail are “subject to the state 4.225% sales tax as well as a local sales tax.” Verify: the exact combined rate at any single address (state 4.225% + local general sales taxes + 6%/4% cannabis tax + any 3% local adult-use tax) must be checked address-by-address in DOR's Sales and Use Tax Lookup / tax-rate tables. DOR points users there, but this research did not extract a jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction combined-rate table from that interactive tool. Illustrative arithmetic only, not a quoted rate: 6% adult-use + up to 3% local adult-use = 9% cannabis-specific tax, before general state/local sales tax. Secondary summaries putting the combined adult-use burden roughly 13–17% depending on locality were not verified from DOR and are not adopted here.

2.3 Local adult-use tax — up to 3%, voter approval required, one local government only

Constitutional text — §2.6(5), opened from the Revisor:

“Pursuant to Article III, Section 49 of this Constitution, the governing body of any local government is authorized to impose, by ordinance or order, an additional sales tax in an amount not to exceed three percent on all tangible personal property retail sales of adult use marijuana sold in such political subdivision. The tax authorized by this paragraph shall be in addition to any and all other tangible personal property retail sales taxes allowed by law, except that no ordinance or order imposing a tangible personal property retail sales tax under the provisions of this paragraph shall be effective unless the governing body of the political subdivision submits to the voters of the political subdivision, at a municipal, county or state general, primary or special election, a proposal to authorize the governing body of the political subdivision to impose a tax.”

Key definition — §2.2(12): “‘Local government’ means, in the case of an incorporated area, a village, town, or city and, in the case of an unincorporated area, a county.” That definition is the whole stacking fight.

DOR administration (opened 30 Oct 2026): “Section 2.6(5), Article XIV… allows the governing body of any local government to impose an additional sales tax on retail sales of adult use marijuana. This tax shall be in addition to any and all other tangible personal property retail sales tax.” “You may view our tax rate tables that include adult use marijuana tax rates.” “Additional adult use marijuana tax is effective on the first day of the second calendar quarter following Department of Revenue notification from a city or county imposing the tax. Example: The Department receives notification… in February. The tax will go into effect July 1.” Source: dor.mo.gov — Marijuana tax. No single official published list was found. DOR provides tax-rate tables / an address lookup, not a downloadable “all jurisdictions that adopted the 3% tax” list in the pages opened. See Part 3 for the jurisdictions confirmed individually.

Stacking — decided 22 July 2025, Missouri Supreme Court, Robust Missouri Dispensary 3, LLC v. St. Louis County, No. SC100898, 6–1:

“The plain language of article XIV, section 2 limits the definition of ‘local government’ to allow only one local government to impose a 3 percent tax—a village, town, or city in an incorporated area, and a county in an unincorporated area.” / “article XIV, section 2 authorizes a county to impose a retail sales tax at a dispensary located in an unincorporated area of that county but not at a dispensary located within the boundaries of an incorporated village, town, or city such as Florissant.”

Practical effect: in an incorporated city, only the city's 3% (if voter-approved) applies; in unincorporated county territory, only the county's 3% applies. Pre-ruling “stacking” of city + county 3% + 3% is no longer lawful in incorporated areas. Background confirmed in the Court of Appeals judgment (12 November 2024, No. ED112642): Florissant voters approved 3% on 4 April 2023; St. Louis County voters approved Prop M the same day; DOR then told the Florissant dispensary to remit both. The Supreme Court vacated the circuit-court judgment for the counties. Sources: Supreme Court opinion text via secondary reproduction (aboutbtax.com — Robust opinion reproduction); Court of Appeals: Justia — Robust, Court of Appeals (2024) — Verify: obtain the slip opinion from courts.mo.gov directly for dossier citation; the opinion text above was read via a secondary reproduction, not courts.mo.gov. Fiscal consequence reported by St. Charles County (official county site): “Ruling could cost St. Charles County more than $1 million a year” and “The 3% tax on recreational marijuana sales in St. Charles County is projected to generate $2 million a year. The money raised so far has been set aside unallocated pending the outcome of the court fight.” Source: sccmo.org — Robust ruling notice. Secondary estimate, not official: industry sources put the former stacked collection at ~$3 million/month statewide. Do not quote as an official figure.

Local tax does NOT apply to medical sales. §2.6(5) by its terms covers “adult use marijuana” only; §1.4(5) bars additional taxes on medical sales beyond general sales/use tax.

2.4 Revenue distribution — constitutional allocation order

Adult-use fund — §2.6(2), in order:

“The fund shall be a dedicated fund and shall be distributed as follows: (a) First, as determined by appropriation, to the department an amount necessary for the department to carry out its responsibilities under this section, including repayment of any cash operating transfers, payments made through contract or agreement with other state and public agencies necessary to carry out this section, and a reserve fund to maintain a reasonable working cash balance… (b) Second, as determined by appropriation, to governmental entities in amounts necessary for carrying out responsibilities in the expungement of criminal history records under this section; (c) Next, the remaining fund balance shall be distributed in thirds as follows: a. One-third… to the Missouri veterans commission and allied state agencies, as determined by appropriation, exclusively for health care and other services for military veterans and their dependent families; b. One-third… to the department to provide grants… to increase access to evidence-based low-barrier drug addiction treatment, prioritizing medically proven treatment and overdose prevention and reversal methods… to support overdose prevention education, and to support job placement, housing, and counseling for those with substance use disorders… c. One-third… to the Missouri public defender system. Any moneys credited to the Missouri public defender system shall be used only for legal assistance for low-income Missourians, shall not be diverted to any other purpose. (d) All monies… shall provide new and additional funding… and shall not replace existing funding.”

Source: Revisor, §2, opened 30 Oct 2026 (link in Part 1). Medical fund — §1: Fees and taxes, less operational expenses, go to the Missouri Veterans Commission (MVC) for health and care services for military veterans. No thirds split applies to medical money.

Official FY2026 transfers — DHSS press release, 18 June 2026 (opened 30 Oct 2026): Medical: “DHSS transferred $13,000,000 to MVC from medical marijuana funds in Fiscal Year 2026. DHSS has transferred a total of $65,978,820 to MVC to date.” Adult-use: “DHSS transferred $19,629,271 to each of the beneficiary agencies of adult-use marijuana funds in Fiscal Year 2026, bringing the total amount of adult-use marijuana funds transferred during Fiscal Year 2026 to $58,887,813 and the total amount of adult-use marijuana funds transferred to date to $128,613,859.” Beneficiaries named: Missouri Veterans Commission; Missouri State Public Defender; DHSS drug-addiction-treatment grant programme. Source: content.govdelivery.com — DHSS FY2026 transfers.

Other officially-sourced figures — use with the caveats stated:

  • FY2025 context (secondary analysis citing DOR FY2025 Financial and Statistical Report and DCR): $88 million in new FY2025 marijuana taxes added to an $84 million carryover = $172 million pool; legislature authorised $152 million, including $32 million DCR operations and $4 million expungement reimbursement. Actual distribution lagged authorisation — this is the “money sitting unspent” issue. Sources (secondary, citing official reports): mostpolicyinitiative.org — Economic impact of adult-use marijuana and mostpolicyinitiative.org — FY26 marijuana tax appropriation/transfer memo.
  • Missouri State Auditor, Marijuana Program, Report No. 2026-014, February 2026 (official audit, not opened in full in this pass): reported in secondary coverage as finding an $82.4 million balance in the Veteran Health and Care Fund and an $89.2 million balance in the Veterans, Health, and Community Reinvestment Fund, and that revenues “have not been distributed in accordance with the constitution.” Official audit index: auditor.mo.gov — Report 2026-014 — Verify: read the audit PDF itself before quoting balances in the final dossier.
  • Calendar-2025 totals reported from DHSS/DCR data via press coverage: ~$1.51–$1.52 billion total sales in 2025 (~$1.3 billion adult-use); $255.57 million total state + local sales-tax revenue in 2025 ($151.72 million state, $103.84 million local, per that reporting). Source (secondary reporting of DHSS data, not an opened DHSS page): mjbizdaily.com — Missouri sales hit record $1.5B in 2025 — Verify: confirm against DCR Data and Reports / 2025 annual report at health.mo.gov — Data and Reports before using the $255.57m split as an official figure. The DCR 2024 Annual Report PDF was located (health.mo.gov — 2024 Annual Report) but its full revenue tables were not extracted in this pass.
  • February 2023–June 2025 (secondary, citing DCR/DOR): ~$1.3 billion sales in that window is inconsistent with the $1.34bn (2023) + $1.46bn (2024) calendar-year figures also reported; do not combine windows. Use calendar-year or fiscal-year labels exactly as the source labels them.

Local-tax revenue: local 3% revenue is collected by DOR and distributed to the imposing city/county for local use; it is not part of the state thirds split. Statewide local total for 2025 is the $103.84m figure above only if the secondary DHSS-derived reporting is confirmed — see Verify above. No official DOR by-jurisdiction local-tax revenue table was extracted in this pass.

2.5 Hemp product tax — current position and the 12 November 2026 change

As of the dossier date (30 October 2026) — NOT yet in effect: DOR (opened 30 Oct 2026): “Missouri's House Bill 2641 will broaden the definition of marijuana to include all hemp-derived cannabinoid products, effective November 12, 2026, under the Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act.” “Beginning November 12, 2026, hemp-derived cannabinoid products, as outlined in Section 195.900, RSMo, are subject to the legal framework contained in Article XIV of the Missouri Constitution, including all applicable marijuana taxes. This is in addition to the applicable state and local sales or use taxes.” Source: dor.mo.gov — Marijuana tax.

Before that date: hemp-derived cannabinoid products sold at ordinary retail are subject only to general state (4.225%) + local sales tax, per the DOR fiscal note for HB 2641 (January/April 2026 versions). DOR noted it “does not collect information on the type or number of these products on the market.” Verify / timing conflict: the DOR fiscal note says “for fiscal note purposes only, DOR assumes this tax will begin January 1, 2027” because of relocation time to dispensaries, even though the statutory effective date is 12 November 2026. Do not state a first-collection date without checking DOR's post-November guidance. Also, “industrial hemp, as defined by Missouri statute” is expressly excluded from the Article XIV definition of “marijuana” (§2.2(13)) — HB 2641's reclassification is what changes treatment for intoxicating hemp-derived products, not for all hemp. No separate hemp excise tax was found. Do not invent one. (The 2% hemp excise taxes in HB 393 (2025) and HB 2768 (2026) introduced summaries were never enacted — Part 1 §1.9.)

2.6 IRS §280E — Missouri decoupling (state law only)

Missouri DOES decouple, by constitution — §2.6(8), opened from the Revisor:

“For taxpayers authorized to do business pursuant to this Article, the amount that would have been deducted in the computation of federal taxable income pursuant to 26 U.S.C. Section 280E of the Internal Revenue Code as in effect on January 1, 2021, or successor provisions, but is disallowed because cannabis is a controlled substance under federal law, shall be subtracted from the taxpayer's federal adjusted gross income, in determining the taxpayer's Missouri adjusted gross income.”

Source: Revisor, §2 (link in Part 1). DOR implementation, 2025 forms: “Marijuana Business Deduction – Enter the amount that is eligible to be claimed as a federal income tax deduction but is disallowed by section 280E… This deduction is exclusively limited to taxpayers authorized to do business under Article XIV of Missouri's Constitution.” Source: dor.mo.gov — MO-PTE Instructions 2025 (and MO-1120S instructions, same language).

Interaction with the given federal baseline: Missouri's subtraction is a state computation. It does not change federal treatment. Under the given baseline, federal §280E relief from 28 April 2026 covers Schedule III medical activity only; adult-use remains fully subject federally, so a dual-market Missouri business still faces a federal medical/adult-use split even though Missouri allows the subtraction for state purposes. Entity-specific treatment (corporation vs. pass-through, medical vs. adult-use revenue allocation) is Verify with a tax adviser / DOR guidance — the constitution states the subtraction, not the allocation method.

Not law — do not cite: SB 226 (2021), which contained similar §143.121 deduction language, was vetoed 9 July 2021 by Governor Parson and was not overridden. The operative decoupling for adult-use is the 2022 constitutional provision above, not SB 226. Earlier bills HB 2704 (2022) / HB 2 (2023) proposing the same deduction were also bills, not enacted law as cited.

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

3.1 Constitutional authority — counties / cities / villages / St. Louis City

St. Louis City vs. St. Louis County — do not conflate. St. Louis City is an independent city, not part of St. Louis County. Under §2.2(12), each is a “local government” for its own incorporated territory; St. Louis County is the “local government” only for its unincorporated areas (post-Robust).

What a local government CANNOT do — §2.5(5), opened from the Revisor:

“Except as otherwise provided in this subsection, no local government shall prohibit marijuana facilities or entities with a transportation certification either expressly or through the enactment of ordinances or regulations that make their operation unduly burdensome in the jurisdiction. However, local governments may enact ordinances or regulations not in conflict with this section, or with regulations enacted pursuant to this section, governing the time, place, and manner of operation of such facilities in the locality.”

“Unduly burdensome” is defined at §2.2(23) as measures requiring “such a high investment of money, time, or any other resource or asset that a reasonably prudent businessperson would not operate the marijuana facility.”

Other local powers in §2.5: §2.5(3): “The only local government ordinances and regulations that are binding on a marijuana facility are those of the local government where the marijuana facility is located.” §2.5(6): local ordinances may govern (a) time/place of smoking marijuana in public areas, and (b) consumption of marijuana-infused products in designated areas, including on-site culinary preparation by local restaurants. Local governments may establish civil penalties for violation of time/place/manner ordinances — §2.5(5), last sentence.

Medical facilities are different: under §1 (Amendment 2, 2018), a local government may not prohibit medical facilities, expressly or by unduly burdensome regulation. The voter ban power in §2.5(1) applies only to non-medical (adult-use) microbusiness dispensaries and comprehensive dispensaries. It does not authorise a ban on cultivation, manufacturing, testing, wholesale (other than microbusiness dispensary), or medical facilities.

3.2 The voter ban — text, threshold, timing, ballot form

§2.5(1)(a)–(b), opened from the Revisor — quote in full for the dossier:

“Except as provided in this subsection, a local government may prohibit the operation of all microbusiness dispensary facilities or comprehensive marijuana dispensary facilities regulated under this section from being located within its jurisdiction either through referral of a ballot question to the voters by the governing body or through citizen petition, provided that citizen petitions are otherwise generally authorized by the laws of the local government. Such a ballot question shall be voted on only during the regularly scheduled general election held on the first Tuesday after the first Monday in November of a presidential election year, starting in 2024… A citizen petition… shall be signed by at least five percent of the qualified voters in the area proposed to be subject to the prohibition, determined on the basis of the number of votes cast for governor in such locale at the last gubernatorial election held prior to the filing of the petition.”

Ballot form (§2.5(1)(b)):

“Shall (insert name of local government) ban all non-medical microbusiness dispensary facilities and comprehensive marijuana dispensary facilities from being located within (insert name of local government and, where applicable, its ‘unincorporated areas’) and forgo any additional related local tax revenue? ( ) Yes ( ) No.”

“If at least sixty percent of the votes cast on the question by the qualified voters voting thereon are in favor of the question, then the ban shall go into effect as provided by law. If a question receives less than the required sixty percent, then the jurisdiction shall have no power to ban non-medical microbusiness dispensary facilities or comprehensive marijuana facilities regulated under this section, unless voters at a subsequent general election on the first Tuesday after the first Monday in November of a presidential election year approve a ban…”

Repeal — §2.5(2): a ban may be repealed by the governing body's own ordinance or by a vote of the people (same presidential-year general-election timing for a referred repeal question; citizen petition at 5%). Repeal ballot form: “Shall [local government] allow non-medical microbusiness dispensary facilities and comprehensive marijuana dispensary facilities to be located within [local government and where applicable, its ‘unincorporated areas’] as regulated by state law?” — repeal requires only a majority, not 60%.

Consequence for existing medical licensees — §2.4(19): if a medical dispensary is in a jurisdiction that bans non-medical retail facilities, it may apply for a comprehensive licence in a new location within the same congressional district, and the application “shall be granted so long as the new location meets all the requirements.”

3.3 Zoning, buffers, moratoriums

State buffer — §2.5(4), opened from the Revisor: “Unless allowed by the local government, no new marijuana facility shall be initially sited within one thousand feet of any then-existing elementary or secondary school, child day-care center, or church.” Measurement: from the external wall of a freestanding facility closest to the school/daycare/church to the closest point of that property line; if either is part of a larger structure, to/from the closest entrance/exit; “along the shortest path… that can be lawfully traveled by foot.” Implementing rule (19 CSR 100-1.100(1)(C), via Cornell reproduction — Verify against sos.mo.gov CSR): “Unless expressly allowed by the local government, no medical or marijuana facility… shall be sited… within one thousand feet (1,000') of any then-existing elementary or secondary school, day-care, or church.” Source: Cornell — 19 CSR 100-1.100. Local governments may allow a shorter distance / waive the buffer — the constitution says “unless allowed by the local government.” Local zoning may also be stricter on time/place/manner, provided it is not unduly burdensome and does not conflict with Article XIV or DCR rules. No statewide local-zoning code was found; zoning is city-by-city.

Worked examples (official city documents):

  • Des Peres (St. Louis County) — conditional-use permit required for a comprehensive dispensary in C-1/C-2 commercial districts; 1,000-foot rule applied (Ord. 2947, 8 August 2022, medical; Ord. 2976 / Bill 23-3010, 2023, comprehensive, Root66 Des Peres LLC, 12095 Manchester Road). The CUP itself provided that if voters approved a 60% ban in November 2024, the comprehensive approval would be null and void but the medical CUP would remain. Source: desperesmo.org — Root66 CUP.
  • Kansas City, Missouri — Ord. 230128 (9 February 2023, passed as substituted): amended Ch. 88, §88-354, “Medical Marijuana Facilities,” to allow medical and non-medical facilities as authorised by Article XIV, §§1–2. Source: clerk.kcmo.gov — Ord. 230128.
  • St. Louis City — Ord. 71633, effective 16 February 2023, amended the zoning code to incorporate Amendment 3 uses (comprehensive, microbusiness, testing definitions, use table); Ord. 71897, effective 11 October 2024, extended permitted operating hours of marijuana dispensaries. Source: stlouis-mo.gov — ordinance index.
  • Springfield, Missouri (Greene County) — 17 April 2023, unanimous (9–0) ordinance governing recreational marijuana, including no possession/consumption at schools (preschool through higher education) or correctional facilities. This is a time/place/manner ordinance, not a ban. Source: ksmu.org — Springfield ordinance, April 2023.

Moratoriums — interim only, not bans: Des Peres: moratorium on consideration of adult-use applications, extended to 21 August 2023 (Bill 23-2996, second extension). The city's own papers state the constitutional ban could not be voted until November 2024, creating a “26 month” gap that interim regulation/moratorium was meant to bridge. Source: desperesmo.org — Moratorium extension. No statewide moratorium list was found. Any other city moratorium is Verify city-by-city; a moratorium adopted after November 2024 that purports to be a permanent ban without a 60% presidential-year vote would conflict with §2.5(1).

3.4 Bans by vote — what was actually found, and what was NOT found

There is NO single official state list of ban jurisdictions. Neither DCR nor DOR nor SOS publishes a “banned jurisdictions” list in the pages opened. SOS election results are county-level / local-authority canvasses; a ban question appears only on that jurisdiction's ballot. The table in §3.5 therefore records, for each named jurisdiction, only what was confirmed, and marks the rest Verify rather than asserting “no ban.”

Confirmed ban-ballot placement (not a confirmed ban outcome): Des Peres, Missouri — Ord. 2976 (adopted 12 June 2023, 5–0) called an election for 5 November 2024, Proposition 1, to “Ban All Non-Medical Microbusiness Dispensary Facilities and All Comprehensive Marijuana Dispensary Facilities from Being Located in the City of Des Peres,” with the 60% instruction on the ballot. Earlier: Des Peres voters approved Amendment 2 (medical, 2018) 61.9% (3,187–1,962 in the ordinance recital) and rejected Amendment 3 (adult-use, 2022) locally, 49.2% Yes (2,336 Yes) — i.e. a local majority against adult-use legalisation, but that is not a ban vote. Source: Des Peres Ord. 2976 (PDF). Verify / Unavailable: the 5 November 2024 Proposition 1 result (Yes/No votes and %) was not retrieved from the St. Louis County canvass or SOS in this pass. Do not state that Des Peres banned dispensaries, or that the ban failed, until that canvass is checked. Note the CUP consequence above: if the ban had passed at ≥60%, the Root66 comprehensive CUP would have become void; if it failed, Des Peres “shall have no power to ban” until the next presidential-year general election (2028).

No other 2024 ban adoption was confirmed in this pass. Searches for a statewide 2024 ban list returned only the Des Peres placement, the constitutional text, and municipal-law guidance noting the 27 August 2024 certification deadline and that after November 2024 the question cannot be submitted again until 2028. Source for deadline/guidance: municipalfirm.com — Ban ballot certification deadline.

Important negative clarification: local opt-out by ordinance alone is not lawful for adult-use dispensaries. A city council vote, a zoning exclusion of all dispensary uses, or a moratorium is not a §2.5(1) ban. If a dossier elsewhere lists a Missouri city as “banned” on the basis of a council ordinance or a moratorium alone, that listing should be treated as unverified unless a ≥60% presidential-year vote is cited.

Key: Tax 3% = voter-approved local adult-use tax (city, or county for unincorporated areas only after Robust). “—” = not confirmed in this pass, NOT “no tax / no ban.” All named jurisdictions below were at least searched; only the rows marked Confirmed should be quoted as established.
