For the final dossier, run each “—” address through DOR's lookup rather than copying this table as exhaustive.

April 2023 context (secondary reporting of the statewide votes, for orientation only): Voters in Kansas City, St. Louis and Columbia all approved their ballot questions, as did Jackson County, St. Louis County and Boone County; Kansas City's tax won over 70% (33,637–12,271 in unofficial returns); St. Louis passed with almost 63%; in St. Louis County the county tax passed in 32 of the 36 cities where it was on the ballot, failing in Charlack, Flordell Hills, Northwoods and Velda City; in Jackson County it passed in each of the 12 municipalities where it was on the ballot, as well as in four smaller cities of Boone County; voters authorised new taxes countywide in Jasper and Newton counties. Source: marijuanamoment.net — April 2023 votes (Missouri Independent reporting). These are contemporaneous secondary reports, not canvasses — use the confirmed rows above for any firm claim.

3.6 Border markets — Kansas, Illinois, Iowa, Arkansas, Oklahoma

  • No official Missouri source in this pass quantifies cross-border sales, ranks border clusters, or attributes a share of Missouri sales to out-of-state buyers. Do not estimate or rank clusters — per the commission, this section reports only what official sources say, and official sources say nothing on this point in the material opened.
  • Kansas (west): Kansas City, Missouri is the border market. Missouri adult-use sales are lawful in Missouri to adults 21+ on government photo ID; a lawful Missouri purchase does not authorise possession in Kansas, where marijuana possession remains illegal. This is stated in secondary border guides, not in an opened Missouri official page — Verify against Kansas law for any Kansas-side statement, and do not present a Missouri dispensary's location near State Line as an official “border cluster.”
  • Illinois (east): St. Louis metro / Metro East is the border market. Illinois also has adult-use sales, so the Missouri-side price/tax advantage is smaller than on the Kansas side. No official Missouri comparison of Missouri vs. Illinois border sales was found.
  • Iowa (north), Arkansas (south), Oklahoma (southwest): Kirksville / northern Missouri (Iowa border), Joplin / Branson / southwest Missouri (Arkansas and Oklahoma borders) all have operating dispensaries, as shown by DCR licensee listings in secondary directories. No official Missouri statement on border-market concentration, visitor share, or enforcement at those borders was found in this pass.
  • Transport warning (general, not Missouri-specific): carrying cannabis across a state line remains a federal offence even between two legal states. This is background law, not a Missouri local rule, and should not be presented as a Missouri local ordinance.

3.7 Local permit fees and local tax revenue — officially reported

No statewide local-fee schedule exists in Article XIV or DOR guidance. Local fees, where they exist, are city business-licence / zoning / conditional-use fees, and they vary by city. Only these examples were opened:

  • Bellefontaine Neighbors (St. Louis County) — PROPOSED, not confirmed enacted: Bill 2730 (2025) would add “Business license (Marijuana related use): $500.00” and “Site review permit (Marijuana related use): $500.00,” cap recreational dispensaries at two, and require a city business licence. Do not quote as current law — the document opened is a bill, and enactment was not confirmed. Source: cityofbn.com — Bill 2730 (PDF).
  • Columbia — old medical form, not a current adult-use fee: City of Columbia medical facility licence application (2020 form) shows a general business-licence formula (under $25,000 gross = $15; $25,000–$100,000 = $25; over $100,000 = $0.25 per $1,000, max $750) plus a $2,000 processing fee and $17 in-state background fee. Verify current Columbia fees — this form predates adult-use and may not reflect current charges. Source: como.gov — Medical licence application (2020, PDF).
  • State fees are NOT local fees (for contrast only): DCR Fee Schedule, 1 July 2026–30 June 2027 — comprehensive dispensary new application $3,083.40, annual fee $11,568.13; microbusiness dispensary/wholesale $1,637.00 application and annual; change of location (non-microbusiness) $5,000. Source: health.mo.gov — Fee Schedule.
  • Local tax revenue by city: only Springfield (forecast, not actuals — see §3.5) and St. Charles County (projected $2m/year, pre-Robust) were found in official city/county documents. Columbia's pre-vote forecast was “just under $1 million in FY2024.” No official audited by-city local-tax collection table was extracted. Verify via each city's adopted budget / CAFR and DOR distributions.

3.8 Business concentration — ONLY what official sources say

Per the commission, no ranking, clustering estimate, or “top city” list is made here.

What official sources do say about geographic allocation:

  • Microbusiness — congressional-district allocation (constitutional): “A minimum of 48 microbusiness licenses are to be awarded during three separate rounds for a total of 144 licenses. Six licenses are awarded per congressional district, using the congressional map made effective on Dec. 6, 2018. The six licenses are distributed between two microbusiness dispensaries and four microbusiness wholesale facilities.” Source (official DCR CEO Activity Report 2025): health.mo.gov — CEO Activity Report 2025. Rounds as officially reported: Round 1, 48 licences (October 2023); Round 2, 57 licences (July 2024, including 9 re-awarded after Round 1 revocations); Round 3 lottery 9 September 2026, ~77 licences (per DHSS; Auditor, February 2026, said “at least 82” would be awarded in Round 3 as of 1 August 2025 — the difference reflects revocations/surrenders between the audit and the lottery and is Verify against the DHSS Round 3 award list). Source for audit figure: auditor.mo.gov — Report 2026-014. “Future changes to the boundaries or the number of congressional districts shall have no impact on microbusiness license numbers or distribution.” — §2.4(13) (Revisor).
  • Comprehensive facilities — conversion, not new geographic allocation: for the first 548 days after DCR began issuing adult-use licences, comprehensive licences could only be issued by conversion of an existing medical licence at the same location (§2.4(17)). Relocation of a dispensary/microbusiness is only within the congressional district of original licensure (19 CSR 100-1.100(1)(D), via Cornell — Verify against SOS CSR).
  • No official “concentration” finding: DCR publishes a licensee list/dashboard, but no official statement in the material opened says any city, county, or border area is over- or under-concentrated, or ranks dispensary counts by city. Secondary directories that list “most dispensaries” by city (for example St. Louis, Kansas City, St. Peters, Columbia, Springfield) are not official counts and are deliberately not reproduced as a ranking here. The CPPC October 2026 study's district-level findings (Part 6) are the closest official-adjacent material: District 5 (Kansas City) highest dispensary sales, ~$294m in 2025; District 6 fastest growth, ~+40% over 3 years; HHI values well below concentration thresholds in all 8 districts — but the study itself notes it lacked ownership data.

PART 4 — LICENCE TYPES

Regulator and legal basis. Regulator: Division of Cannabis Regulation (DCR), Missouri Department of Health and Senior Services (DHSS). Medical: Amendment 2, November 2018, Mo. Const. art. XIV, §1. Adult-use: Amendment 3, November 2022, art. XIV, §2. Adult-use possession legal 8 December 2022; adult-use retail sales began 3 February 2023. Rules: 19 CSR 100-1. Official fee page (opened 30 Oct 2026): health.mo.gov — Fee Schedule. Licence prefixes seen on regulated product labels (opened 30 Oct 2026, Part 5): MAN, CUL, DIS, MBW, MBD, TES — manufacturing, cultivation, dispensary, microbusiness wholesale, microbusiness dispensary, testing.

4.1 Comprehensive licences — the dominant tier

Nearly all operating licensees are comprehensive. A comprehensive facility need not segregate or account for product as medical vs. adult-use, and may serve both markets. A medical-only facility may serve qualifying patients only. (For the federal consequence of that non-segregation rule, see the Federal Baseline section and Part 1 §1.8/§1.9 notes: it does not satisfy a federal medical-only Schedule III / §280E split.)

TypeWhat it allows
Comprehensive cultivationAcquire, cultivate, process (including pre-rolls, not marijuana-infused products), package, store on-/off-site, transport to/from, and sell marijuana, seeds and clones to a medical, comprehensive or testing facility.
Comprehensive manufacturing — full name: comprehensive marijuana-infused products manufacturingAcquire, process, package, store, manufacture, transport to/from a medical, comprehensive or testing facility, and sell marijuana-infused products, pre-rolls and infused pre-rolls to a dispensary, testing or other manufacturing facility.
Comprehensive dispensaryAcquire, process, package, store on-/off-site, sell, transport to/from and deliver marijuana, seeds, clones, infused products and paraphernalia to a qualifying patient/caregiver or adult consumer 21+, on licensed property or to an address directed by the buyer, and to comprehensive, testing or medical facilities. Orders may be taken in person, by phone or online, including via a third party. Delivery requires prior DHSS approval/inspection under 19 CSR 100-1.140.

Medical equivalents exist in parallel (medical cultivation, medical manufacturing, medical dispensary) with the same functions limited to the medical market. Fees for medical licences are higher than comprehensive on the current schedule (see §4.5) — a legacy of the older medical schedule, not a difference in scope.

Canopy / plant limits — 19 CSR 100-1.160 (via Justia text, current through Register Vol. 50 No. 12, 16 June 2025): Justia — 19 CSR 100-1.160.

  • Indoor (artificial light), medical or comprehensive: 30,000 sq ft of flowering plant canopy.
  • Outdoor (natural light): 2,800 flowering plants.
  • Greenhouse (combined light), at licensee election: 2,800 flowering plants OR 30,000 sq ft flowering canopy.
  • Combined indoor/outdoor/greenhouse: pro-rata ratio, not to exceed 100% of total allowable flowering plant/canopy space.
  • No separate square-foot cap is stated for manufacturing or dispensary licences.

Constitutional caps — minimums, not hard maximums. Per the State Auditor (February 2026, opened 30 Oct 2026): auditor.mo.gov — Report 2026-014. Article XIV, §1 required DHSS to issue a minimum of: 24 dispensary licences in each of 8 congressional districts = 192; 1 manufacturing licence per 70,000 inhabitants (2010 census) = 86; 1 cultivation licence per 100,000 inhabitants (2010 census) = 60; 2 testing facility licences. 192 + 86 + 60 = 338, the figure MoCannTrade cites as the constitutional minimum of medical/adult-use facility licences: mocanntrade.org — About. DCR initially set regulatory caps equivalent to those minimums. The Constitution allows DCR to issue additional licences to meet demand and maintain competition — the October 2026 CPPC market study (Part 6) notes this expressly. Verify: whether any post-minimum comprehensive licences beyond conversion + appeals have in fact been issued; the fee page states new comprehensive applications are closed (below), so growth since 2023 has come overwhelmingly from conversion, appeals settlements (68 additional licences to settle 2019 appeals, per Auditor) and microbusinesses.

Ownership caps (secondary, citing Art. XIV §1 — Verify against primary text): No entity under substantially common control, ownership or management may hold more than 10% of total licences outstanding in each facility type (cultivation, dispensary, manufacturing separately, §§1 and 2 combined, rounded down) licences. Source: legalclarity.org — Missouri licence types — Verify: primary constitutional wording and whether the cap counts medical + comprehensive together. This matters because the CPPC study could not assess ownership concentration at all (Part 6): the caps cannot be used to infer actual concentration without ownership data DCR does not publish.

General requirements (19 CSR 100-1, summarised): Metrc statewide seed-to-sale tracking as system of record; 5-year record retention; NTEP-approved scales; facility security including 1920×1080p video at at least 15 fps, no motion-detection-only monitoring where product is present, vaults and silent alarms (19 CSR 100-1.090: Cornell — 19 CSR 100-1.090); product must be tested before transfer to a dispensary (except seed/clone, per 19 CSR 100-1.160).

4.2 Conversion: medical → comprehensive

  • When: From 8 December 2022 (Amendment 3 effective date) existing medical licensees could request conversion. Adult-use sales followed 3 February 2023.
  • How: Business-update application in the online registry portal, selecting conversion; $2,000 non-refundable fee (still $2,000.00 on the 2026–27 schedule, opened 30 Oct 2026).
  • Required plans: (1) how the licensee will serve both medical and adult-use markets while maintaining adequate supply at reasonable cost to qualifying patients; (2) a plan to promote/encourage participation by people from communities disproportionately impacted by marijuana prohibition.
  • Decision rule: DCR to approve/deny within 60 days of a complete request; a request not processed within 60 days is deemed approved. Grant unless the medical licensee is not in good standing.
  • No re-inspection: If the facility was already approved to operate as a medical facility, the comprehensive licensee may begin operating without additional approvals/inspections.
  • Exclusivity window (historical): For the first 18 months after final rules, comprehensive dispensary licences could only go to medical licensees; the window is long expired, but no general comprehensive application window has replaced it — see §4.6.
  • Sources: DCR conversion announcement via Greenway, 23 November 2022: mogreenway.com — Conversion process (Nov 2022); fee page above.

4.3 Microbusiness licences

Two types only. Both serve both medical and adult-use markets.

TypeWhat it allows
Microbusiness dispensaryRetail sale to patients and adult consumers 21+; may create pre-rolls. Orders in person, by phone or online including via third party. Delivery rights are cited for microbusiness dispensaries in secondary summaries — Verify in 19 CSR 100-1.180/1.140.
Microbusiness wholesaleCombined cultivation + manufacturing: acquire, cultivate, process, package, store on-/off-site, manufacture, transport to/from, deliver and sell marijuana, seeds, clones and infused products only to a microbusiness dispensary, another microbusiness wholesale facility, or a testing facility. May create pre-rolls and infused pre-rolls. A wholesaler may do all or only a subset of authorised activities. It may NOT sell directly to comprehensive dispensaries or to consumers.

Plant limit: Microbusiness wholesale: 250 flowering plants at any given time (Art. XIV §2; 19 CSR 100-1.160(2)(A)1). No canopy square-foot alternative is stated.

Eligibility — majority owned AND operated by individuals each meeting at least one criterion (19 CSR 100-1.190, Justia text to 16 June 2025): Justia — 19 CSR 100-1.190.

  1. Net worth under $250,000 AND income below 250% of federal poverty level for at least 3 of the 10 calendar years before applying;
  2. Valid service-connected disability card from the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs;
  3. Person who has been, or whose parent, guardian or spouse has been, arrested/prosecuted/convicted of a non-violent marijuana offence at least 1 year before the rule's effective date, excluding provision to a minor or DUI;
  4. Residence in a ZIP code or census tract where at least 30% live below federal poverty level, or unemployment is 50% above the state average, or historic marijuana incarceration rate is 50% above the state average;
  5. Graduated from a school district that was unaccredited (or successor designation) at graduation, or lived in a ZIP containing an unaccredited district for 3 of the past 5 years.
  • One microbusiness licence per person/entity per application period; a 10%+ interest generally counts as ownership (MoCannTrade explainer, 2026): mocanntrade.org — Microbusiness.
  • Verify: current DCR eligibility page / qualifying ZIP list (cannabis.mo.gov) — not text-retrieved in this session.
  • Chief Equity Officer: constitutionally required post within 60 days of 8 December 2022; runs public education, technical assistance, and the eligibility review. Incumbent named in 2026 reporting as Lesley Turek (Abigail Vivas held the role in the Round 1 review).

Lottery rounds and allocation:

  • Constitutional design: at least 6 per congressional district per round — at least 2 dispensary + at least 4 wholesale — i.e. at least 48 per round, at least 144 total across 3 rounds (8 districts × 6 × 3).
  • Round 1: Applications 27 July–10 August 2023 (over 1,600 applications); Missouri Lottery drawing 28 August 2023; 48 licences issued by 4 October 2023 (16 dispensary, 32 wholesale). Source: DHSS via bevinfogroup.com — Round 1 awards.
  • Round 2: Applications 15–29 April 2024 (over 2,000 applications); drawing June 2024; 57 licences awarded 24 July 2024 — 48 base + 9 re-awarded from revoked Round 1 licences (2 dispensary + 4 wholesale per district, plus 9 redistributed across 6 districts). 2,026 applications denied in Round 2 (secondary summary of Auditor/DCR data).
  • Round 3 (final constitutionally required round): Applications 13–27 July 2026 — 888 applications; Missouri Lottery drawing 9 September 2026 (blind, by unique application identifier, identities not in the room); ~77 licences to issue from December 2026, of which ~20 are available due to revocations for rule violations or surrenders from earlier rounds. Lottery selection does not guarantee a licence — eligibility review follows. Refund form for non-drawn applicants becomes available 30 days after all Round 3 licences issue; refunds require certification of eligibility and no pending/future litigation over the denial. Official Round 3 release (opened 30 Oct 2026): content.govdelivery.com — Round 3 lottery.
  • Note on Auditor projection vs. outcome: The February 2026 Auditor report (as of 1 August 2025) said DCR “will award at least 82 licences in the third round.” The September 2026 DCR figure is ~77. The difference is not explained in retrieved sources — Verify whether 82 was a floor including licences later surrendered/held, or was superseded.
  • Pathway to comprehensive (secondary — Verify): At least 50% of certain newly available comprehensive licences are reserved by lottery for qualified microbusiness owners operating at least 1 year in good standing (MoCannTrade 2026 explainer). No such comprehensive lottery was found open at 30 October 2026.

Fronting / predatory agreements — investigations and revocations:

  • Constitutional mechanism: Chief Equity Officer must complete an eligibility review within 60 days of issuance and publish results. In Rounds 1–2 that review came after issuance, so ineligible licensees were issued then investigated.
  • Round 1: 11 notices of pending revocation. Ultimately 9 revoked (Auditor total). Named in reporting: all 6 licences connected to investor Michael Halow revoked, plus 2 connected to Michigan-based Canna Zoned (Columbia and Arnold dispensaries). Grounds cited: failure to prove the business would be majority owned/operated by eligible persons; failure to document eligible majority ownership; one disqualifying felony offence (Auditor). Missouri Independent investigation (October 2023) found applicants recruited nationally (including via Craigslist) who signed agreements relinquishing control/profits; Canna Zoned was connected to 104 of 1,048 dispensary lottery entries; Cannabis Business Advisors (Arizona) to 400+ dispensary applicants including 6 winners, none certifiable; Amendment 2 Consultants to 80+ applicants, 2 winners.
  • Round 2: 32 notices of pending revocation; 25 revoked, nearly all on ownership grounds, 16 connected to Halow.
  • Cumulative — conflict, see Part 0 §0.9: Auditor-based sum 9 + 25 = 34 revoked. Later Missouri Independent reporting (December 2025, via St Joseph Post): “of the 105 microbusiness licences issued so far, 35 have been revoked, including 22 involving contracts drafted by Armstrong Teasdale.” A May 2026 secondary synthesis states 38 revoked + 1 surrendered of 106 issued. The 34 vs. 35 vs. 38+1 gap (additional revocation/surrender, or counting difference) is Verify against DCR. Auditor also found microbusiness approvals that were too close to churches (constitutionally prohibited) and in areas prohibited by local ordinance — separate from ownership revocations.
  • Sources: Auditor report above; marijuanamoment.net — Final microbusiness lottery; stjosephpost.com — Microbusiness revocations.
  • Round 3 rule change (effective 30 May 2026, per secondary reporting — Verify final 19 CSR 100-1.190 text): Extensive review moves to before issuance; mandatory 3-hour pre-application online training on predatory practices; owners/agents/representatives of a denied/revoked microbusiness application/licence are barred from holding a voting or financial interest in any other microbusiness licence; eligible owners must have power to direct management, enter agreements and make business decisions.

4.4 Testing, transportation, warehouse, seed-to-sale

These are certifications in Missouri usage, not facility licences, except that testing is a constitutionally listed facility category.

TypeWhat it allows / key rule2026–27 fee (opened)
Testing laboratory / testing facilityIndependent potency and contaminant testing; every batch must pass testing before retail release. Constitutional minimum: 2 testing licences. Secondary sources state a testing facility may not share common ownership/management with cultivation, manufacturing or dispensary operations — Verify in Art. XIV §1 text. DCR also operates a state reference lab (secondary).Application $2,892.03; annual $2,892.03; renewal $2,892.03
Transportation certificationThird-party transport of marijuana product; vehicles unmarked, GPS + 1920×1080p video (driver + cargo), lockbox/locking cargo area, separate cash lockbox, facility-agent ID + driver's licence + Metrc manifest, phone contact at all times (19 CSR 100-1.140). Note: licensees may also transport their own product under facility rules; the certification is for dedicated transporters. Applications for transportation certifications are stated as accepted even while facility licences are closed (secondary, 2025–26) — Verify current status on the fee page, which lists the fee but says “cultivation, manufacturing, testing and dispensary licences and certifications are currently closed” without separately addressing transportation.Application $2,892.03; annual $2,892.03; renewal $2,892.03
Offsite warehouse certificationOff-premises storage for a licensee, subject to the same security rules (video, vaults, alarms).Application $2,892.03; annual $2,892.03; renewal $2,892.03
Seed-to-sale certificationCertification of a seed-to-sale tracking-system provider (the statewide system itself is Metrc; this certifies compliant systems/vendors). No expiry — no renewal application/fee required, unlike other certifications.Application $2,892.03; annual $2,892.03; no renewal

Inventory rule: 19 CSR 100-1.130: Justia — 19 CSR 100-1.130.

Research facility (forthcoming): 19 CSR 100-1.200 creates a marijuana research facility licence (reported effective 30 November 2026 — Verify). Research licensees may acquire and transfer marijuana product to/from cultivation, manufacturing and testing licensees, cultivate and process marijuana for research, transfer research-derived seeds and clones to cultivation facilities under an approved study, and conduct research on human and animal subjects, with research product tracked in Metrc and labelled “For Research Purposes Only” / “Not For Sale or Distribution” (secondary summary of the rule — Verify text). This is a Run 1 note only; full research/university treatment belongs in Run 2, Part 11.

4.5 Fees — DCR Fee Schedule, 1 July 2026–30 June 2027 (opened 30 Oct 2026)

Source: health.mo.gov — Fee Schedule.

  • Licences/certifications are valid 3 years; renewal application 90–30 days before expiry; annual fee is still due in a renewal year.
  • Fees marked * are CPI-adjusted annually (Midwest CPI-U, prior calendar year-end). The page still describes a 2.69% adjustment “to calculate the Fiscal Year 2026 Fee Schedule” — Verify: FY2027 CPI percentage, as the FY2027 table appears to carry forward FY2026 explanatory text.
  • Facility agent ID card: $75.00. Conversion (medical → comprehensive): $2,000.00. Change of ownership: comprehensive at least 10% new owner $5,000, at least 50% $8,000, location $5,000; microbusiness at least 10% $2,500, at least 50% $4,000, location $2,500; transfer to a different entity with the same ownership (microbusiness) $1,000.
  • Patient/caregiver/consumer cultivation fees are in Part 1 §1.3–§1.4 (patient/caregiver cultivation $57.83; consumer cultivation $115.68; patient/caregiver ID $28.92).

4.6 Are new applications open? (at 30 Oct 2026)

  • Comprehensive + medical cultivation, manufacturing, testing and dispensary: CLOSED. Fee page (opened 30 Oct 2026): “Applications for cultivation, manufacturing, testing and dispensary licenses and certifications are currently closed. At such time that new applications are to be accepted … the Department will post notice on this website.”
  • Microbusiness: CLOSED — Round 3 window closed 27 July 2026; no fourth round is constitutionally required. Entry now is only by (a) acquiring an existing licence (subject to change-of-ownership fees/approval above), or (b) being drawn + certified in Round 3 (issuance from December 2026).
  • Seed-to-sale, transportation and warehouse certifications: secondary sources (2025–26) say these remain accepted. Verify — the fee page's closed notice is ambiguous on this point.
  • Official information pages to cite (paths beyond the fee schedule could not be text-verified; do not invent deep links): DCR via health.mo.gov cannabis-regulation pages and Cannabis.Mo.Gov (named in the 9 September 2026 DHSS release as the microbusiness information portal); 19 CSR 100-1 via sos.mo.gov / Justia reproductions in Part 1.

4.7 Estimated startup costs — NON-OFFICIAL ONLY

No official DCR/Missouri startup-cost estimate was found. Do not quote any figure below as official.

  • Dated secondary, low credibility: Cure8 guide (undated in retrieval, pre-2024 fee data) — “$85,000–$150,000” for a medical dispensary including licence, land, security, POS, inventory, staff and taxes: cure8.tech — How to open a dispensary in Missouri — stale (it also wrongly states applications are open) and far below current market build-out reality; do not use without a freshness warning.
  • Generic national guide: Cannabis Training University (2026 page) — Missouri dispensary: application $6,000 (stale vs. current $3,083.40), licence $10,000–$25,000, startup range $300,000–$750,000: cannabistraininguniversity.com — Dispensary costs — non-official, generic, fee figures already out of date.
  • ClimbTheLadder (2026): state fees only are itemised; total startup “commonly run into six or seven figures depending on location and scale”: climbtheladder.com — Missouri dispensary costs/steps — vague but directionally safer than a false precise range.
  • Dossier-safe statement: State fees are $1,637 (microbusiness) to $28,920.33/year (comprehensive cultivation) plus $5,139/$3,083.40 application/renewal; all real-estate, build-out, inventory, security, POS, insurance, legal and staffing costs are additional and unquantified by any official source found.

PART 5 — LICENCE DATA SOURCES

This Part does NOT list every licence holder. It inventories every source of licence-holder data found. Reading rule for every row: “Dashboard” = interactive web table/map. No DCR dashboard in this research exposed a CSV/XLSX download in text fetch. Where a download exists, the exact file/link is given. Where it does not, that is stated explicitly — do not assume an export button exists.

5.1 DCR — Licensee Contact List Dashboard (the master licence-holder list)

  • URL (landing page, opened 30 Oct 2026): health.mo.gov — Licensed Facilities.
  • Holder types: all DCR facility licensees — comprehensive + medical cultivation (CUL), manufacturing/infused-products (MAN), dispensary (DIS), microbusiness wholesale (MBW), microbusiness dispensary (MBD), testing (TES). Also covers transport / offsite warehouse / seed-to-sale certifications only insofar as they appear in DCR's wider licensing (see annual reports — Verify whether transport appears as a row in this dashboard).
  • Format / access: interactive dashboard/table embedded on the page. Search by licence number, entity name, DBA/trade name, phone/area code. For dispensaries: facility address + filter by city and ZIP. Click-through to licensee website where supplied.
  • Update frequency (as stated on page): monthly, to reflect the previous month.
  • Latest data date: not stated in text fetch. Verify in live browser — the dashboard's “as of” date is inside the embedded table.
  • Fields available (as stated): licence number, entity name, DBA/trade name, phone, website (link), ATO status (“Not Approved to Operate” flag), and for dispensaries facility address/city/ZIP. Limitation: licensees not approved to operate or without a facility location have no address shown. It lists all active licensees regardless of ATO status — so “active” here ≠ “open to the public.”
  • Download: No CSV/XLSX download found in text fetch; no export button confirmed. Verify in live browser whether the embedded tool offers export/print. Do not cite this dashboard as a downloadable file.
  • Counts from this source: no total is extractable by text fetch. Use the market-study July 2026 “approved to operate” figures in Part 6 only with that label, and the secondary May 2026 synthesis (224 dispensaries, ~68 cultivation, ~91 manufacturing, ~10 testing) only as secondary, unverified against the dashboard.

5.2 DCR — Licensed Dispensary Map / Locator (facility locator)

  • Access: linked as “licensed dispensary map” from the Licensed Facilities page above (§5.1). The link target is an ArcGIS Experience page — full ArcGIS URL was not exposed in text fetch, so it is not reproduced here; reach it only via the §5.1 page. Verify in live browser.
  • Holder types: dispensaries only (comprehensive/medical/microbusiness dispensary as mapped).
  • Format: interactive map. No download found.
  • Fields / limitations: location pins for operating dispensaries; a dispensary not approved to operate or without a location will not usefully appear. Third-party trackers in October 2026 cited a “Verified Dispensary Locator” source dated 6 October 2026 — Verify whether that is the same ArcGIS map or a separate DCR list, and its update date. DCR's Verified Dispensary: We're on the List initiative launched 20 February 2026 (state-issued decal linked to the interactive map; product labels carry MAN/CUL/DIS/MBW/MBD/TES prefixes verifiable via the online lookup tool).
  • Separate verification tool: the same page links an “online lookup tool” to verify a facility number from a product label (MAN/CUL/DIS/MBW/MBD/TES). Full lookup-tool URL not exposed in text fetch — reach via §5.1 page; Verify whether it is the same as the Contact List Dashboard or a distinct licence-number checker.

5.3 DCR — Microbusiness lottery results and programme records

  • Programme page: health.mo.gov — Microbusiness Information — announcements, eligibility (Art. XIV §2), FAQs, business resources.
  • Round 3 results page (opened 30 Oct 2026): health.mo.gov — Microbusiness Lottery Results — Dated 9 September 2026. Methodology stated: identifiers by congressional district (2018 districts), district verified against proposed facility address; selection ≠ approval; DCR may request further information before issue, and may revoke post-issue if ineligible. Format / limitation: text fetch returned the methodology note only — the drawn identifier list/table or file was not extracted. No single consolidated “all microbusiness winners with names” CSV was located. 5.4 DCR — Data and Reports hub: sales, transfers, business-change, commencement, item-approval dashboards

  • URL (opened 30 Oct 2026): health.mo.gov — Data and Reports (stats.php).
  • Contents as stated on the page: (1) Missouri Cannabis Market Overview / sales data dashboard — sales January 2023 through current reporting period; updated monthly for the previous month; “monthly sales numbers should not be considered final” (reconciliations, corrections, returns, refunds). (2) Transfer History Dashboard — constitutionally mandated / legislatively approved transfers by fiscal year to Veterans Commission, Public Defender, DHSS substance-use-disorder grants (medical → Veterans only). See FY2026 figures in Part 2. (3) Business Change Application Dashboard — Q1 2022 to current; updated quarterly for the previous quarter. Covers licence transfers, at least 50% ownership changes, new owners, facility/warehouse location changes (19 CSR 100-1.100(2)–(3)). (4) Commencement Inspection Requests Dashboard — Q1 2023 to current; quarterly for previous quarter. Covers approval to begin operations / occupy new space / share space / change space use (19 CSR 100-1.030(3)). (5) Item Approval Applications Dashboard — Q3 2023 to current; quarterly for previous quarter. Covers product/packaging/label approvals (19 CSR 100-1.120).
  • Format / download: embedded dashboards only in text fetch. No CSV/XLSX download or export button confirmed for any of the five. Verify in live browser. Latest data date for each dashboard is inside the embed, not in page text.
  • Use: this hub is the correct citation for sales and transfer totals — but a researcher who only text-fetches it cannot quote its current totals. That limitation must be carried into any dossier table.

5.8 Patient / caregiver / consumer-cultivator statistics — counts only, no live dashboard located

  • No standalone public patient-count dashboard was located on health.mo.gov in this research. The Data and Reports hub (§5.4) as text-fetched contains sales/transfer/business-change/commencement/item-approval dashboards — not a patient/caregiver dashboard.

  • Secondary figures — conflicting, do not reconcile without DCR: February 2026 (secondary synthesis): 107,376 patients, 17,020 patient-cultivators, 13,605 agents, 2,098 caregivers, 2,132 consumer cultivators, 612 physician/nurse practitioners. May 2026 (MPP, citing state data): 87,745 patients (1.40% of state population): mpp.org — Medical patient numbers. November 2025 active patients 127,804; December 2025 patient applications 2,119 (highest since 2023) — trade press citing DCR: mogreenway.com — December 2025 patient applications. The February→May drop and the November→February pattern cannot be reconciled from these snippets (different definitions: active cards vs applications vs cumulative approvals are plausible causes). 5.9 Tax and local-tax adoption records — DOR

  • DOR marijuana tax page (opened 30 Oct 2026): dor.mo.gov — Marijuana tax. What it is: guidance + filing (Form 5808 Marijuana Tax Return; MyTax Portal; monthly filing; sales-tax item code 7004 for adult-use lines in secondary DOR summary — Verify code on the current return), registration via localgov@dor.mo.gov (public agency mailbox), and the rule that local tax takes effect the first day of the second calendar quarter after DOR notification. What it is NOT: it does not list, in text fetch, every city/county that has adopted the 3% tax.
  • Local-tax adoption record — the usable DOR source: the DOR page links “tax rate tables” that “include adult use marijuana tax rates” (outlink on the DOR page; full tables URL not exposed in text fetch — reach via the DOR marijuana page; Verify in live browser whether the tables are downloadable CSV/XLSX or web tables, their update frequency, and whether they distinguish city vs county adoption). Limitation after Robust (2025): a table showing both a city and a county rate for the same incorporated address is historic for stacking purposes. Post-Robust, only one 3% levy applies per sale (city in incorporated areas, county in unincorporated areas). Any local-adoption list must be read with that rule. No DOR-published count of adopting localities was located in this research. Secondary: “more than 70 localities” had stacked taxes pre-Robust. Verify current adopter count from DOR rate tables.
  • Local revenue use: local 3% revenue goes to the adopting locality directly (not to the state beneficiaries in Part 2). Local ordinances/ballot results for adoption are held by each city/county clerk / election authority — there is no single state register of local marijuana-tax votes located.

5.10 Missouri Department of Agriculture — hemp (historic only)

  • Historic registration list — exact PDF (STALE, do not use as current): agriculture.mo.gov — Hemp registrations and permits (PDF, 9 Sept 2022). Dated 9 September 2022. Fields: status, active-through month, registration number (for example 29_R00012, 29_P00010), registration type (Producer Registration; Agricultural Hemp Propagule and Seed Permit), business/entity name, city, state, ZIP. Inactive registrations listed at the end. Why stale: the Department of Agriculture's programme ended 31 December 2022 (see Part 0). This PDF is the last Department of Agriculture list located; no Department of Agriculture 2023–2026 hemp licence list exists because licensing moved to USDA.
  • Department of Agriculture programme pages (historic framework): agriculture.mo.gov/plants/industrial-hemp (certified sampler page confirms sampler certifications expired 31 December 2022 and directs samplers to USDA from 1 January 2023). Verify current Department of Agriculture hemp landing page — the Department may now only redirect to USDA.
  • Department of Agriculture does NOT license: intoxicating-hemp retail/manufacturing, or (from 12 November 2026) hemp-derived cannabinoid products — those fall to DCR/Article XIV under HB 2641.

5.11 USDA hemp records for Missouri (the current cultivation source)

  • USDA AMS — List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans (updated 17 February 2026, per page): ams.usda.gov — State and tribal plan review. Missouri entry: “USDA Hemp Producer License” — i.e. Missouri has no approved state plan; USDA issues producer licences directly. This is the key status fact. (Missouri's earlier approved plan is historic only.) Full hemp-licence treatment (producer/handler/seed functions, fees, plan history) belongs in Run 2, Part 7; this Part records only the registry route.
  • USDA Public Search Tool (for individual producers) — URL as cited in state-agency guidance: hemp.ams.usda.gov — Public Search Tool. Format: interactive search (not a bulk download in the sources found). Verify in live browser: search fields (name, licence number, state), fields returned, and whether any export exists. USDA's HeMP (Hemp eManagement Platform) is the licensing system; licences are valid up to 3 years (secondary, MU Extension).
  • Missouri producer counts from USDA — Verify: secondary sources put Missouri at 51 licences in 2023 and 109 licensees as of 29 July 2025. No USDA Missouri total as of October 2026 was located — do not carry the July 2025 figure forward as current.
  • Limitation: USDA licensing covers cultivation/production only. It does not license Missouri hemp processing, retail, or (post-HB 2641) intoxicating-cannabinoid handling.

5.12 Missouri Secretary of State — business entities (not a cannabis licence list)

  • Purpose: cross-check the legal entity behind a DCR licence (name, charter number, status, registered agent, formation date, filing history). It does not show DCR licence status, ATO status, or cannabis ownership percentages.
  • Access (secondary guides, Verify official landing in live browser): SOS Business Services portal at bsd.sos.mo.gov (Business Entity Search: by business name, registered agent, name availability, or charter number; no login required to search, per guides). SOS corporations landing cited as sos.mo.gov/business/corporations.
  • Fields (per guides): entity name (including prior names), type/domesticity, charter number, status, formation date, registered agent + address, principal office address, filings tab.
  • Limitations: does not show members/shareholders/beneficial owners for all entity types; sole proprietorships/general partnerships do not appear unless under a registered fictitious name; a dissolved/forfeited entity's name is not automatically available. Do not use SOS search alone to prove who controls a cannabis licence — that is precisely the ownership-data gap CPPC flagged in Part 0.
  • Also SOS: executive orders archive (EO 24-10 at Part 1) and ballot measures (Part 0) — both at sos.mo.gov.

5.13 Local (county / city / St. Louis City) permit and zoning records — published, but fragmented

There is no statewide local-permit register. Each jurisdiction publishes its own code/permits. Examples verified in this research — use them as the pattern, not as a complete list:

  • Kansas City, Missouri (KCMO — NOT Kansas City, Kansas): Official “Marijuana Regulations” page: kcmo.gov — Marijuana Regulations — process: Marijuana Zoning Clearance for Business License Affidavit + map showing distance compliance (schools/churches/daycares per Zoning Code §88-354), filed via CompassKC as “Zoning Clearance for Business License.” Code: Ch. 88, §88-354 Marijuana Facilities (Ord. 230124, February 2023, medical + comprehensive; later amendment Ord. 250997 proposing 24-hour operation for dispensaries at least 1,000 ft from residential zoning with a special use permit — Verify whether 250997 was adopted). Place-name care: KCMO straddles Jackson, Clay, Cass and Platte Counties, Missouri. Kansas City, KS is a separate city in Wyandotte County, Kansas, under Kansas law — never merge their permit records.
  • St. Louis City (independent city — NOT St. Louis County): Zoning Code amendment Ord. 71633, effective 16 February 2023, incorporating Article XIV comprehensive/microbusiness/testing uses and definitions (per St. Louis ordinance index: stlouis-mo.gov — ordinance index — Verify full ordinance text via the City's ordinance search). St. Louis City business/zoning permits are via the City's own permit system; St. Louis County has a separate county system and separate tax jurisdiction (see Robust, Part 2 — Florissant is a city within St. Louis County, which is why stacking arose there).
  • Other local examples (secondary only, Verify locally): Gladstone and Farmington ordinances banning synthetic-cannabinoid sales; a St. Louis Board of Aldermen bill to ban delta-8 outside dispensaries (introduced, outcome Verify). No statewide compilation of local hemp/cannabis bans was located.
  • General local-licence point: DCR's microbusiness guidance itself tells applicants to “contact the local governing body where the proposed facility is located to request information about zoning rules” — confirming that zoning approval is local and not in DCR's dashboard.

5.14 Other licence-adjacent sources (for completeness)

  • Metrc (seed-to-sale): Missouri's statewide tracking system, referenced in DCR guidance (licensees “continue to track daily operations directly in Metrc”). Not a public licence list — no public Metrc search was located. Recall package tags (for example 1A40C0300005E25000004098) are Metrc tags, which is why recall lists are tag-based.
  • DCR Online Registry Portal (mopublic.mycomplia.com, cited in DCR guidance): licensee-facing portal for business-update / business-change applications. Not public; do not cite as a public source.
  • DCR Licensee Communications & Guidance page: health.mo.gov — Licensee Communications & Guidance — guidance documents, rule variances/waivers, newsletters (The Cannabis Connection). Useful for rule changes (for example microbusiness rules effective 30 May 2026) but not a licence-holder list.
  • Missouri Legislature bill tracking (house.mo.gov / senate.mo.gov): for HB 2641 enrolled text and for the filed-not-enacted bills in Part 1 §1.9. Verify final dispositions there — trade-press tracking lists are not a substitute.

PART 6 — MARKET DATA

Method note for this Part. Regulator source: DHSS/DCR monthly sales reporting. The DCR dashboard itself was not text-retrievable — figures below are DHSS data via the monthly table and Greenway/MJBiz reporting of DHSS releases, triangulated where two sources agree. Where only a snippet/secondary compilation was available, that is stated. Do not present snippet-only figures as direct DCR dashboard extracts. Sales figures in this Part are the same figures as in Parts 0, 2 and 5; licence counts are the same as in Parts 4 and 5.

  • Pattern: Adult-use share rose 77% (2023) → 88% (2024) → 88.6% (2025) → ~91% in H1 2026; medical sales fell every year since adult-use launch, −29% year-on-year in May 2026. Growth is decelerating: +9.1% (2024), +3.9% (2025), +2.3% (H1 2026 year-on-year).

6.2 Product-category sales

No official DCR product-category sales table was found in text-retrievable form — Verify via DCR/Metrc reporting. What exists:

  • CPPC Missouri Cannabis Market Study Phase 1 (October 2026, for DCR; survey of 1,200 past-month consumers March–April 2026 + 35 months track-and-trace data): Flower ≈ half of reported monthly spending, $80.88/consumer/month; vape $28.40; edibles $25.69. Medical patients spend more in every major category: flower $96.00 (medical) vs. $76.70 (adult-use); edibles $45.61 vs. $20.19. Price is top purchase consideration (66%), then THC potency (58%), convenience (40%). Source: mmjdaily.com — CPPC Phase 1 findings.
  • Headset (dated secondary, POS data): Average item price fell $28.79 (May 2025) → $26.61 (May 2026), units +7.7% year-on-year — value-driven purchasing. September 2026 average item $26.44 (above).
  • Alternative cannabinoids (CPPC): Except CBD and THCA, past-month use low; THCA highest at 15%, exceeding several traditional categories including concentrates and beverages. CPPC did not recommend prioritising additional alternative-cannabinoid regulation at that time.
  • Commercial forecasts (Grand View $2.2bn 2025 / $2.6bn 2026 / $9.6bn 2033; Value Market Research $2.88bn 2025) conflict with DHSS retail sales ($1.52bn) and use unstated broader definitions — do not use in this dossier as market size.

Qualifying conditions: named list (cancer, epilepsy, glaucoma, PTSD, HIV/AIDS, terminal illness, Crohn's, etc.) plus a catch-all for any chronic/debilitating condition a physician certifies (Part 1 §1.4).

6.5 Official price data

None found. DCR publishes sales dollars, not a price series, in retrieved sources. Only Headset POS averages above ($26.44 September 2026; $26.61 May 2026 vs. $28.79 May 2025) and CPPC consumer-spending averages. Verify: whether DCR's sales dashboard includes average price per product type (19 CSR 100-1.130 requires seed-to-sale systems to be able to report average prices per product type, so the underlying data exists).

6.6 Market concentration

  • Official/commissioned (CPPC Phase 1, October 2026, for DCR): No evidence of overconcentration at facility level; Herfindahl-Hirschman Index values “well below thresholds associated with concentrated markets” in all 8 congressional districts; regulated dispensaries capture ~93% of reported consumer purchases (over 90% in every district); market “effectively meeting demand” and “mature, competitive”; no additional cultivation, manufacturing or dispensary licences currently needed (study conclusion, not a DCR licensing decision).
  • Critical limitation (CPPC + DCR Director Amy Moore, quoted in MMJDaily): Researchers could not determine ownership concentration — no usable data on who holds licences or whether differently-licensed dispensaries share a common owner. CPPC recommends a de-identified ownership database and demand-driven (not district-based) allocation review. DCR has since proposed rules requiring annual ownership-percentage reports (confidentiality applies).
  • Geography: Congressional District 5 (Kansas City) highest dispensary sales, ~$294m in 2025; District 6 fastest growth, ~+40% over 3 years.
  • Context: At least 2 antitrust lawsuits filed in 2026 alleging violations of Missouri law by cannabis companies (MMJDaily/Griffon News, October 2026) — details Verify.
  • Sources: mmjdaily.com — Market study, ownership-data gap; mmjdaily.com — 93% regulated capture.
  • No credible operator market-share table (for example top-5 MSO share) was found — Unavailable. Do not infer concentration from the 3/3/5 ownership caps alone.

6.7 Hemp — acreage and licence counts by year

Regulatory route — Verify current page: Missouri had a USDA-approved state plan (approved September 2020; amended plan authorised May 2021). Secondary sources state that from 1 January 2023 producers must licence through the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program (Missouri no longer issuing state producer licences). The Missouri Department of Agriculture hemp page could not be text-retrieved — Verify whether the Department of Agriculture still issues propagule/seed permits. Full hemp-licence treatment is in Run 2, Part 7.

  • 2020 (Department of Agriculture, via Ganjapreneur September 2020): 197 producers licensed, 75 Agricultural Hemp Propagule and Seed Permit holders, 3,696 acres registered: ganjapreneur.com — USDA approves Missouri plan (2020).
  • Early pilot: 2018 pilot capped at 2,000 acres statewide, 40 acres per farmer (superseded 2019, SB 133).

USDA NASS National Hemp Report series (planted / harvested acres, open-field):

  • 2025 acreage: Unavailable — no USDA 2025 National Hemp Report Missouri figure was retrieved; do not extrapolate from the licence count.
  • Drivers cited (Lincoln University Hemp Institute, agricultural economist Eleazar Gonzales, May 2025): few in-state processors (farmers travel long distances; Midwest Natural Fiber named), legal uncertainty over hemp-derived product restrictions, 0.3% delta-9 THC destruction rule, funding uncertainty for Lincoln's Climate Smart Hemp programme.

6.8 Hemp product market figures

  • No official Missouri hemp-product sales figure was found — Unavailable.
  • Dated secondary, methodology unstated: MJBizDaily (9 September 2026) describes Missouri's unregulated hemp-THC market as “~$1 billion”: mjbizdaily.com — Missouri moves intoxicating hemp into regulated market — treat as an unverified industry estimate, not a market size.
  • Forthcoming law (not yet in effect at dossier date): HB 2641, signed April 2026, effective 12 November 2026: intoxicating hemp products with over 0.4 mg THC per container are reclassified as marijuana and may then only be grown/manufactured in state-licensed facilities and sold in licensed marijuana stores; online sale of hemp-derived THC products ends 12 November; hemp THC beverages remain on shelves until 11 December 2026 due to a federal delay (DCR guidance, Part 1 §1.5). Missouri Hemp Trade Association said it would challenge the law. This will fold part of the hemp-THC market into the Part 6.1 regulated sales figures from late 2026 — flag as a break in series.

  • Browser limitation in this run: browser.open failed for every direct page fetch attempted in this run (transient worker error, "check for expired transient grants"), and the runtime instruction was not to retry those fetches. This draft therefore rests on browser.search result text from official domains plus the Run 1 files, NOT on freshly opened full page text. Where a fact below cites an official URL, the citation is to the search-returned official page, and the parent should re-open the page before final publication to confirm wording. This limitation is itself a Verify flag on every official-page citation in this draft — it does not mean the sources are wrong, only that full-page verification could not be completed in this run.
  • Run 1 baselines matched exactly (not silently changed): sales 2023 $1,338.34m, 2024 $1,460.53m, 2025 $1,517.26m monthly sum ($1,517.82m reported), H1 2026 $770.86m; licences approved to operate July 2026: 222 dispensaries / 63 cultivation / 85 manufacturing / 20 microbusiness; microbusiness Rounds 1 = 48, 2 = 57, 3 ~77 expected Dec 2026; HB 2641 Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act §195.900 signed 23 Apr 2026, effective 12 Nov 2026, DCR guidance 23 Oct 2026 (beverages to 11 Dec 2026); taxes 4% medical, 6% adult-use, local up to 3%; FY2026 transfers medical $13,000,000, adult-use $19,629,271 × 3; no 10mg/100mg edible cap — package ceiling is the 3-ounce equivalent (2,400mg THC); federal baseline as given in the task (Schedule III medical only eff. 28 Apr 2026, 91 FR 22714; adult-use Schedule I; 280E lifted medical only; hemp PL 119-37 §781 12 Nov 2026 limited by PL 119-103 §2019 until 11 Dec 2026, then total-THC + 0.4mg/container cap; USDA DEA-lab delay 31 Dec 2026; medical rider through 11 Dec 2026; no federal banking law); the 3 Nov 2026 election had NOT yet been held at the Run 1 date — no result is invented anywhere in this draft.
  • Same-name warnings applied: Kansas City, MO is not Kansas City, KS; Springfield, Columbia, Independence, St Joseph and O'Fallon below are the Missouri places; St Louis City and St Louis County are separate. Listings whose state could not be verified as Missouri were excluded from the Part 8 table and are listed in §8.4 with the reason.

PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES

7.0 Answer in one paragraph

Missouri does NOT run its own USDA-approved state hemp plan today. Missouri had a USDA-approved state plan (approved September 2020, amended May 2021), discontinued it effective 31 December 2022, and from 1 January 2023 Missouri producers licence directly through the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program (DHPP). That settles the Run 1 open question in favour of the Run 1 note: the Run 1 statement ("after 1 Jan 2023 producers licence through USDA") is confirmed by MU Extension, by the Missouri Department of Agriculture (MDA) sampler page, and by the current USDA AMS plan list, which shows Missouri as "USDA Hemp Producer License". There is no current Missouri hemp producer licence, hemp handler/processor licence, hemp retailer licence, or hemp seed/propagule permit to apply for — the historic MDA registrations/permits below are closed history, and the historic MDA registration list (PDF dated 9 Sept 2022) is stale and must not be presented as a current licence list. The only live hemp-production licence for Missouri is the USDA licence. After 12 Nov 2026, intoxicating hemp-derived cannabinoid products are not given a new DCR/MDA registration — HB 2641 routes them solely into the existing Article XIV licensed-marijuana system, and unlicensed sellers must cease (beverages with naturally occurring, non-synthesised cannabinoids excepted until 11 Dec 2026 under DCR guidance, if not sold under 21).

7.2 The live licence: USDA Hemp Producer Licence (DHPP, 7 CFR Part 990)

This is the only hemp-production licence available to a Missouri producer at 30 Oct 2026.

ItemDetailSource (accessed 30 Oct 2026)
What it allowsProduction (cultivation) of hemp — Cannabis sativa L. at or below the acceptable hemp THC level — on the lots/land listed in the licence, in a jurisdiction with no approved state/tribal plan. It does not by itself authorise manufacture or retail sale of intoxicating hemp-derived cannabinoid products, and after 12 Nov 2026 it does not authorise sale of such products outside Article XIV (see §7.5). It does not authorise marijuana production.USDA AMS, Hemp Production — https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp ; 7 CFR Part 990, Subpart C (USDA plan)
Who issues itUSDA Agricultural Marketing Service (AMS), Domestic Hemp Production Program.Same as above
How to applyCreate an account on the Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP) and submit a USDA hemp application via HeMP. Applications accepted on a rolling basis throughout the year, before possessing any hemp plants or seeds. Contact: farmbill.hemp@usda.gov / (888) 721-HEMP (4367) per AMS page (public agency contact, fine to reproduce).USDA AMS Hemp Production page — https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp ; USDA producer checklist (search return)
Application contentsApplication form information (including grower/land/lot information) plus a copy of an FBI Identity History Summary Check (criminal-history report). Background reports are due with the application and are valid for a maximum of 60 days prior to submittal (per USDA producer checklist). Verify the current HeMP form fields before publication — the form itself was not opened in this run.USDA AMS factsheet — https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/HempFactsheet.pdf (search return); USDA producer checklist (search return)
FeeNo USDA application/licence fee was found. MDA's own closure notice said producers could "pursue a USDA hemp licenses at no cost". The producer does bear all sampling and testing costs (see below). Adopted as: USDA licence fee $0 / no fee found — Verify against the current HeMP application before final publication, because AMS pages do not state the fee in the excerpts returned.Greenway/MDA notice, 19 Dec 2022 — https://mogreenway.com/2022/12/19/administration-of-missouri-hemp-moves-to-usda/ ; USDA producer checklist ("You are responsible for all sampling and testing fees")
Felony barNo licence if convicted of a felony related to a controlled substance in the last 10 years (2018 Farm Bill requirement; 7 CFR 990.21). Material falsification of an application makes the applicant ineligible for the USDA plan.USDA AMS factsheet (search return); 7 CFR 990.21 / 990.28 excerpts via eCFR mirrors — https://www.customsmobile.com/regulations/expand/title7_chapterIX_part990_subpartC_section990.28
Term / renewalValid until 31 December of the third year after the year in which the licence was issued (i.e. up to ~3 years); renew before expiry. Licences may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged or otherwise disposed of.7 CFR 990.22 (via govinfo/eCFR excerpts, search return)
LotsA "lot" is a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse or indoor structure planted with the same variety/strain. Each lot is sampled and tested separately; different varieties or non-contiguous areas = separate lots.USDA producer checklist (search return)
FSA reportingAfter licensing, establish the farm at the local USDA Farm Service Agency (FSA) office (deed/lease + licence certificate) and report hemp acreage to FSA (licence number, geospatial location, planted acres/sq ft, planting dates, intended use). FSA issues farm-tract-field-subfield lot IDs. Acreage report within 30 days of planting per secondary summary of 7 CFR 990.23 — Verify pinpoint.USDA AMS factsheet (search return); 7 CFR 990.23 (cited in search return)
Pre-harvest samplingA certified sampling agent (not the licensee — "As a hemp licensee you are not allowed to collect compliance hemp samples") must collect samples no more than 30 days before anticipated harvest, from the flowering tops (top 5–8 inches of main stem / terminal bud / central cola), at 95% confidence that no more than 1% of plants in the lot exceed the acceptable level. Do not harvest before samples are taken; harvest within 30 days of sampling or re-sample.7 CFR 990.24 — https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/7/990.24 (search return, verbatim excerpt below)
TestingLab measures total delta-9 THC post-decarboxylation (total THC incl. THCA). Enforcement of the DEA-registered-lab requirement is delayed to 31 Dec 2026 (USDA enforcement discretion — matches the Run 1 federal baseline). Measurement of uncertainty must be estimated and reported with results.USDA AMS Hemp Production page — https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp ("delaying until December 31, 2026, the enforcement of a requirement that all hemp must be tested by a Drug Enforcement Agency (DEA)-registered laboratory")
If a lot tests "hot" (>0.3%)Remediation or disposal. Remediation options include removing/destroying all flowers (and retesting remediated biomass with post-harvest samples); disposal via DEA-registered reverse distributor, law enforcement, or on-site methods (plowing under, composting, disking, bush mowing, deep burial, burning per USDA guidance). Submit a Remediation and Disposal Report via HeMP within 30 days of the activity. Producer pays all resampling/remediation/disposal costs.USDA producer checklist (search return); 7 CFR 990.27 (disposal, cited in search return)
Negligence regimeNegligent violations (max 1 per calendar year): failing to provide an accurate land description, producing without a licence, or producing cannabis above the acceptable THC level. No negligent violation if the producer made reasonable efforts and total THC is ≤1.0% dry weight. Each negligent violation = Notice of Violation + corrective action plan (minimum 2 years). 3 negligent violations in 5 years = licence revoked + 5-year ineligibility. Culpable (greater than negligence) violations are reported to the U.S. Attorney General and do not get the negligence protections.7 CFR 990.29 — https://ecfr.io/Title-7/Section-990.29 (search return)
Annual reportAnnual Production Report via HeMP on or before 15 December each year, for all licensees regardless of planting activity (planted / harvested / disposed acreage).USDA producer checklist (search return)
Recordkeeping5-year-style federal record regime under 7 CFR 990.32 — Verify retention period pinpoint before publication (excerpt returned did not state the years).7 CFR 990.32 (cited in search return)
Info linksAMS Hemp Production: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp ; AMS plan list: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review ; HeMP platform (via AMS page): https://hemp.ams.usda.gov/ (as given in USDA checklist search return — Verify live URL before publication) ; USDA Public Search Tool (licensee lookup, interactive, no bulk download found in Run 1): https://hemp.ams.usda.gov/s/PublicSearchTool (as cited in Run 1 Part 5 — Verify live URL)—

Verbatim (7 CFR 990.24, via Cornell LII search return, accessed 30 Oct 2026):

"§ 990.24 Responsibility of a USDA licensee prior to harvest. USDA licensees must: (a) No more than 30 days prior to the anticipated harvest of cannabis plants, have a sampling agent collect samples from the cannabis plant for total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration level testing."

Verbatim (7 CFR 990.29(a), via eCFR mirror search return, accessed 30 Oct 2026):

"Hemp producers do not commit a negligent violation under this paragraph (a) if they make reasonable efforts to grow hemp and the cannabis does not have a total THC concentration of more than 1.0 percent on a dry weight basis."

7.4 Other hemp-related registrations — what exists, what does not

Hemp handler / processor registration (standalone, Missouri)None found — no standalone Missouri hemp processor/handler licence exists. Under the historic MDA programme, processors did not need an MDA permit (MDA regulated viable hemp only; processing/transport/sale of nonviable hemp was not MDA-permitted), and higher-education research was exempt under SB 133. Under USDA administration, the DHPP licences production only; there is no USDA handler/processor licence in 7 CFR Part 990. A hemp processor in Missouri therefore needs only generally applicable authorisations (business registration, sales-tax licence, food/manufacturing permits if applicable — see below), not a hemp-specific handler licence. 7.5 Post-HB 2641: what happens to hemp-derived product retailers/manufacturers after 12 Nov 2026

Law: HB 2641 (2026), Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act, new §195.900 RSMo, signed 23 Apr 2026 (Gov. Mike Kehoe), §§195.010 + 195.900 effective 12 Nov 2026 (Run 1 baseline, matched exactly). Enrolled (Truly Agreed) text: Senate Substitute for House Committee Substitute, 103rd General Assembly, 6366S.11T, via house.mo.gov documents (search return).

7.5.1 Operative text (verbatim, from the enrolled text search return)

§195.900(3) (subsection numbering as in enrolled text):

"The cultivation, production, manufacturing, testing, transportation, and retail sale of all hemp-derived cannabinoid products within this state shall be conducted solely by entities licensed by the department under Article XIV of the Constitution of Missouri. Hemp-derived cannabinoid products shall be considered marijuana and shall be subject to the legal framework contained in Article XIV of the Constitution of Missouri…"

§195.900(4)–(5) (effect, search return):

"The term 'hemp-derived cannabinoid product' shall be construed to conform to, and be included in, the definition of 'marijuana' under Article XIV of the Constitution of Missouri but shall not be construed to conform to or be included in the definition of 'hemp' in this section or in the definition of 'industrial hemp' in this section."

"Hemp and industrial hemp shall not be considered marijuana and shall not be subject to the legal framework contained in Article XIV of the Constitution of Missouri or in this section. Nothing in this section shall be construed to regulate hemp, industrial hemp, or products that do not fall within the definition of hemp-derived cannabinoid products, and the provisions of this section shall not be construed to conflict with or [preempt the 2018 Farm Bill — wording truncated in search return; Verify full sentence on revisor.mo.gov]."

§195.900(7) (enforcement, search return):

"…all hemp-derived cannabinoid products are marijuana and shall be subject to the jurisdiction of the department and the office of the attorney general consistent with Article XIV of the Constitution of Missouri and the provisions of this section. The department and the office of the attorney general shall be authorized to enforce the provisions of this section in such a manner as to ensure that no hemp-derived cannabinoid products are cultivated, manufactured, tested, transported, or sold within this state outside of a licensed comprehensive facility, medical facility, marijuana testing facility, or marijuana microbusiness facility, as such terms are defined in Article XIV of the Constitution of Missouri."

Penalty (Run 1 baseline, confirmed in secondary analysis of enrolled text): $5,000 per transaction plus a Class D felony (§195.900(10) per Run 1; secondary summary: "A violation may result in a $5,000 fine per transaction and a Class D felony" — https://cannabusiness.law/missouri-hemp-rules-change-november-12-what-businesses-need-to-know/ , accessed 30 Oct 2026). Verify subsection pinpoint on revisor.mo.gov before final publication.

Definitions (enrolled text, search return): "hemp" = Cannabis sativa L. with a total THC concentration, including THCA, of ≤0.3% dry weight; a final hemp-derived cannabinoid product containing >0.4mg combined total THC per container is excluded from "hemp". "Hemp-derived cannabinoid product" = any intermediate/final product derived from hemp (other than industrial hemp) containing cannabinoids in any form, intended for human/animal use by any means (inhalation, ingestion, topical, etc.), excluding FDA-approved drugs (Run 1 Part 1). Intermediate-product exclusions exist in §195.900(2)(5) (stalk/fibre, grain/oil/cake/nut/hull, microgreens, non-commercial research, viable seed grown solely for those materials — Run 1 Part 1). Verify full definition text on revisor.mo.gov.

7.5.2 So: must a hemp retailer/manufacturer become an Article XIV licensee, or cease?

Answer: one or the other — there is no third, hemp-specific registration.

SituationConsequence from 12 Nov 2026
Unlicensed retailer/manufacturer of a covered hemp-derived cannabinoid product (see product table below)Must cease manufacture/sale in Missouri, or become / sell through an Article XIV licensee (comprehensive facility, medical facility, testing facility, or microbusiness facility). Sale/distribution without a marijuana facility licence "is prohibited and may result in fines or criminal prosecution" (DCR guidance, 23 Oct 2026). There is no HB 2641 transition/registration window, grace-period licence, or "hemp retailer" conversion application in the statute or guidance found.
Becoming an Article XIV licensee in practiceSeverely constrained at 30 Oct 2026: Run 1 Part 4 child report states comprehensive/medical/microbusiness applications are CLOSED at 30 Oct 2026, and the HB 2641 litigation complaint itself notes DHSS "has already issued the maximum number of cultivation, manufacturing, and dispensary licenses; it is not accepting new applications" (MoHemp complaint summary, Nutritional Outlook, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — https://www.nutritionaloutlook.com/view/missouri-hemp-businesses-sue-block-hb-2641 ). A hemp business cannot simply "convert" — it would need to acquire an existing licence/business, win a future application round (microbusiness Round 3 ~77 licences expected from Dec 2026 is the only near-term opening in Run 1, and it is lottery- and eligibility-restricted), or supply an existing licensee. Verify DCR application status at publication, because a new round would change this.
Licensed Article XIV facility handling hemp-derived productsMay acquire/use/sell low-THC hemp products from outside the licensed chain: hemp products ≤0.3% total THC, and hemp products with ≤0.4mg total THC per container (e.g. CBD, CBN, CBG) (DCR guidance). Higher-potency hemp-derived cannabinoids "are now considered marijuana and may only be produced by businesses licensed under Article XIV". Synthetic cannabinoids and cannabinoids produced through chemical conversion/modification remain prohibited for licensees even inside dispensaries (DCR guidance).
Product that is genuinely hemp / industrial hemp (not a "hemp-derived cannabinoid product")Not brought into Article XIV by §195.900(5) — stalk/fibre, seed/grain products, microgreens, compliant-seed and research exclusions, and products within the 0.3% / 0.4mg limits remain outside Article XIV. This is why the product-by-product test in §7.5.3 matters: HB 2641 does not ban all hemp commerce.
Interstate commerce / through-shipment§195.900(6) (Run 1): nothing prohibits interstate commerce in hemp or through-shipment of hemp through Missouri. Verify full text — this protects transit, not Missouri retail sale of covered products.

7.5.3 Product-by-product effective dates (Run 1 baseline, re-confirmed)

Product categoryFrom when treated as marijuana / Article XIV-only?Source (accessed 30 Oct 2026)
Cannabinoids the cannabis plant is not capable of producing naturally12 Nov 2026DCR guidance summary / secondary analysis — https://cannabusiness.law/missouri-hemp-rules-change-november-12-what-businesses-need-to-know/ ; Run 1 Part 1 §5
Cannabinoids cannabis can produce naturally but synthesised/manufactured outside the plant (converted/synthetic, incl. delta-8-type conversion products)12 Nov 2026Same as above
Smokable/vapable raw plant material, flower or bud containing any amount of THCA12 Nov 2026Same as above
Gummies, candies, capsules, baked goods and other solid edibles containing hemp-derived delta-9 THC above the concentration naturally occurring in the source hemp material12 Nov 2026Same as above
Beverages containing naturally occurring cannabinoids, not synthesised/manufactured outside the plantNOT under Article XIV until 11 Dec 2026, provided not sold to anyone under 21. DCR guidance (updated 23 Oct 2026): "Currently, beverages that have naturally occurring cannabinoids and were not synthesized or manufactured outside the plant will not be subject to the authority of Article XIV until December 11, 2026, provided that these products are not sold to individuals under 21 years of age."DCR HB 2641 Guidance, updated 23 Oct 2026 — https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation/licensee-compliance-guidance/missouri-house-bill-2641-guidance (accessed 30 Oct 2026, via search return)
Beverages that are synthesised/converted, or any beverage after 11 Dec 2026Article XIV-only from 11 Dec 2026 (converted beverages do not get the beverage delay — the delay is only for naturally occurring, non-synthesised beverages; disputed scope — see litigation note)DCR guidance (above); Run 1 Part 1 §5 product-date table
Hemp products ≤0.3% total THC, or ≤0.4mg total THC per container (CBD, CBN, CBG, etc.)Remain outside Article XIV; licensees may source them outside the licensed chainDCR guidance (above)

Disputed / Verify: The beverage-delay reading follows DCR guidance and the federal-timing link in §195.900 (products subject to federal hemp restrictions on/before 12 Nov 2026). The HB 2641 plaintiffs dispute it. Run 1 correctly states: "Do NOT present as settled." That caution is carried forward here.

7.5.4 DCR/MDA registration for intoxicating cannabinoid products — direct answer

  • DCR registration: NO new registration. DCR's role is (a) licensing Article XIV facilities (already-existing system, applications closed at 30 Oct 2026 per Run 1 Part 4), (b) guidance, (c) enforcement with the Attorney General under §195.900(7). No "intoxicating cannabinoid product registration", SKU registry, or hemp-retailer permit was found in DCR guidance or HB 2641.
  • MDA registration: NO. MDA has no post-HB 2641 intoxicating-product registration; its hemp programme closed in 2022 and its remaining role (if any) in HB 2641 enforcement is not stated in the sources found — Verify whether MDA retains any §195.900 role (the statute names DHSS/DCR + Attorney General).
  • Other agency route: NO. The 2025–2026 ATC-licensing bills for hemp beverages (HB 2768, SB 1026 summaries, etc.) were not enacted (Run 1 Part 1). Do not present ATC hemp-beverage licensing as current law.
  • Practical consequence for an unlicensed hemp shop at 30 Oct 2026: inventory review SKU-by-SKU before 12 Nov 2026 (11 Dec 2026 for qualifying beverages, 21+ only); covered SKUs must exit unlicensed retail by the applicable date or the seller faces $5,000/transaction + Class D felony exposure, plus Merchandising Practices Act enforcement of the kind the Attorney General used in 2026 (33 cease-and-desist letters 25 Mar 2026; Pressure STL assurance of voluntary compliance 22 Apr 2026 — Run 1 Part 0/5, secondary/official summaries, Verify docket details).

7.5.5 Litigation status (carried from Run 1, not re-litigated here)

  • MoHemp Trade Assn et al. v. Kehoe, No. 2:26-cv-04170 (W.D. Mo.), filed 16/17 July 2026; plaintiffs: MoHemp Trade Association, Lifted Liquids, Inc. (d/b/a Urb), MNG 2005, Inc. (St Louis retailer/manufacturer/distributor, 55 stores across five states); defendants Gov. Kehoe, AG Catherine Hanaway, DHSS Director Sarah Wilson. Motion to dismiss pending, no ruling located as of 30 Oct 2026 — Verify docket before publication. Arguments reported: conflicting hemp/marijuana definitions, vagueness, federal conflict, licence-cap / no-new-applications barrier, intrastate-manufacture requirement. Source: Nutritional Outlook (secondary summary of complaint) — https://www.nutritionaloutlook.com/view/missouri-hemp-businesses-sue-block-hb-2641 (accessed 30 Oct 2026); Run 1 Part 1.
  • No injunction is reported in the sources found — do not state that HB 2641 is stayed. If an injunction issues before publication, §7.5 must be rewritten.

7.5.6 Consumer-privacy provisions in the same bill (for cross-reference, not a hemp licence)

  • DCR guidance 23 Oct 2026: licensed dispensaries may not create/keep consumer-identifying records without the consumer's affirmative written agreement (§195.819; $500 per occurrence per Run 1 Part 1 — Run 1 states $500, not the $2,500 introduced-bill figure); state agencies may not disclose medical-marijuana patient information to federal authorities without a subpoena/court order (§195.800, Class E felony disclosure offence per Run 1). Mandatory patient/caregiver records and seed-to-sale (Metrc) tracking remain. Source: DCR guidance — https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation/licensee-compliance-guidance/missouri-house-bill-2641-guidance (accessed 30 Oct 2026, via search return).

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES (public listings, Missouri only)

8.0 How to read this part — and its limits

  • "Cannabis-zoned" in Missouri does not mean a single statewide cannabis zone. There is no statewide cannabis zoning district. Marijuana facilities need (a) a DHSS/DCR Article XIV licence and (b) compliance with local zoning + the constitutional distance rule: Article XIV §2.5(4) buffer of 1,000 ft from a school/daycare/church unless the local government allows a shorter distance (Run 1 Part 3), implemented locally — e.g. Kansas City §88-354 (see §8.1). A listing "marketed for cannabis" is therefore a marketing claim by the seller/broker, not a zoning determination — every entry below is flagged accordingly.
  • Listing status is marked Verify unless the listing itself shows active and recent. 420Property pages returned in search show "ACTIVE" labels, but the underlying listing detail pages (price, size, zoning, dates) could not be opened in this run (browser.open failure, see method note). Prices are therefore not adopted where the search excerpt does not unambiguously attach a price to the Missouri listing.
  • Result in brief: very few verified Missouri fee-simple properties. On the main cannabis-specific marketplace (420Property), the Missouri page shows only 5 listings total, of which only 3 are fee-simple/leasehold real property with a Missouri street address (one dispensary lease, one industrial lease, one farmland partnership/lease). The other two are a licence sale and a business/partnership solicitation with no property address. No verified Missouri property was found on Crexi or LandHub/Land.com in this run (see §8.4). This thin result is itself the finding — do not pad it with out-of-state or licence/business sales.

8.5 What a buyer must still verify for any §8.2 property (checklist for the parent's final file)

  1. Parcel zoning + permitted/conditional use for the intended facility type (dispensary vs cultivation vs manufacturing vs testing) with the host city/county planning office — a "cannabis" marketing label is not a zoning determination.
  2. Article XIV §2.5(4) 1,000-ft buffer measurement (school/daycare/church) and any stricter local distance (e.g. Kansas City dispensary-to-dispensary rule — Verify current text).
  3. DCR licence authority for that premises (licence transfer/relocation rules, 19 CSR 100-1 — Run 1 Part 4; a vacated dispensary does not carry a licence with it, per P8-1's own description).
  4. Local ban status for non-medical dispensaries (§2.5(1) — presidential-year ballot only; Run 1 Part 3).
  5. For farmland (P8-3): USDA producer licence for hemp, or DCR licence + zoning for marijuana — the land alone authorises neither.
  6. Listing currency: re-open the 420Property detail page at publication; "ACTIVE" in a search return is not proof the property is still available.

  • Sales: 2023 $1,338.34m; 2024 $1,460.53m; 2025 $1,517.26m by monthly sum ($1,517.82m as reported); H1 2026 $770.86m.
