PART 9 — REGULATORS AND HISTORY

9.1 Every state agency regulating cannabis/hemp

9.1.1 Lead regulator — DHSS Division of Cannabis Regulation (DCR)

  • Role: Regulates Missouri's medical and adult-use marijuana programmes. Constitutional authority: Article XIV, §§1 and 2; rules: 19 CSR 100. DCR's own About Us page states: "The Division of Cannabis Regulation (DCR) operates within DHSS. DCR is responsible for regulating Missouri's medical and adult‑use marijuana programs to help ensure safe access to marijuana. The Division's regulatory authority is established under Article XIV, Sections 1 and 2 of the Missouri Constitution, as well as 19 CSR 100." — Source: https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation/about-us — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Parent: Missouri Department of Health and Senior Services (DHSS).
  • Leadership (as published by DCR): Director Amy Moore; Deputy Director Erica Ziegler. — Source: same DCR About Us page — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Note: an earlier DCR newsletter / functional listing named a different deputy director; the About Us page is adopted as the current published listing. Verify at final compile against the live About Us page, as staffing pages change without a dated amendment.
  • Structure (DCR, Oct 2025 organisational chart / functional listing): Director's Office (including Policy Director, Public Outreach Director, Chief Equity Officer — the Chief Equity Officer "is responsible for development and implementation of programs to inform the public of microbusiness application opportunities through public education, targeted technical assistance, and making resources available to potential applicants"); Office of Operational Support; Office of Policy and Research; Section for Patient and Application Services (Individual Licensing Unit — patient/caregiver/consumer-cultivation/agent ID applications and call-centre front line; Facility Application Services Unit — facility applications); Section for Compliance and Enforcement (including Bureau of Investigation and Enforcement and Testing and Research Unit, which "verifies testing licensee compliance through inspections, compliance monitoring, statewide track and trace system reviews, education, and violation issuances and resolution" and may be contacted at the public TestingandResearchUnit@health.mo.gov address). — Sources: https://health.mo.gov/safety/cannabis/pdf/dcr-functional-listing-508.pdf (DCR organisational chart, updated 10/2025) and https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation/about-us — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Verify: the About Us summary lists "three offices and two sections" while the chart's headings differ in detail; do not present the internal unit list as a statute.
  • Public contacts (agency, not personal): PO Box 570, Jefferson City, MO 65102-0570; Toll-Free Access Line 866-219-0165 (9:00 a.m.–4:00 p.m. CST Monday–Friday; call centre closes last Friday of each month 9:00–10:00 a.m. for a mandatory staff meeting, per DCR page); general inquiries Cannabisinfo@health.mo.gov; complaints Cannabiscomplaints@health.mo.gov; outreach DCRC@health.mo.gov; system-issue reports CannabisDevelopment@health.mo.gov. — Source: https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation and https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation/licensee-compliance-guidance/licensee-communications-guidance — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Websites: https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation ; also cited by DCR as Cannabis.Mo.Gov. — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • What DCR does NOT regulate (boundary): DCR does not license hemp production (see §9.1.3, USDA route since 1 Jan 2023) and does not collect the marijuana tax (see §9.1.4, DOR). Its intoxicating-hemp role before HB 2641 was via DHSS food-code enforcement under EO 24-10 (see §9.2), not via Article XIV facility licensing; from 12 Nov 2026 HB 2641 brings covered hemp-derived cannabinoid products inside the Article XIV framework (Run 1 baseline — do not re-litigate here).

9.1.2 DHSS beyond DCR — food-code / environmental health limb

  • Role under EO 24-10: DHSS (Bureau of Environmental Health Services, food programme) was directed from 1 Sept 2024 to treat foods containing "unregulated psychoactive cannabis products" as from an unapproved source / containing an unapproved additive, and as deleterious/poisonous/adulterated under §§196.070 and 196.085, RSMo, with embargo/condemnation powers. DHSS stated 29 Aug 2024 that no FDA- or Missouri-recognised "approved source" existed, so the food limb operated as an effective ban on such foods until an approved-source framework existed. — Source: Run 1 EO 24-10 summary + DHSS 29 Aug 2024 position as recorded in Run 1 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (Run 1). Verify: current DHSS food-programme page for any post-HB 2641 successor guidance; EO 24-10 has no sunset clause on its face (Run 1) and no rescission was located in Run 1 — do not state it as expired without an official rescission.
  • Verbatim operative paragraph (EO 24-10, Run 1): DHSS, "beginning on September 1, 2024, to designate foods containing unregulated psychoactive cannabis products as foods that come from an unapproved food source or as foods that contain an unapproved food additive that comes from an approved food source as set forth in the 2022 FDA Food Code and the 2013 Missouri Food Code… [and] to find foods that contain unregulated psychoactive cannabis products are deleterious, poisonous, and adulterated under Sections 196.070, RSMo, and 196.085, RSMo, and to take the necessary steps… to embargo and condemn any food containing unregulated psychoactive cannabis products." (Quoted as in Run 1; pinpoint check against sos.mo.gov EO text at final compile.)

9.1.3 Missouri Department of Agriculture (MDA) — hemp (historical, now largely federal)

  • Historical role: MDA ran the Missouri Industrial Hemp Program: Producer Registration (to grow viable hemp) and Agricultural Hemp Propagule and Seed Permit (to sell/distribute viable seed/propagules). MDA regulated only viable hemp; it did not regulate processing, transport, sales or handling of non-viable hemp. Application fee in the state-programme era was $750 per application (secondary summaries of MDA forms; Verify against archived MDA forms — the fee is historical, not current).
  • Current position (official USDA list, updated 17 Feb 2026): Missouri is listed as "USDA Hemp Producer License" — i.e. USDA, not MDA, issues the producer licence. — Source: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Transition date: Missouri's state-run programme was discontinued 31 Dec 2022; from 1 Jan 2023 producers licence through the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program. — Source: MU Extension, https://extension.missouri.edu/publications/mx77 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (secondary, university extension, corroborating Run 1). USDA's list is the official confirmation; the MU Extension date is the dated secondary corroboration.
  • MDA public contacts (still published): Missouri Department of Agriculture, PO Box 630, 1616 Missouri Boulevard, Jefferson City, MO 65102; Tel (573) 751-4211; Email aginfo@mda.mo.gov. Historical hemp-programme email hempprogram@mda.mo.gov appears on archived MDA application forms — Verify before publishing as a current contact, as the programme has moved to USDA. — Source: http://agriculture.mo.gov/contact/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • What MDA does NOT do now: Do not present MDA as the current hemp-producer licensor. Any 2026 hemp-producer count must come from USDA (Public Search Tool / HeMP), not MDA. Run 1 producer figures (51 in 2023; 109 as of 29 July 2025, secondary) are carried as secondary, Verify against USDA.

9.1.4 Missouri Department of Revenue (DOR) — marijuana tax collection

  • Role: Registers, collects and remits the Article XIV marijuana taxes (4% medical, 6% adult-use, plus general sales/use tax and the local up-to-3% adult-use tax where voter-approved). DOR's marijuana page states: "Article XIV of the Missouri Constitution applies a 4% tax on the retail sale of marijuana for medical use sold at marijuana dispensary facilities within the state" and "applies a 6% tax on the retail sale of marijuana for adult use sold at marijuana dispensary facilities within the state"; and "Beginning November 12, 2026, hemp-derived cannabinoid products, as outlined in Section 195.900, RSMo, are subject to the legal framework contained in Article XIV of the Missouri Constitution, including all applicable marijuana taxes." — Source: https://dor.MO.gov/taxation/business/marijuana.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Public contacts (agency): Taxation Division, PO Box 3380, Jefferson City, MO 65105-3380; reporting email localgov@dor.mo.gov, phone 573-751-4876; registration PO Box 3300, Jefferson City, MO 65105-3300, email businesstaxregister@dor.mo.gov, phone 573-751-5860. MyTax Missouri portal for filing. — Source: same DOR marijuana page — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Boundary: DOR does not license facilities, set product rules, or decide expungement; it administers tax registration, returns (Form 5808, Marijuana Tax Return), local-tax distributions and, after Robust Missouri Dispensary 3 v. St. Louis County, SC100898 (22 July 2025, 6–1, Run 1 Part 2/3), refunds where a county tax was collected inside an incorporated city. DOR's 30 Jan 2026 refund-process memo (Form 472S/472P, 10-year window under §144.190.2) is a secondary-circulated DOR document — Verify against dor.mo.gov before quoting its procedure in final HTML.

9.1.5 Department of Public Safety (DPS) — Alcohol and Tobacco Control (ATC) and Highway Patrol

  • ATC — liquor-licence limb of EO 24-10 and HB 2641 enforcement support:

- EO 24-10 directed ATC "to amend its regulations such that unregulated psychoactive cannabis products are prohibited from being sold on the premises of a liquor-licensed facility." ATC's emergency rule under that limb was rejected by the Secretary of State (Run 1); the Governor then directed ATC to use regular rulemaking, with a 6–8 month delay noted at the time (secondary, Sept 2024). — Source: Run 1 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. - HB 2641 era: ATC Industry Circular 27-01, 21 Sept 2026, "The Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act", states: "Enforcement of the act's provisions is delegated to the office of the attorney general as having primary jurisdiction. The office of the attorney general may, in turn, authorize DHSS, the Department of Public Safety, ATC, the Missouri State Highway Patrol, or any other state agency" [to enforce]. — Source: https://atc.dps.mo.gov/IndustryCircular/circular-27-01.pdf — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Important distinction: ATC is therefore an authorisable enforcer under HB 2641, not the primary regulator; do not present ATC as licensing intoxicating-hemp retailers under HB 2641. (Contrast: 2025 bills such as HB 1328 that would have given ATC a hemp-retail licensing role did NOT pass — see §9.2; do not present them as law.) - ATC public contacts (central office, agency only): Division of Alcohol and Tobacco Control, Central Office, 1738 E. Elm Street, Lower Level, PO Box 837, Jefferson City, MO 65102-0837; Voice 573-751-2333 (general) / 573-751-2964 (circular letterhead); Fax 573-526-4369; Tobacco Control Program phone (573) 526-4026. Enforcement section has five districts with offices in Kansas City, St. Louis, Springfield and Jefferson City (Central District). — Sources: https://atc.dps.mo.gov/reports/ and http://atc.dps.mo.gov/enforcement/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Individual staff names/emails on ATC pages are NOT reproduced here (personal-contact rule).

  • Missouri State Highway Patrol (MSHP) — criminal / impaired-driving / fingerprinting role:

- MSHP is the statewide law-enforcement agency for impaired driving (including drug-impaired driving) and for general enforcement of Chapter 579 / Chapter 195 offences outside licensed activity; it is NOT a cannabis licensor. Article XIV §2 requires facility owners to "submit fingerprints to the Missouri state highway patrol for the purpose of conducting a state and federal fingerprint-based criminal record check in accordance with U.S. Public Law 92-544" — Source: Missouri Constitution Article XIV §2 via https://revisor.mo.gov/main/OneSection.aspx?section=XIV Section 2 — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (search-result extract; pinpoint Verify against Revisor text at final compile). - Article XIV §2 also states the section "is not intended to allow for … driving while under the influence of marijuana" — same Revisor source. MSHP roadside oral-fluid screening (SoToxa) was rolled out Nov 2023 with 18 units to MSHP (2 per troop) under a MoDOT Highway Safety grant — secondary programme report, Verify against MSHP/MoDOT before presenting device counts as current; the programme's existence is not a per se THC limit (Missouri has no statutory per se THC driving limit in Article XIV — see Run 1 Part 1). - Verify: MSHP public contact page / drug-enforcement unit listing was not directly opened for this draft; do not invent an MSHP cannabis phone number. General DPS/MSHP contact must be taken from dps.mo.gov at final compile.

9.1.6 Missouri Lottery — microbusiness lottery drawer (not a cannabis regulator)

  • Role: Conducts the random drawing for microbusiness licences, without reference to applicants' identities. DCR's 9 Sept 2026 release states: "To ensure the integrity of the applicant selection process, the Missouri Lottery conducted the drawing without reference to applicants' identities. Unique identifiers were assigned to each application, and each applicant was emailed their identifier prior to the lottery." — Source: DHSS/DCR release, https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/MODHSS/bulletins/429467b (9 Sept 2026) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Boundary: The Lottery does not set eligibility, review applications, or issue licences; DCR reviews drawn applications in order drawn for eligibility, and "the lottery selection does not guarantee the applicant a facility license" (same release). DCR microbusiness guidance states the same process: applications sorted by congressional district and licence type (wholesale/dispensary), sequential identifier assigned, results posted to DCR's website. — Source: https://health.mo.gov/business-professionals/cannabis-regulation/microbusiness-information/guidance-microbusiness-applicants — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Website: Missouri Lottery site not cited with a verified URL in this draft — Verify at final compile (do not guess a lottery URL). The DCR release is the authority for the Lottery's role, not the Lottery's own site.

9.1.7 State Auditor — oversight (not a day-to-day regulator)

  • Role: Audits DCR's administration of the Marijuana Program under the Auditor's constitutional audit authority. Report No. 2026-014, February 2026, rated the programme "Fair".
  • Key findings (official Auditor summary, 18 Feb 2026): flawed 2019 application scoring design undermining "blind scoring" (applicants allowed to create their own unique identifier, which could be based on the company name; review of 67 applications found 12 (18%) included such identifiers; 21 of 45 scorers (47%) made at least one scoring assessment contradicting DCR's own minimum); many licensees operated without ongoing inspections required by DCR's own rules, passing grades sometimes given without proof of compliance, minimal inventory inspections for diversion; dispensaries retained confidential customer information without consent; Metrc "does not currently have the capability to identify purchases over the legal transaction quantity limits in real time"; audit attributed more than $12.5 million in litigation/appeal costs to 2019 licensing deficiencies (secondary summary of the report — Verify the dollar figure against the full PDF before final use). Audit period: 5 years ending 30 June 2023; 366 licensed facilities approved to operate in that period; ~$1.23 billion retail sales generating ~$46.8 million sales-tax revenue in that period (Auditor's own framing, not the Run 1 sales series — do not merge). — Sources: https://auditor.mo.gov/AuditReport/ViewReport?report=2026014 and https://auditor.mo.gov/news/item/auditor-fitzpatrick-finds-the-states-marijuana-program-used-a-license-application-and-evaluation-process-that-lacked-consistency-and-transparency — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Licence-minimum figures via Auditor (Run 1): 24 dispensaries per congressional district = 192; 1 manufacturing per 70,000 inhabitants (2010 census) = 86; 1 cultivation per 100,000 = 60; 2 testing minimum — carried from Run 1, sourced there to Auditor Report 2026-014.

9.1.8 Attorney General (AG) — consumer-protection / unlicensed-market enforcement

  • Role: NOT the Article XIV licensor. Enforces the Missouri Merchandising Practices Act against unlicensed sellers of intoxicating products and, under HB 2641 Circular 27-01, holds "primary jurisdiction" for enforcement of the Intoxicating Cannabinoid Control Act (with power to authorise DHSS/DPS/ATC/MSHP).
  • 2026 action (official AG release, 25 Mar 2026): Attorney General Catherine Hanaway "announced that her Office sent 33 cease and desist letters to unlicensed retailers selling intoxicating cannabis and marijuana products outside of Missouri's Article XIV Constitutional framework", alleging adulterated/deceptive products in violation of the Merchandising Practices Act; breakdown 18 St. Louis region, 13 Kansas City region, 2 Springfield region. Consumers "encouraged to file a Consumer Protection complaint at ago.mo.gov". — Source: https://ago.mo.gov/attorney-general-hanaway-orders-33-unlicensed-dispensaries-to-cease-and-desist/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Earlier step (Run 1): 22 Apr 2026 Pressure STL AVC (City of St. Louis Circuit Court) — carried from Run 1, Verify docket/caption at final compile. A separate AG statewide investigation issuing Civil Investigative Demands to smoke shops (Nov 2025 CIDs reported in secondary coverage) is Verify against ago.mo.gov releases; do not present CID targets as adjudicated violations.
  • Boundary / correction: Secondary summaries that attribute the 2025 AG enforcement unit to "Attorney General Andrew Bailey" describe the predecessor AG; the Mar 2026 release is under Hanaway. Do not merge the two office-holders into one action.

9.1.9 Courts — expungement and review (not regulators, but the expungement engine)

  • Role: Missouri circuit courts (with the Office of State Courts Administrator, OSCA, as administrative coordinator) execute Article XIV §2 automatic expungement. OSCA supplied courts with lists from digital records and Highway Patrol data as a starting point; clerks then manually reviewed paper files (paper filing largely to ~2014; first marijuana-related drug statutes cited to OSCA guidance as 1971 — a figure disputed in secondary commentary, see Gaps).
  • Deadlines (Article XIV §2, Run 1): Misdemeanours by 8 June 2023; felonies by 8 Dec 2023. Both deadlines were missed in practice (see §9.2 timeline). Interim deadlines also reported in secondary coverage: petitions for incarcerated persons immediate; pending misdemeanour/pretrial matters by 8 Mar 2023; Class E felony matters by 6 June 2023; Class D by 4 Sept 2023 — Verify each interim date against Article XIV §2 text at final compile; they are not adopted as verbatim constitutional text here.
  • Counts — CONFLICT, do not reconcile silently (see Gaps): >100,000 by Dec 2023/Jan 2024 (NORML/OSCA via Marijuana Moment, Dec 2023); ~123,000 as of mid-May 2024 (Missouri Supreme Court figures via Missouri Independent); >140,000 by Jan/Feb 2025 (JDP summary, 12 Feb 2025, citing Missouri courts); ~150,000 quoted Oct 2025 (secondary, Run 1); Supreme Court estimate ~307,000 cases reviewed, ~46% expunged, excluding paper records (Missouri Independent / IBN summary, 2024–2025). Run 1 adopted "~140,000 by Jan 2025 as latest dated count with Verify for 2026 total" — that adoption is RETAINED here; no 2026 OSCA total was located for this draft (Unavailable — see Gaps).
  • Judicial narrowing (Run 1, Verify): C.S. v. Lafayette County Prosecuting Attorney, SC100944, 22 July 2025, narrows "marijuana offense" (weapon offence held not a marijuana offence) — carried from Run 1; pinpoint and holding Verify against courts.mo.gov opinion before final use. Lozano SCOTUS petition No. 26-5456 filed 1 Sept 2026, pending — carried from Run 1, Verify docket.
  • Municipal-court question (secondary, Verify): EVOLUTION Magazine Mar 2025 argues tens of thousands of municipal marijuana cases remain unexpunged and that municipal courts, as subdivisions of circuit courts, are not exempt; a Court of Appeals case was said to be pending to clarify. This is advocacy/secondary, NOT a court holding — do not present municipal exemption or its rejection as decided law.

2014 (July, signed; criminal-code changes effective 1 Jan 2017)HB 2238 — Missouri Hemp Extract Registration Program (MHERP): residents with intractable epilepsy could possess hemp extract ≤0.3% THC and ≥5% CBD, on a neurologist's recommendation, via a registration card; two production/cultivation centres licensed by MDA. 9.3 Ballot history with official results

9.3.1 Name-collision warnings (read before using any "Amendment 2/3" figure)

  • Amendment 2 (2018) = medical marijuana. Amendment 2 (2020) = Medicaid expansion (676,687 yes / 593,491 no, 53.27% — Ballotpedia). Amendment 2 (2024) = sports betting. These are different measures that happen to share a number. Any citation of "Amendment 2 passed with X%" must carry its year.
  • Amendment 3 (2018) = FAILED medical marijuana (15% tax / research institute). Amendment 3 (2022) = PASSED adult-use marijuana. Amendment 3 (2024) = reproductive rights. Amendment 3 (2026) = reproductive-rights repeal / gender-care measure (on the 3 Nov 2026 ballot, not yet voted at draft date). Same warning applies.
  • Proposition C (2018) = FAILED statutory medical marijuana. Proposition C in other years/states is unrelated.

9.3.3 What the losing 2018 measures would have changed (why three measures were on one ballot)

  • If all three 2018 measures had passed, conflicting constitutional/statutory provisions would have arisen; in practice Amendment 2's passage and the others' failure avoided that conflict. Amendment 3 (2018) differed principally in its 15% tax and its creation of a state Biomedical Research and Drug Development Institute; Proposition C differed principally in being a statute (2% tax) rather than a constitutional amendment, which would have left the legislature freer to amend it. These distinctions are from Ballotpedia measure titles/summaries and MPP's 2018 summary ("Amendment 2 … passed with 66% … Amendment 3 and Proposition C — failed 69%–31% and 56%–44%, respectively" — https://www.mpp.org/policy/ballot-initiatives/2018-ballot-initiatives/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, rounding differs from certified percentages above; certified percentages govern).
  • Turnout context (secondary): 2018 general-election turnout ~58.23% (Wikipedia, 2018 Missouri elections) and ~55% per a St. Louis midterm roundup — Verify against SOS turnout release; do not present either turnout figure as certified in final HTML without that check.

9.3.4 Later measures and the 3 Nov 2026 ballot — verified, no cannabis measure, no results invented

  • SOS certified list for 3 Nov 2026 (as reported Oct 2026, before the election): five statewide measures — Amendment 3 (HJR 73, reproductive-rights repeal / gender-transition care for minors), Amendment 6 (initiative/referendum protections, Petition 2026-106), Amendment 7 (Show-Me Prosperity Fund endowment), Amendment 8 (county sheriffs), Proposition A (referendum on HB 1, 2025 extraordinary-session congressional map). No cannabis or hemp measure is among them. Amendments 4 and 5 were August 2026 primary measures, not November measures; numbers are reused across years (2026 Amendment 3 ≠ 2022 Amendment 3). — Sources: SOS fair-ballot-language publication for the 3 Nov 2026 general election (via Christian County reproduction: https://www.christiancountymo.gov/wp-content/uploads/Fair-Ballot-Language-11-3-2026.pdf) and secondary ballot guides summarising the SOS certified list (Pulaski Talks, 16 Oct 2026: https://pulaskitalks.com/missouri-november-2026-ballot-guide-receipts/; Ballot Explained: https://ballotexplained.com/states/missouri) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026. Verify at final compile directly against sos.mo.gov's 2026 ballot-measures page; the SOS page itself was not text-extracted for this draft.
  • CONFLICT FLAGGED — do not adopt: A secondary CannaCon archive item (5 May 2026) states "voters are expected to weigh in on a proposed amendment this November that would remove licensing caps for cultivation, manufacturing, testing and dispensary operations." That claim is INCONSISTENT with the SOS certified five-measure list above, which contains no licensing-cap measure. It is NOT adopted. Possible explanations (a filed-but-uncertified initiative; a misdated reference to another state's measure; a proposal that failed to qualify) are all Verify — check SOS initiative filings for 2026 and do not present a cap-removal vote as scheduled.
  • Single Market initiative (filed Sept 2025, secondary): Run 1 notes a "Single Market initiative filed Sept 2025 — status Verify, not certified for Nov [2026]". Retained as Verify; no signature, certification or ballot status is asserted here.
  • No later statewide cannabis/hemp vote: No statewide cannabis/hemp measure passed or failed in 2019, 2020, 2021, 2023, 2024 or 2025 is identified in this draft's research. Local votes (local taxes 2023, local ban questions) are Run 1 Part 3 territory, not statewide ballot history, and are not repeated as statewide measures here.

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.1 Federally recognised tribes, land and cannabis/hemp operations in Missouri — conclusion: NONE

Plain conclusion: No federally recognised tribe is headquartered in Missouri, no federally recognised tribe holds a reservation or trust land in Missouri, and no tribal cannabis or hemp operation under tribal jurisdiction has been identified in Missouri. Missouri law (Article XIV licensing, Chapter 579/195 enforcement, DOR taxation) therefore applies statewide without a tribal-jurisdiction carve-out, and there is no Missouri tribal cannabis compact, tribal licensing programme, or tribal hemp plan to report. The reasoning and its limits are set out below so the parent can defend the negative.

10.1.1 Official basis — the BIA list

  • The Bureau of Indian Affairs (BIA) publishes the list of "Indian Tribal Entities Within the Contiguous 48 States Recognized by and Eligible To Receive Services From the United States Bureau of Indian Affairs" in the Federal Register under 25 U.S.C. §§2, 9 and Pub. L. 103-454 §104. The 29 Jan 2021 list (86 FR, document 2021-01606) contains NO entity headquartered in Missouri and NO Missouri reservation entry; the only Missouri-name entry is the "Sac & Fox Nation of Missouri in Kansas and Nebraska" — a tribe whose name preserves a Missouri history but whose government and land are in Kansas/Nebraska, not Missouri. — Source: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/html/2021/01/29/2021-01606.html — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Currency caveat: The 2021 Federal Register list is the list text-extracted for this draft; BIA updates the list periodically (most recently, by the search record, a 2025 list of 575 tribes nationally). Verify at final compile against the current BIA Tribal Leaders Directory / current Federal Register list that no Missouri-headquartered tribe has been added since 2021. The negative conclusion is nevertheless strongly corroborated by two independent Missouri sources below, which state it in terms.
  • Corroboration 1 (State Historical Society of Missouri, official state history body): "There are no federally recognized Native American tribes within the state today" and "By the 1830s most Native Americans had been pushed out of Missouri." — Source: https://shsmo.org/research/guides/native-american — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • Corroboration 2 (state-by-state BIA-derived list): Missouri entry reads "No federally recognized tribes" (same entry as Kentucky and other no-tribe states). — Source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federally_recognized_tribes_by_state — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (secondary compilation of the BIA list — used only as corroboration, not as the primary authority).
  • Corroboration 3 (USDA Forest Service, Mark Twain National Forest Tribal Relations): the Forest Service lists tribes "connected with" Missouri / beneficiaries of Missouri land-cession treaties (including Osage Nation, Iowa Tribe of Kansas and Nebraska, Kaw Nation, Miami Tribe of Oklahoma, etc.) as consultation partners for federal land in Missouri, not as Missouri-resident tribes. That distinction matters: treaty/consultation connection ≠ present-day Missouri tribal land or jurisdiction. — Source: https://www.fs.usda.gov/main/mtnf/workingtogether/tribalrelations — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.

10.1.2 Missouri tribal history (why the negative is historical, not an oversight)

  • At statehood (1821) Missouri's Native population was estimated at ~20,000, including Kickapoo, Shawnee, Ioway, Otoe, Delaware and Osage peoples; the Osage were the most powerful, with territory across present-day Missouri, Kansas, Oklahoma and Arkansas (SHSMO, above). In an 1808 treaty the Osage gave up most of their land in southern Missouri Territory (while believing hunting/fishing rights continued).
  • Through the Indian Removal Act era (1830) and the 1830s forced relocations, most Native peoples were pushed out of Missouri to Indian Territory (present-day Oklahoma, Nebraska, Kansas); Cherokees passed through Missouri on the Trail of Tears. The state name itself derives from the Missouria (Missouri) people ("one who has dugout canoes"; self-name Niuachi, "People of the river's mouth") — a linguistic legacy, not a present tribal government in Missouri (SHSMO, above).
  • No reservation/trust land today: No current federally recognised reservation exists within Missouri's boundaries (secondary explainer, corroborating SHSMO/BIA: "there are no current federally-recognized reservations in Missouri" — https://www.studycountry.com/wiki/why-are-there-no-reservations-in-missouri — Accessed 30 Oct 2026, low-authority secondary, used only as corroboration). BIA's Indian-land map likewise shows no Missouri tribal-land polygon (map PDF via govinfo — text extraction too degraded to cite for boundaries; Verify against BIA Land Area Representations / TIGER/Line AIANNH data at final compile if a map is required — do not reproduce a map from this draft alone).
  • Consequence for cannabis/hemp: Because there is no tribal land in Missouri, the federal tribal-hemp-plan route (7 CFR Part 990 tribal plans) and tribal marijuana jurisdiction questions that arise in states with reservations (e.g. tribal licensing vs state licensing, tribal tax compacts, Public Law 280) do not arise in Missouri. No Missouri agency needs a tribal-consultation or compact section for cannabis/hemp, and none was found. If a future federal recognition or trust acquisition occurred, this section would need rewriting — no such pending petition outcome was located (Verify BIA Office of Federal Acknowledgment pending-petition list at final compile for completeness).

10.1.3 State-recognised tribes/groups and claimed operations — examined, NOT verified as state-recognised

This is the section most vulnerable to error, so claims and counter-evidence are kept separate:

Group / claimWhat the group claimsWhat official / reliable evidence showsStatus for final HTML
Northern Cherokee Nation of the Old Louisiana Territory (NCNOLT), Columbia, Missouri (501(c)(3) nonprofit, incorporated 15 Sept 1978 as Northern Cherokee Tribe of Indians; renamed 17 Mar 2014)Its website states: "The Northern Cherokee Nation is the only Tribe of Indians officially recognized by the Missouri government." — http://ncnolt.com/Who-Are-We — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.The claim rests on a June 1983 proclamation/letter from Gov. Kit Bond acknowledging the group's existence and declaring 24 June 1983 Northern Cherokee Recognition Day. Multiple reliable assessments conclude this did NOT create state recognition: (a) Missouri has no established statutory process for state tribal recognition; (b) the National Conference of State Legislatures master list records Missouri as recognising no tribes except federally recognised ones; (c) a state-recognition bill (HB 1742, 2012, recognising the Northern Chickamauga Cherokee Nation, §1.198) was introduced but did NOT become law (its text would have expressly denied any land claim: "Nothing in this section shall be construed as a recognition of any claim to land"). A later governor (John Ashcroft) vetoed a recognition bill on the ground that only the federal government could recognise tribes.UNVERIFIED / NOT ADOPTED as state-recognised. Present NCNOLT only as a self-identified heritage nonprofit that claims state recognition, with the counter-evidence stated. Do NOT list it as "Missouri's state-recognised tribe". DCR licensees operate under Article XIV, not tribal law; USDA hemp licensees in Missouri operate under the federal programme, not a tribal plan.Unavailable / no operation found. If the parent locates a claimed operation, it must be reported as a claim with the operator's licensing basis checked against DCR/USDA lists before any tribal characterisation.

10.1.4 What "no tribal presence" does and does not mean for consumers and businesses

  • A Missouri dispensary, cultivator or manufacturer cannot claim tribal sovereign immunity, tribal tax exemption, or tribal licensing as a basis to operate outside Article XIV/DCR rules in Missouri — no tribal jurisdiction exists in-state to confer it.
  • Individual Native Americans in Missouri are, of course, subject to the same Missouri cannabis/hemp law as any other resident (medical card, adult-use purchase, home-grow rules); tribal citizenship in an out-of-state tribe (e.g. Osage Nation, Cherokee Nation) does not create a Missouri cannabis right, and Missouri's out-of-state medical-card possession protection (Article XIV §1.5(1), Run 1 — purchase right NOT established) is a state-law protection, not a tribal-reciprocity rule.
  • Federal Indian Country drug law (e.g. 18 U.S.C. §1152/§1153, tribal jurisdiction over Indian Country) has no Missouri Indian Country to attach to for cannabis/hemp purposes. Do not import other states' tribal-cannabis analyses into Missouri.

10.2 Interstate — what can and cannot cross a Missouri border

10.2.1 The governing distinction (three different regimes, do not conflate)

  1. Marijuana (including, from 12 Nov 2026, HB 2641-covered hemp-derived cannabinoid products treated as marijuana): Interstate transport is prohibited by federal law and by Missouri law. Missouri's Article XIV protection is intrastate: it protects possession/purchase/use in Missouri under Missouri rules. It does not authorise export, and it does not protect the same product once it is in a neighbouring state whose law prohibits it.
  2. Compliant hemp / hemp products (pre-12 Nov 2026 definition; post-11 Dec 2026 total-THC definition — given federal baseline): Interstate transportation/shipment through a state is federally protected for hemp produced in accordance with a USDA-approved plan or the federal plan. That protection covers transit; it does not compel a destination state to allow retail sale into that state of a product that state bans.
  3. A lawful purchase in Missouri by a non-resident: Adult-use purchase in Missouri requires only age 21+ and valid ID — residency is not required (see §10.2.3). The purchase is lawful in Missouri; carrying the product home is a separate act governed by the home state's law and federal transport law.

10.2.2 Verbatim federal hemp-transport rule (law, quote it — do not paraphrase it away)

2018 Farm Act §10114 (Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018), codified in substance at 7 U.S.C. §1639o note / §10114:

"(a) Rule Of Construction.—Nothing in this title or an amendment made by this title prohibits the interstate commerce of hemp (as defined in section 297A of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (as added by section 10113)) or hemp products."

"(b) Transportation Of Hemp And Hemp Products.—No State or Indian Tribe shall prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with subtitle G of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (as added by section 10113) through the State or the territory of the Indian Tribe, as applicable."

— Source: 2018 Farm Act §10114 as quoted in https://cultivalaw.com/protecting-transportation-under-the-2018-farm-bill/ and https://cannabusiness.law/usda-hemp-regulations-analysis-interstate-transport-of-hemp/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (law-firm summaries quoting the statute; pinpoint Verify against the enrolled Farm Act / U.S. Code at final compile). USDA's hemp rule federalism discussion concludes in the same terms: "States and Indian Tribes may not prevent the movement of hemp through their states or territories even if they prohibit its production" (USDA Interim Final Rule, 29 Oct 2019, 7 CFR Part 990 — as summarised in the Kight analysis above). Limit: the protection is for hemp "produced in accordance with" the federal/state plan. Hemp that fails the THC test (delta-9 >0.3% pre-Dec 2026; total-THC failure post-11 Dec 2026 under the given baseline) is not compliant hemp and loses the protection; it is treated as marijuana for transport purposes. Practical consequence for Missouri shippers: A Missouri USDA-licensed producer shipping compliant hemp through Kansas, Iowa or any other state to a lawful destination is federally protected in transit, but must still carry plan-compliance documentation (licence, test results) because roadside THC field tests cannot distinguish hemp from marijuana — a practical enforcement risk, not a legal exception. No Missouri statute found in this draft creates a Missouri hemp-transport permit or a Missouri hemp-shipment manifest — Verify against §§195.740–195.767 (historical MDA era) and USDA HeMP guidance at final compile; do not invent a manifest requirement.

10.2.3 Out-of-state buyers — who can buy in Missouri, and what happens at the border

  • Adult-use: Any adult 21+ with valid government photo ID may purchase at a licensed Missouri dispensary, regardless of residency. Missouri is listed among states that "sell to any visitor 21 or older" (secondary 50-state visitor guide, 25 Oct 2026: Alaska, Arizona, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, Illinois, Maine, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, Montana, Nevada, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, Ohio, Oregon, Rhode Island, Vermont, Washington). — Source: https://markets.financialcontent.com/smdailypress/article/marketersmedia-2026-10-25-alexis-420-publishes-a-50-state-guide-to-where-tourists-can-legally-buy-weed-ahead-of-holiday-travel — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (secondary directory — corroborates Run 1 adult-use purchase rule; DCR consumer guidance is the authority at final compile).
  • Medical (visiting patients): Missouri's Article XIV §1.5(1) gives possession protection to a qualifying patient with an equivalent out-of-state card (Run 1); whether a visiting patient may purchase in Missouri was NOT established in the rule text read in Run 1 (Verify — do not state a purchase right for visiting patients without that check). The 50-state guide above lists Arkansas, Hawaii, Kentucky, Louisiana, Mississippi, New Hampshire, North Dakota, Oklahoma, South Dakota and Utah as "visiting-patient purchase" states and does NOT list Missouri in that category — consistent with the Run 1 caution, but the guide is secondary and is not adopted as law.
  • No Missouri export licence, no reciprocity purchase scheme, no border-check programme was found. Dispensaries are not required by any rule located in this draft to ask about a buyer's home state or intended destination, and no Missouri rule was found that deems a sale to a known out-of-state buyer unlawful as a Missouri sale — the unlawfulness, where it exists, attaches to the transport/possession in the destination state and under federal law.

10.2.4 Neighbouring-state map (Missouri's eight land neighbours — status at 30 Oct 2026, secondary summaries, NOT law)

Missouri borders eight states: Iowa (north), Illinois (east), Kentucky (southeast, Mississippi River), Tennessee (southeast), Arkansas (south), Oklahoma (southwest), Kansas (west), Nebraska (northwest). The table gives the direction of travel risk, not a full dossier on each neighbour (those dossiers are separate commissions). Neighbour-law summaries are secondary (Oct 2026 50-state guides) and must be re-checked against each neighbour's official code at final compile if the final HTML reproduces them; the Missouri-side rule (lawful purchase in Missouri; unlawful transport out) does not depend on them.

NeighbourMarijuana status (secondary summary, Oct 2026)What a Missouri purchase taken there meansSource — Accessed 30 Oct 2026
Kansas (west; Kansas City metro straddles the line)No medical, no adult-use; marijuana Schedule I under Kansas law; possession of any amount criminal (1st offence Class B misdemeanour, up to 6 months / $1,000 fine; 2nd Class A; 3rd+ felony, per secondary summary of K.S.A. 21-5706). Limited CBD defence (Claire and Lola's Law, 2019: CBD oil ≤5% THC relative to CBD). Kansas does NOT recognise out-of-state medical cards.A Missouri purchase carried into Kansas is unlawful in Kansas AND a federal transport offence. Kansas City, Kansas police/prosecutors apply Kansas law; the state line (State Line Road) is the legal boundary, not the metro area. ~2,200 Kansas residents hold Missouri medical cards (secondary) — that card gives zero protection in Kansas.https://legalclarity.org/kansas-medical-marijuana-laws-compliance-and-legal-overview/ ; https://thecannex.com/kansas-cannabis-legalization-poll/ (Kansas Speaks Fall 2026: 65% medical support, 58.3% recreational support, 603 adults, 23 Sept–16 Oct 2026 — opinion only, Kansas has no citizen-initiative route so the legislature alone can change the law) — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Illinois stores may stay open to 2 a.m. with local approval (from 12 June 2026, secondary).Missouri→Illinois: carrying Missouri marijuana into Illinois is unlawful as transport, even though Illinois has its own legal market — Illinois legality attaches to Illinois-licensed product, not to imported product. Illinois→Missouri: carrying Illinois product into Missouri is likewise unlawful transport, even though Missouri has its own legal market. Arkansas (south)Medical only (patients only; visiting patients may buy under Arkansas rules per the 50-state guide). No adult-use.Missouri adult-use product taken into Arkansas is unlawful unless the carrier is an Arkansas qualifying patient acting within Arkansas's programme — and even then, Arkansas's programme does not authorise importation of Missouri product as such (Verify Arkansas Dept. of Health rules before stating any visiting-patient import rule).Same 50-state guides — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (secondary).

Non-neighbour note (to prevent a common error): Indiana, which does not border Missouri, is sometimes listed with Missouri's neighbours in error — it borders Illinois, not Missouri. Missouri's neighbours are the eight above only.

10.2.5 Border markets — Kansas City and St. Louis (what is actually evidenced vs what is asserted)

  • Kansas City, Missouri vs Kansas City, Kansas: The metro is a single urban area split by a state line. Secondary dispensary guides describe Kansas City, Missouri shops (e.g. State Line, Westside/I-35, Brookside, Westport, Midtown, Parkville locations) as convenient for Kansas-side shoppers, with drive-thru service widespread, and repeat the rule that "Kansas residents 21 and older may buy cannabis in Missouri but must not take it back into Kansas, where marijuana possession remains illegal" (K.S.A. 21-5706). — Source: https://herb.co/best-dispensaries/best-dispensaries-kansas-city — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (secondary commercial guide — market description only, not sales data). No official Missouri or Kansas sales-by-buyer-residence dataset was located; any claim of a specific dollar share or buyer count from Kansas is Unavailable in this draft. Do not present Kansas-buyer share estimates from dispensary marketing as market data. CPPC District 5 (Kansas City) highest 2025 sales ~$294m (Run 1 Part 4/6) is a district sales figure, not a Kansas-buyer figure — do not conflate them.
  • St. Louis, Missouri vs Illinois Metro East (Belleville, Fairview Heights, O'Fallon, Illinois — note: O'Fallon, Illinois ≠ O'Fallon, Missouri): Illinois has its own adult-use market, so the flow is two-way price/choice shopping rather than a one-way prohibition-arbitrage flow. Secondary summaries state Missouri's lower prices attract some southern-Illinois shoppers. — Source: https://ganjly.com/is-cannabis-legal-in-missouri-2026/ — Accessed 30 Oct 2026 (secondary). No official cross-border sales dataset for the St. Louis metro was located; price-comparison claims (tax-inclusive shelf prices, Illinois vs Missouri) are Verify against live dispensary menus / DOR / Illinois Dept. of Revenue data at final compile and must not be stated from this draft alone.
  • Other border corridors (no specific evidence located): Springfield, Missouri ↔ northwest Arkansas; Joplin ↔ Oklahoma/Kansas corner; St. Joseph, Missouri ↔ Kansas/Nebraska; Cape Girardeau ↔ southern Illinois/Kentucky; Hannibal/Clarksville ↔ Illinois; Maryville ↔ Iowa. No corridor-specific sales, enforcement or buyer data was located for these corridors — Unavailable. Do not invent a "border boom" narrative for any corridor beyond Kansas City and St. Louis, and for those two, limit claims to the sourced statements above.
  • Delivery across a border: Missouri licensed delivery (where offered by a dispensary) is to eligible Missouri addresses only in the secondary guides retrieved ("Delivery available: Home delivery to eligible Missouri addresses"). No Missouri rule located authorises delivery into another state. A delivery that crosses a state line is interstate transport (§10.2.1).

10.2.6 Interstate laws, agreements and compacts — verified negative (with one false-positive flagged)

  • No Missouri interstate cannabis compact, reciprocity agreement, tax-sharing agreement, or law-enforcement memorandum specific to cannabis was located after searches covering: Missouri DCR/DOR/AG releases, Missouri–Kansas, Missouri–Illinois, and general interstate-cannabis-agreement sources. The expected finding ("none") is therefore reported as a verified negative within the limits of those searches — final HTML should phrase it as "No interstate cannabis agreement involving Missouri was located as of 30 Oct 2026" rather than as a metaphysical certainty.
  • FALSE POSITIVE FLAGGED — do not reproduce: A low-authority aggregator page ("Cross-Border Issues in Missouri", State Regs Today) claims Missouri joined the "Midwestern States' Organization for Marijuana Reform (MOSMR)" and a "Tri-State Compact between Missouri, Kansas, and Oklahoma", and that MSHP maintains a dedicated marijuana-laws page. No official source, no other secondary source, and no plausible institutional record corroborates MOSMR or a Missouri–Kansas–Oklahoma cannabis compact; Kansas and Oklahoma have no adult-use market to compact about, and the page's other claims are demonstrably stale (it describes Missouri as medical-only, pre-2023). Treat MOSMR / Tri-State Compact as Unverified and almost certainly invented; do NOT list them as agreements. — Source of the claim (for the flag only): https://www.stateregstoday.com/health/marijuana-legalization/cross-border-issues-in-missouri — Accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • What DOES exist interstate (and is not a cannabis compact):

- Federal hemp-transport protection (§10.2.2) — federal law, not a Missouri agreement. - USDA federal hemp programme (Missouri producers under the federal plan) — federal administration, not a compact. - CANNRA membership/communication (DCR Office of Policy and Research "facilitates communications with the Cannabis Regulators Association") — a regulators' association, not a binding agreement, tax compact or reciprocity scheme. - General law-enforcement cooperation (HIDTA, interstate drug-task-force cooperation, MSHP liaison with neighbouring agencies) — generic drug-enforcement cooperation, not a cannabis-specific agreement; no cannabis-specific HIDTA memorandum was located for this draft (Verify against HIDTA / MSHP releases if the final HTML wishes to mention HIDTA at all). - Missouri's participation in the national track-and-trace vendor ecosystem (Metrc) is a vendor contract, not an interstate agreement; Metrc data-sharing across states, if any, was not established in this draft (Verify — do not assert interstate Metrc data sharing).

  • Federal transport ban for marijuana — statement of law: Marijuana remains a Schedule I controlled substance for adult-use purposes under the given federal baseline (medical Schedule III only), and interstate transport of marijuana is a federal offence (21 U.S.C. §§841, 844, 853 framework; Controlled Substances Act) regardless of the legality of the product at origin or destination under state law. This draft does not re-research federal criminal penalties; the parent should cite the CSA directly in final HTML rather than relying on this summary. State-law analogue: Missouri Chapter 579 trafficking/possession provisions (Run 1 Part 1) apply to unprotected marijuana, including marijuana brought into Missouri from another state without Article XIV protection — but a person who lawfully possesses in Missouri under Article XIV and then transports out does not commit a Missouri transport offence by the act of leaving; the offence is in the destination state / under federal law. That distinction should be stated carefully in final HTML to avoid implying Missouri police the destination.

10.2.7 Hemp shipments — Missouri-specific practical rules

  • Outbound: Missouri USDA-licensed hemp (compliant, tested, documented) may be shipped interstate under §10114 protection (§10.2.2). Processors/handlers of non-viable hemp (grain, fibre) were not MDA-regulated even in the state-programme era and are not USDA-producer-licensed as such — they ship as agricultural commodities, subject to destination-state product rules (notably FDA food/drug rules for CBD in food/supplements in interstate commerce).
  • Inbound: Hemp/hemp products produced under another state's USDA-approved plan or the federal plan may transit Missouri and be sold in Missouri subject to Missouri law at the time of sale — which, from 12 Nov 2026, means HB 2641's Article XIV treatment for covered intoxicating products (Run 1). A product that is lawful hemp in its origin state but is a covered intoxicating product under §195.900 may therefore be lawful to transit Missouri but unlawful to sell in Missouri outside Article XIV facilities after 12 Nov 2026. That transit-vs-sale distinction (federal protection vs destination retail law) is the single most important interstate-hemp point for the final HTML and should be illustrated with the §10114 quote in §10.2.2.
  • No Missouri hemp-export/import statistics (tonnage, value, destination states) were located for this draft — Unavailable. USDA NASS/FSA acreage figures (Run 1) are production figures, not shipment figures; do not present acreage as exports.

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

11.1 Testing: required tests, sampling, failed tests and recalls

11.1.1 Rule and who must test

  • Governing rule: 19 CSR 100-1.110, Testing. Justia text current through Register Vol. 50, No. 12, 16 June 2025. Source: https://regulations.justia.com/states/missouri/title-19/division-100/chapter-1/section-19-csr-100-1-110/ — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Verbatim, 100-1.110(1)(A): "Testing licensees shall test all lots of marijuana product produced by medical and marijuana facilities, including prerolls created at dispensary facilities but excluding seeds and plants, before it may be sold for use by a patient or consumer." Source: same Justia page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Lab qualification: ISO/IEC 17025 accreditation by an ILAC-recognised body, achieved within 1 year of approval to operate and maintained while certified; loss of accreditation must be reported to the department within 24 hours; a lab that loses accreditation must not test until approved in writing, and may be fined up to $1,000 per day of non-compliance. Source: 100-1.110(3)(B), same Justia page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Lab director: degree in a natural science (biology, chemistry, physics, engineering, environmental sciences) + at least 5 years in a regulated laboratory, OR another applicable degree + at least 10 years. Persons performing/overseeing sampling/testing: bachelor's in a natural science OR at least 2 years applicable experience. Source: 100-1.110(3)(A), same Justia page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Proficiency testing: annual programme from an ISO/IEC 17043-accredited provider; results to the department within 2 business days; failed proficiency tests must be repeated until acceptable; more than one failure may lead the department to require suspension of mandatory testing of the failed analyte(s). Source: 100-1.110(3)(C), same Justia page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Records: sampling/testing records retained at least 5 years; remaining sample material retained at least 30 days after testing, securely stored, available to the department, then disposed of under the waste rule. Source: 100-1.110(3)(D),(F), same Justia page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Inter-lab comparison: at department direction, a lab must provide remaining sample material up to twice a year to other facilities and receive material up to 10 times a year for testing; the department may use comparisons to investigate or take corrective action. Source: 100-1.110(3)(E), same Justia page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Methods: peer-reviewed, independently third-party-validated for cannabis, internally verified per AOAC Appendix J/K; the lab must report its method to the department before use and submit validations before offering the test to other licensees. Source: 100-1.110(4), same Justia page, accessed 30 October 2026.

11.1.2 Mandatory tests and failure limits (verbatim thresholds)

Test familyFailure rule in 100-1.110(7)Source, accessed 30 Oct 2026
Cannabinoid profile8 analytes: delta-9-THC, delta-9-THCA, CBD, CBDA, CBN, THCV, CBDV, delta-8-THC; reported dry-weight for dried unprocessed marijuana and prerolls, "as is" otherwise; acceptable limit = ≤15% deviation from the mean using at least 3 sampleshttps://regulations.justia.com/states/missouri/title-19/division-100/chapter-1/section-19-csr-100-1-110/
MicrobialFail if: total mycotoxins incl. aflatoxins + ochratoxin A >20 micrograms/kg; pathogenic E. coli or Salmonella detectable in 1 g; pathogenic Aspergillus species A. fumigatus, A. flavus, A. niger, or A. terreus detectable in 1 gSame Justia 100-1.110 page
Chemical residue (pesticides etc.)Fail if any listed analyte exceeds its action limit (ppm). The rule table lists ~60 analytes, e.g. Abamectin >0.5, Acephate >0.4, Bifenthrin >0.2, Chlorpyrifos >0.2, Imidacloprid >0.4, Myclobutanil >0.2, Permethrins (cumulative cis+trans) >0.2, Spinosad >0.2, Vitamin E acetate >0.2 (inhalables and concentrates only). Full table is in the rule; do not quote a shortened list as the whole list.Same Justia 100-1.110 page
Heavy metalsInhalation product fail if: total arsenic >0.2 ppm, cadmium >0.2, total chromium >0.6, lead >0.5, mercury >0.1. Infused-product fail if: arsenic >1.5, cadmium >0.5, chromium >2.0, lead >0.5, mercury >3.0Same Justia 100-1.110 page
Residual solventsFail above the inhalation / infused limits in the rule table (20 solvents), e.g. benzene >1 ppm inhalation / >2 infused; butanes >800 / >5,000; ethanol >1,000 / >5,000; methanol >250 / >3,000; propane >2,100 / >5,000; toluene >150 / >890Same Justia 100-1.110 page
Water activity / moistureFail if: dry unprocessed marijuana, prerolls, infused prerolls — water activity >0.65 aw, or moisture <5.0% or >15.0%; manually extracted concentrates that are not oil (hash, kief) — water activity >0.65 aw; all solid infused products — water activity >0.85 awSame Justia 100-1.110 page
Foreign matterFail if: >5.0% stems ≥3 mm diameter, or >2.0% other foreign matter (powdery mildew, mold, mites, hair, dirt, etc.); assessed on total representative sample after microbial/water-activity preparation, under high-power magnification, exterior + interiorSame Justia 100-1.110 page
TerpenesOptional for mandatory samples; if offered, must be in accreditation scope and proficiency scope. Not a mandatory pass/fail family in the rule text opened.Same Justia 100-1.110 page
  • Note on secondary summaries: a lab-industry summary states Missouri tests "61 chemicals and 20 residual solvents" (NCIA blog, secondary). That count is consistent in outline with the rule tables above but was NOT independently counted against the rule table in this pass — Verify before quoting "61/20" as exact. Source (secondary): https://THECANNABISINDUSTRY.ORG/navigating-missouris-stringent-cannabis-testing-requirements-a-deep-dive/ — accessed 30 October 2026.

11.1.3 Sampling rules

  • Sampling is by the testing licensee at harvest-lot or process-lot level; samples must be collected, stored and transported to mitigate contamination/degradation. Source: 100-1.110(5)(A), Justia page above, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Dry unprocessed marijuana: maximum 15 lbs per sampling unit; minimum 0.5% of a harvest lot sampled. Source: 100-1.110(5)(B)1, same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Extracts/concentrates/distillates/isolates: sample counts (1±0.2 g each) scale with process-lot weight — 4 samples at 0–0.50 lb, 8 at 0.51–1.5 lb, 12 at 1.51–3.00 lb, 16 at 3.01–6.00 lb, 20 at 6.01–10.00 lb, 32 at 10+ lb. Source: 100-1.110(5)(B)2, same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Vape cartridges, prerolls, infused prerolls and all other infused products/methods of administration: representative units — 2 units for 2–15 for sale, 3 for 16–50, 5 for 51–150, 8 for 151–500, 13 for 501–3,200, 20 for 3,201–35,000+. Product sold in a method of administration must be sampled AFTER processing into that method — this is the rule behind the 27 May 2026 CPC recall (see §11.1.5). Source: 100-1.110(5)(B)3–4, same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Prohibited sampling conduct: a testing licensee shall not desiccate samples, pre-test samples, select the best/most desirable material, or manipulate sample integrity/homogeneity; all increments must have equal chance of selection; sampling must be random. Source: 100-1.110(5)(C), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Chain of custody: the originating licensee must make the ENTIRE lot available, an employee of that licensee must be physically present to observe sampling, sampling occurs in a designated sample area, and both parties create a chain-of-custody record (licence numbers, names/addresses, product category, tag numbers, lot mass, units, tests requested, mandatory vs voluntary, re-sample/remediation flags, date/name/signature of both representatives), retained at least 5 years by both. Source: 100-1.110(6)(A)–(F), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Ordering limits: the originating licensee must order the tests needed, ensure processing is complete and lot size compliant, order only for product it produced, not order more than one test for the same lot without written department approval, ensure product is not on administrative hold or awaiting retest approval, and ensure remediation was department-approved. Source: 100-1.110(6)(G), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Sampling/manipulation violations: fines up to $100,000 and suspension or revocation. Source: 100-1.110(6)(H), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Reporting: within 10 days of collecting a sample AND within 24 hours of completing analysis, the testing licensee files a report in the statewide track-and-trace system showing pass/fail per required test, the certificate of analysis, and a photo of the sample received; system reporting must coincide with or precede notice to the originating facility. Source: 100-1.110(7)(B)–(C), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Passed product: must remain in the same lot; must not be repackaged into a new lot in the track-and-trace system; a copy of the certificate of analysis must go to every licensee receiving the lot (electronic copy permitted). Passed lots may not be retested to replace mandatory results without written department approval. Source: 100-1.110(6)(J)–(K), (7)(D), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Voluntary testing: permitted on material not collected under mandatory sampling rules, but results do NOT satisfy mandatory testing and must still be reported in the track-and-trace system. Source: 100-1.110(9), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.

11.1.4 Failed tests: hold, reanalysis, remediation, destruction

  • Administrative hold: the department places product that fails mandatory testing on administrative hold through the track-and-trace system. Source: 100-1.110(10)(A), Justia page above, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • 3-month / 15-day clocks: all failed product must be reanalysed, remediated, or destroyed within 3 months of initial failure. Within 15 days of failure the licensee must notify the department of its intent: (A) reanalysis of the previously tested sample; (B) remediation through a rule-allowed process; (C) destruction; or (D) request to remediate by a method not specifically allowed (written department approval required BEFORE acting). Source: 100-1.110(10)(B), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Reanalysis: may be by the original testing facility or a different one; if the sample passes reanalysis, a facility that did neither the initial analysis nor the reanalysis may sample the lot and test a new sample under mandatory-testing rules. Source: 100-1.110(10)(B)2.A, same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Remediation: after remediation, the original or a different testing facility resamples and retests under mandatory rules. Source: 100-1.110(10)(B)2.B, same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Heavy metals — NO remediation: "Product that fails testing for heavy metals may not be remediated." It stays on administrative hold pending disposal or, if department-approved, reanalysis. Source: 100-1.110(10)(C), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Transfer disclosure: if a licensee is permitted to transfer a lot that failed testing, it must notify the receiving licensee of the failed test. Source: 100-1.110(6)(I), same page, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Verify: the full list of rule-allowed remediation processes in 100-1.110(11) was NOT fully reproduced in this pass (page tail beyond line 621). Do not list specific remediation methods (e.g. for microbials vs pesticides) until that subsection is opened verbatim.

11.1.5 Recalls — Run 1 list verified and extended; no contradiction

Recall authority (verbatim), 19 CSR 100-1.100(4)(P): "The department may issue notice of marijuana product recall to licensees or the public if, in its judgment, any particular marijuana product presents a threat or potential threat to the health and safety of qualifying patients or consumers. All facilities are responsible for complying with recall notices. Recalled items must be immediately pulled from production or inventory and quarantined until such time as the department determines the item is safe, may be remediated, or must be destroyed." Source: https://regulations.justia.com/states/missouri/title-19/division-100/chapter-1/section-19-csr-100-1-100/ — accessed 30 October 2026.

  • Scale note (secondary only, do NOT present as DCR's own count): a secondary analysis of the official 2 September recall workbook counted 11,459 distinct package tags and 192 distinct licence numbers (188 dispensary + 4 manufacturing). Those are workbook records, not consumer units, purchases, or people exposed, and DCR had not publicly disclosed how many listed packages were sold. Source (secondary): https://w4news.org/missouri-cannabis-recall-192-licenses-11459-package-tags/ — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify against the DCR workbook before quoting.
  • Routine sampling initiative: DCR's July 2025 statewide sampling/testing initiative (unannounced inspections/investigations, mainly cultivation/manufacturing) with the Missouri State Public Health Laboratory as reference laboratory. Both May and September Aspergillus recalls cite it. Verify against the original DCR July 2025 release.
  • Consumer instruction pattern across notices: stop using the product; discard or return unused product to the dispensary where purchased; report adverse reactions via DCR complaint form or CannabisComplaints@health.mo.gov (public agency mailbox, already in Run 1 — reproduced here only as the recall-notice instruction, not as a personal contact). Sources: notices above, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Proposed-rule flag (NOT current law): DHSS filed proposed amendments to 19 CSR 100-1.100 on 1 October 2026 that would clarify recall procedures for product that cannot be properly traced back to Missouri-grown cannabis; comments 2 November–2 December 2026; final rule expected 2027. Do NOT apply these procedures to 2026 recalls. Source (official): https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/MODHSS/bulletins/42d722c — accessed 30 October 2026.

The DCR Licensee Contact List Dashboard is the authoritative list but is interactive/embedded and was NOT text-extractable in Run 1 or in this pass, so no official complete name list could be verified here.

  • Independence rule (settles one structural point): "No marijuana testing facility shall be owned by an entity or entities under substantially common control, ownership, or management as a cultivation facility, marijuana-infused products manufacturing facility, or dispensary facility." Source: 19 CSR 100-1.070(1)(F), https://regulations.justia.com/states/missouri/title-19/division-100/chapter-1/section-19-csr-100-1-070/ — accessed 30 October 2026.
