12.3.3 C.S. v. Lafayette — VERIFIED (narrowing, but narrow)

  • Full caption: C.S. v. Missouri State Highway Patrol Criminal Justice Information Services; Lafayette Prosecuting Attorney, No. SC100944 (Mo. 22 July 2025), 6–1, opinion by Judge Zel M. Fischer; Judge Robin Ransom dissenting. Source (official court summary): https://www.courts.mo.gov/file.jsp?id=222380 — accessed 30 October 2026. Note: the court summary itself states it "is not part of the opinion of the Court… should not be quoted or cited" — cite the OPINION (Justia), not the summary, in final HTML: https://law.justia.com/cases/missouri/supreme-court/2025/sc100944.html — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Holding (majority, as summarised by the Court and Justia): unlawful use of a weapon for possessing a firearm while knowingly possessing a controlled substance is NOT a "marijuana offense" for Article XIV §2 expungement; it is a firearms offence whose purpose is to prohibit conduct endangering others. The circuit court correctly expunged C.S.'s possession conviction but correctly denied expungement of the weapons conviction. The Court of Appeals had reversed (WD86693, 24 Dec 2024) — the Supreme Court reversed that reversal and affirmed the circuit court. Sources: court summary + Justia opinion pages above, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Facts (for context, not as a general rule): 2017 stop; 8.5 g marijuana + handgun in glovebox, 75.1 g in trunk; 2020 guilty pleas — Count I possession >35 g (class D felony, §579.015, 7 years) and Count II unlawful use of a weapon (§571.030.1(11), class E felony, 4 years), suspended execution + 5 years' probation; probation revoked 2021, incarcerated; June 2023 petition while incarcerated. Source: https://law.justia.com/cases/missouri/court-of-appeals/2024/wd86693.html — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Scope limit — do NOT generalise: C.S. decides ONE offence classification (firearm-while-possessing). It does NOT hold that all companion offences, all pre-2000 offences, or all drug-adjacent offences are ineligible, and secondary commentary reading it as a broad narrowing of "marijuana offense" goes beyond the holding. Flag that broader reading as commentary, Verify scope. Related appellate material: K.D.W. v. Missouri State Highway Patrol Records Repository, WD86993 (Mo. App. W.D. 9 Sept 2025) — Article XIV §2.10(8)(a) municipal-ordinance possession question; judgment affirmed (trial court denial of Article XIV expungement, grant of §610.140 expungement) — full holding NOT summarised here because the opinion excerpt opened addresses only the municipal-ordinance issue framing. Source: https://cases.justia.com/missouri/court-of-appeals/2025-wd86993.pdf?ts=1757431809 — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify K.D.W. holding before citing it for any proposition.
  • Federal collateral note (flag, not Missouri law): Lozano v. United States, SCOTUS petition No. 26-5456 filed 1 September 2026 (pending per Run 1), concerns federal sentencing treatment of a Missouri-expunged marijuana conviction (8th Cir. 27 Mar 2026, No. 24-3517). Do not state an outcome — none was located. Source: SCOTUS appendix above, accessed 30 October 2026.

12.4 Consumption lounges and events

  • Constitutional baseline: Article XIV §2.1 states the section "is not intended to allow for the public use of marijuana, driving while under the influence of marijuana, the use of marijuana in the workplace, or the use of marijuana by persons under twenty-one years of age." (Run 1, carried.) Source: Run 1, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Public-use penalty (secondary summaries, consistent): smoking/consuming in a public place = civil penalty up to $100 (civil infraction, not a criminal charge). "Public place" is broadly any area accessible to the public/substantial number of people — sidewalks, streets, parks, playgrounds, stadiums, businesses open to the public, common areas of multi-unit buildings, federal property. Source (secondary): https://legalclarity.org/can-you-smoke-pot-in-public-in-missouri/ — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify the $100 provision pinpoint in Article XIV §2 text before final (the secondary page cites §2 generally).
  • The licensed-area exception (the lounge opening): secondary summaries quote the constitutional exception for consumption in "an area licensed for such activity by the authorities having jurisdiction over the licensing and/or permitting of said activity." DCR's own FAQ formulation (via secondary reporting) is: "Per Article XIV sections 1 and 2, no qualifying patient or consumer shall consume marijuana in a public place, unless otherwise provided by law. Local governments may designate areas for public consumption. Please check with your local government if this is applicable." Source (secondary reporting of DCR FAQ): https://howspotdoing.com/locations/united-states/missouri/ — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify against the DCR FAQ page itself and the constitutional pinpoint.
  • Current position (no statewide lounge licence): "As of mid-2025, Missouri does not offer licensed cannabis consumption lounges, and public use on licensed premises remains prohibited." / "Missouri does not license cannabis consumption lounges. Some operate as private clubs or event spaces (BYOC), but public consumption remains prohibited under state law." Sources (secondary): https://legalclarity.org/can-i-smoke-weed-in-public-in-missouri/ ; https://cannaanswers.com/missouri-cannabis-rules/ — both accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Licensed-premises ban (rule): "Consumption of marijuana product on the licensed premises, including in any approved transport vehicles, is prohibited. All licensees shall post a sign at the employee and public access points to the facility that consumption of marijuana product is not allowed on the licensed premises." Source: 19 CSR 100-1.100(4)(M), Justia 100-1.100 page, accessed 30 October 2026. A dispensary therefore cannot itself authorise on-site consumption; any lounge would need a separate local licensing/permitting authority, not a DCR dispensary licence.
  • Local examples (illustrative, NOT a lounge register): Greenwood, MO ordinance (Ord. 2023-3056, 28 Mar 2023) prohibits consuming/smoking/burning marijuana in a public place/building "other than in an area licensed by the Board of Aldermen," and prohibits consumption in restaurants, liquor-licensed establishments, and places of public accommodation — showing the local-licensing mechanism in municipal text, not showing that Greenwood has licensed a lounge. Source (municipal code): https://ecode360.com/33262107 — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify whether any Missouri municipality has actually licensed a consumption area as of 30 Oct 2026 — none was confirmed in this pass, so the final HTML must NOT list operating lounges.
  • Events: NO Missouri rule authorising public cannabis consumption events was located. Private BYOC events on private property (not open to the public) sit outside the public-place ban on the face of the definitions, but sale/distribution at such events still requires a dispensary (see §11.7.1) and public-consumption, local-ordinance, and liquor-licence overlays apply. Industry events in Run 1 (MJ Unpacked Kansas City 14–15 Oct 2026; CannaCon Midwest St. Louis 28–29 Aug 2026, etc.) are trade events, NOT consumption events — do not present them as lounges or consumption venues. Source: Run 1 report, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Vehicles: consumption in a vehicle on a public road (driver or passenger, moving or parked) is prohibited under the public-place + DWI framework (see §§12.2, 12.3-adjacent summaries). Source (secondary): LegalClarity public-use pages above, accessed 30 October 2026. Verify pinpoint.

12.5 Cannabis tourism and visitor purchase rules

  • Adult-use visitors (21+): NO residency requirement. A valid government-issued photo ID proving age 21+ suffices — Missouri driver's licence, valid driver's licence from any state, U.S. passport/passport card (even if expired, if undamaged, per secondary summaries of DCR FAQ), or U.S. military/military-dependent ID. No visitor card, registration, or pre-approval required. Purchase/possession caps are the same as for residents: up to 3 oz dried unprocessed marijuana or equivalent per transaction and in possession at any time (equivalency 1 oz flower = 8 g concentrate = 800 mg THC infused; Run 1). Sources (secondary summaries of DCR General/Adult-Use FAQs): https://legalclarity.org/can-out-of-state-visitors-buy-recreational-weed-in-missouri/ ; https://legalclarity.org/is-missouri-a-recreational-state-what-the-law-says/ — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify against the DCR FAQ pages themselves before final — the FAQ pages were cited second-hand in this pass.
  • Medical visitors — possession protection vs purchase right (CONFLICT preserved, do not collapse): Article XIV §1.5(1) protects a person producing an equivalent out-of-state patient card from arrest/sanction for POSSESSING marijuana within Missouri patient limits. DCR FAQ reporting states "licensed dispensary facilities may accept out-of-state-issued patient cards," and a secondary reciprocity survey states the same provision "allows purchases at a licensed dispensary, but it does not cover growing." Run 1 Part 1, by contrast, recorded: "purchase right NOT established in rule text read — Verify." This draft therefore states BOTH: (a) possession protection under §1.5(1) is constitutional text; (b) dispensary acceptance of out-of-state cards is reported in DCR FAQ summaries but was NOT established in rule text opened in Run 1 or this pass. Practical advice in secondary sources — call ahead to confirm a dispensary will honour the card — is consistent with that uncertainty. Sources: https://www.drgreenrelief.com/blog/florida-mmj-card-reciprocity/?amp=1 ; Run 1 report — both accessed 30 October 2026. Medical advantage if honoured: up to 6 oz / 30 days (vs 3 oz adult-use transaction) and 4% medical tax (vs 6% adult-use), per secondary summaries — Verify purchase-limit interaction for out-of-state cards specifically.
  • Interstate transport warning (federal, not Missouri law): carrying cannabis across state lines remains a federal offence even if both states have legalised it; visitors must consume in Missouri before leaving. No Missouri rule authorises export. Source (secondary): LegalClarity visitor page above, accessed 30 October 2026. Kansas-side warning (practical, not legal advice): Kansas City MO vs Kansas City KS are separate jurisdictions; Kansas has not legalised adult-use marijuana, so crossing the state line into Kansas with Missouri-purchased product exposes the traveller to Kansas law — state this as a jurisdictional warning, not as a Kansas-law summary (Kansas law was not researched here).
  • Federal land in Missouri: national parks, military installations, federal buildings remain off-limits because adult-use marijuana remains federally Schedule I (Run 1 federal baseline). Source: Run 1 baselines, accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Tourism infrastructure: NO Missouri state cannabis-tourism programme, visitor licence, or consumption-tourism permit was located. Dispensary maps, delivery (where DHSS-approved), and trade events (Run 1 §6) are the tourism-adjacent facts available; do not invent "cannabis tours" as a licensed category. Verify any specific tour operator's licensing status before naming one — none is named here.

12.6 Landlord / tenant rules

  • Owner's general power (secondary summary of Amendment 3, consistent across sources): "The owner or lessee of any private property can prohibit the use, consumption, cultivation, distribution, processing, or sale of marijuana on its premises." Source (secondary law-firm summary): https://cvdl.net/missouris-new-marijuana-law-potentially-affects-employers-and-landlords/ — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify pinpoint in Article XIV §2 text before final.
  • Post-8 December 2022 lease limit (verbatim substance, same source): "any lease agreements that are executed after December 8, 2022 'may not prohibit a tenant from lawfully possessing and consuming marijuana by means other than smoking.'" Source: same CVDL summary, accessed 30 October 2026. Practical reading: a post-Amendment-3 lease MAY prohibit smoking (and cultivation/distribution/sale/processing as above) but may NOT ban a tenant's lawful possession + non-smoking consumption (e.g. edibles, tinctures). Pre-8 December 2022 leases are NOT covered by that sentence — do not apply it retroactively.
  • Corroboration (secondary): "Lease agreements made after December 8, 2022, may not prohibit a tenant from consuming cannabis by non-smoking means." / "Landlords retain the right to prohibit marijuana smoking or consumption within rental properties" (the latter statement, without the non-smoking qualifier, is OVERBROAD for post-2022 leases — do not quote it unqualified). Sources: https://legalclarity.org/is-it-illegal-to-smoke-weed-in-public-in-missouri/ ; https://legalclarity.org/can-i-smoke-weed-in-public-in-missouri/ — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • No protected-class shield for patient status alone: medical-marijuana patient status is not itself a protected class under Missouri/federal fair-housing law in secondary analyses; landlords may refuse tenants who smoke indoors or intend to violate federal law on the property; disability-related fair-housing arguments (where the underlying condition is a disability) are described as developing/untested, not settled. Attorney advice quoted in Greenway (2020, pre-Amendment-3): patient status is not public record; do not assume disclosure is required. That 2020 analysis PREDATES the post-2022 lease sentence — do not use it to override the lease sentence. Source (secondary, 2020): https://mogreenway.com/2020/06/26/rental-tenants-consume-cannabis-at-their-own-risk-even-as-a-patient/ — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • HOA/condo: associations may restrict use in common areas and, via governing documents, in units, subject to the same post-2022 non-smoking-consumption lease limit where it applies (lease vs ownership documents differ — Verify whether the lease sentence extends to HOA declarations; secondary HOA guides assert boards "may be able to forbid marijuana use in some cases" without resolving the non-smoking limit). Source (secondary): https://www.fsresidential.com/missouri/news-events/articles/what-the-new-missouri-cannabis-laws-mean-for-your/ — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Home cultivation + tenancy interaction: a tenant's home-grow right still requires landlord/property-owner permission in practice (owner may prohibit cultivation on the premises per the general power above), plus the enclosed-locked-facility + not-visible-from-public-place conditions (Run 1). A cultivation card does NOT override a lease cultivation ban. Verify pinpoint — this is the combined reading of the owner-power sentence + home-grow conditions, not a single quoted provision.
  • Public housing / federally assisted housing: NOT addressed in sources opened — federal "one strike" / public-housing drug policies are federal law and were not researched here. Unavailable — flag, do not summarise from memory.
  • Eviction procedure: Missouri eviction procedure (notice, unlawful-detainer) was not researched in this pass; do not state a notice period for cannabis-related evictions. Unavailable.

12.7 Gun ownership conflicts — FEDERAL conflict, not Missouri law

  • Missouri position: Article XIV does NOT restrict firearm purchase/possession by patients/consumers on its own terms, and DHSS does not inquire about firearm ownership in the patient-card application. No Missouri statute was located that disqualifies a medical cardholder from owning firearms under Missouri law alone. Source (secondary): https://legalclarity.org/can-you-own-a-gun-with-a-medical-card-in-missouri/ — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify against Article XIV text (no firearms provision found in summaries opened) before stating absolutely.
  • Federal prohibition (the conflict): 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) prohibits a person who is an "unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance" from possessing/receiving firearms or ammunition in/ affecting commerce. ATF has affirmed by open letter that marijuana users, including state-law medical users, fall within this category because marijuana remains unlawful federally. Source (secondary summaries of federal law): same LegalClarity page; https://www.joplinurgentcare.com/blog/guns-and-marijuana-an-illegal-cocktail — both accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Form 4473 (federal purchase process): ATF Form 4473, Firearms Transaction Record, asks: "Are you an unlawful user of, or addicted to, marijuana or any depressant, stimulant, narcotic drug, or any other controlled substance?" with a bolded warning that "The use or possession of marijuana remains unlawful under Federal law regardless of whether it has been legalized or decriminalized for medicinal or recreational purposes in the state where you reside." Answering "yes" = denial; answering "no" while using = false statement on a federal form (federal crime, per secondary summaries). Source: same pages, accessed 30 October 2026. Verify current Form 4473 wording against the ATF form itself before final (form revisions occur).
  • Run 1 federal-baseline interaction (critical nuance — do not flatten it): under the Run 1 baseline, state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026, while ADULT-USE remains Schedule I. Whether/how Schedule III medical status affects the §922(g)(3) "unlawful user" analysis, ATF guidance, or Form 4473 warnings has NOT been established in any source opened in this pass. Secondary gun-law pages still describe marijuana as Schedule I without addressing the 2026 medical Schedule III move. Therefore: present the conflict as it stood under the pre-2026 Schedule I framework, flag the Schedule III question as UNRESOLVED/Verify, and do NOT state that medical Schedule III has resolved the conflict or that it has not. This is the single most important flag in §12.7.
  • Missouri SAPA note (flag, do not overstate): Missouri enacted the Second Amendment Preservation Act (SAPA) in 2021, purporting to limit state enforcement of certain federal firearms laws. Its current enforceability/scope after federal litigation was NOT researched in this pass, and SAPA does not itself authorise a prohibited person to pass a federal NICS check. Do not present SAPA as resolving the 4473 conflict. Source (secondary mention): LegalClarity gun page above, accessed 30 October 2026. Verify SAPA status separately if the parent wants it.
  • Dubious-source exclusion: a "MO Free Press" item claiming a unanimous 9–0 U.S. Supreme Court ruling that the government cannot automatically ban marijuana users from owning firearms was found in search results but is NOT relied on here — no case name, docket, or date verifying a 2026 SCOTUS marijuana-firearms holding was located, and the item's description does not match any verified 2026 SCOTUS decision in sources opened. Do NOT cite it. If the parent wants post-2026 firearms litigation, verify via supremecourt.gov / 5th/8th Circuit dockets first. (Item seen, not cited: https://mofreepress.org/supreme-court-rules-government-cannot-automatically-ban-marijuana-users-from-owning-firearms/ — accessed 30 October 2026.)
  • Practical, non-legal-advice note: Missouri is not reported to cross-reference the patient-card database with gun-ownership records, and the patient database is described as access-limited (law-enforcement/dispensary verification only). That is a practice observation from secondary sources, NOT a legal protection — do not present it as immunity from federal enforcement.

12.8 Pet / veterinary products

  • Core finding: Missouri has NO veterinary marijuana authorisation, and hemp/CBD in pet food/treats is NOT approved in Missouri. Human legalisation does not extend to animals.
  • Missouri Veterinary Medical Association position (via MVMA legal-counsel memo, 5 Jan 2023, as relayed): "The legalization of recreational marijuana for humans in Missouri took effect December 8, 2022 and does not change the law as it applies to veterinarians and their patients. State laws legalizing marijuana use for people do not apply to cannabis use for animals. The FDA has not approved cannabis for any use in animals. With the limited exception of the Epidiolex product, there are no cannabis-based products approved for use in animals, and use or prescription by veterinarians for animal patients is prohibited." Source (secondary relay of MVMA memo): https://myemail.constantcontact.com/The-Legalization-of-Recreational-Marijuana.html?soid=1125900342009&aid=yBGqzAr6zBk — accessed 30 October 2026. Verify against the MVMA memo itself / Missouri Veterinary Practice Act before final; the relay is the only source opened.
  • Old "hemp extract" definition (historical, do NOT present as current pet law): former §195.207 defined "hemp extract" as ≤0.3% THC, ≥5% CBD, no other psychoactive substance, licensed via the Department of Agriculture, issuable only to patients with a DHSS card for intractable epilepsy; the Missouri Bureau of Narcotics and Dangerous Drugs stated (in a letter relayed by dvm360) there were "no Missouri statutes that allow for CBD products as defined above to be provided for veterinary use." That definition predates the current hemp framework and HB 2641 — cite it ONLY as historical context for why vets were warned, not as the current pet-CBD rule. Source: https://www.dvm360.com/view/letter-vetted-cbd-oil-not-legal-veterinary-use-all-states — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Current pet-food rule (official MDA): Missouri Department of Agriculture, Feed & Seed Program, "Hemp/CBD in Pet Food & Treats in Missouri" FAQ — "Can hemp products, like CBD oil, hemp oil, hemp seed or other by-products, be used in pet food (dog or cat) or treats? NO. Currently, the safety and efficacy of CBD oil or any other hemp or hemp by-product has not been reviewed for use in pet food and treats." / "Can I sell pet treats that contain Hemp/CBD in Missouri? NO. Hemp, hemp by-products, and extracts (e.g. CBD concentrates, isolates, or synthetics) are not approved for use in pet food and treats, as there is inadequate data surrounding the safety and utility…" CBD oil is regulated as a drug by FDA and must undergo new-animal-drug approval before distribution. Missouri Commercial Feed Licence ($35/year, 1 July–30 June) applies to in-state manufacturers/distributors/guarantors generally. Source (official MDA PDF): https://agriculture.mo.gov/plants/pdf/hemp-and-cbd-in-pet-food-missouri.pdf — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Directory caution: a March 2025 magazine item states the Veterinary Cannabis Society directory listed NO cannabis-friendly vets in Missouri at publication. That is a directory snapshot, not a legal rule, and is now dated. Source (secondary): https://viewer.joomag.com/the-evolution-magazine-march-2025/0204352001741123756/p20 — accessed 30 October 2026. Do not present "no vet will discuss CBD" as law — veterinarian CLIENT DISCUSSION rules under the Veterinary Practice Act were in the MVMA memo's table of contents but the memo text itself was not opened; Verify discussion-vs-prescription distinction before final.
  • THC toxicity note (general veterinary knowledge, NOT a Missouri rule): THC-containing marijuana products can poison pets (especially dogs); this draft states no dose/threshold and gives no treatment advice. Pet owners should seek veterinary care for suspected ingestion — that is general safety advice, not Missouri law.
  • HB 2641 interaction (flag): from 12 November 2026, hemp-derived cannabinoid products fall under the Article XIV licensed-marijuana framework for human products (Run 1). Whether/how that affects pet-product enforcement (MDA feed rules vs DCR marijuana rules) was NOT established in this pass — Verify; do not assume HB 2641 legalises or bans pet CBD beyond the MDA "NO" above.

12.9 Medical marijuana in schools

  • Core finding: Missouri law does NOT authorise use of medical marijuana ON school premises, nor does it require or permit district employees to administer it to students. What exists is district-level caregiver-administration policy, not a statewide school-administration statute.
  • District policy template (multiple Missouri districts, identical P 2871 text — e.g. Twin Rivers R-X, Southland C-9, West St. Francois County R-IV): "The law does not authorize the use of medical marijuana on school premises, nor does it require or permit district employees to administer the drug to students." / "Overall, the District restricts the administration of medications, including medical marijuana, unless administration cannot reasonably be accomplished outside of school hours." / Caregivers should administer before/after school hours (as early in the morning as possible or after school). In limited circumstances where a qualified student must receive the drug at school: caregiver provides the principal the student's valid authorisation + parent provides doctor's orders confirming daytime administration is required; if satisfied, the principal provides a private place where the CAREGIVER may administer; marijuana is NOT stored at school — caregiver brings it and leaves with any remainder. Procedure applies on school premises, school transportation, and school-sponsored activities inside/outside the district. Violation = revocation of the at-school opportunity + disciplinary action. Sources (district policies, official district postings): http://www.moconed.com/district/TwinRiversRX/district.php?pol=1721 ; http://www.moconed.com/district/SouthlandC9/district.php?pol=1721 ; http://moconed.com/district/WestStFrancoisCountyRIV/district.php?pol=1721 — all accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Status of that template: it is a MISSOURI SCHOOL BOARDS' ASSOCIATION-style model policy (P 2871) adopted district by district, NOT a statute. Do NOT cite "P 2871" as state law, and do NOT assume every Missouri district has adopted it identically — verify the specific district's policy for any individual case.
  • Constitutional/school-grounds overlay (Run 1, carried): use on school/correctional grounds is listed among "what is not legalised" in Run 1 Part 1; Article XIV §§1–2 state the sections are not intended to allow public use / workplace use / under-21 use. School grounds, school buses, and school-sponsored activities are treated as prohibited consumption locations in municipal and district texts (see e.g. Greenwood ordinance: possession/consumption/accessories prohibited on preschool/elementary/secondary school grounds, higher-education grounds, school buses, correctional grounds). Source: Run 1; https://ecode360.com/33262107 — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • No statewide "school nurse may administer" statute was located: a search for SB 1217 (2024) / school-nurse administration returned only district policies and student drug-testing policies, not an enacted school-administration statute. Verify 2024–2026 session bills if the parent wants a legislative-history note — do not cite SB 1217 as law (its status/content was not verified in this pass).
  • Minors + medical programme (Run 1 cross-reference, not changed here): minors access medical marijuana via a parent/guardian caregiver (Run 1 Part 1). That caregiver relationship is the mechanism behind the district caregiver-administration policies above. Student drug-testing policies (e.g. Sheldon R-VIII grades 7–12 random testing for co-curricular/parking) are district policies that treat a legitimate medical explanation via nurse/test-administrator verification as a negative result in their own procedures — district-specific, NOT a statewide rule. Source (district policy): https://www.sheldon.k12.mo.us/pages/uploaded_files/Sheldon-Drug Testing Policy. Drug Testing Policy Grades 7-12 (00279193xBDF2D) (3).docx.pdf — accessed 30 October 2026.
  • Federal overlay (flag): schools receiving federal funds operate under federal Drug-Free Schools requirements; that overlay was not researched in this pass and is NOT summarised here beyond the flag.
  • Employment bridge: school employees who are medical cardholders fall under §12.7's general medical-cardholder employment analysis (off-duty protection, on-duty/under-influence exception, federal-benefit exception) — plus district drug-free-school policies. Do not state a school-employee-specific marijuana rule beyond that bridge without verifying the district policy.

12.10 Cross-cutting consumer rules (for parent placement — do not duplicate Parts 1–3)

  • Employment / drug testing (summary, full analysis belongs with Part 1 employment + this Part's consumer lens): Missouri has NO general drug-testing statute (secondary consensus). Amendment 3 / Article XIV §1.7(15)-type medical protection (pinpoint Verify per Run 1): an employer may not discriminate against a person in hiring/termination/terms/conditions based on (a) status as a qualifying patient/primary caregiver with a valid ID card, including lawful off-premises use during nonworking hours (unless under the influence on premises/during hours), or (b) a positive drug test for marijuana components/metabolites — UNLESS failure to do so would cause the employer to lose a monetary/licensing benefit under federal law. Adult-use (non-medical) users have NO equivalent protection: employers need not permit/accommodate workplace use/possession/sale, and may discipline/fire for working under the influence or for a positive test under a drug-free-workplace policy. Workers' compensation benefit-denial drug-testing requirements sit in §287.120 (secondary summaries) — Verify pinpoint. Sources (secondary): https://missouristatecannabis.org/drug-testing-law ; https://legalclarity.org/can-missouri-employers-test-for-weed/ ; https://mocities.com/common/Uploaded%20files/Annual%20Conference/AC24%20Session%20Materials/7bMarijuanaUseAC24.pdf — all accessed 30 October 2026. Run 1 flagged §1.7(15) pinpoint as Verify — carried.
  • Gifting (Run 1 cross-reference): adults 21+ may deliver/distribute without consideration (gift) within possession limits; sale/gift to under-21s prohibited. Do not re-litigate gift limits here; Part 1 governs.
  • Public smoking vs non-smoking consumption: the $100 public-use civil penalty framework (§12.4) applies to consumption in public places regardless of form in secondary summaries (including edibles consumed visibly in public), but smoke-free-air laws (tobacco) and local ordinances add a second layer for SMOKING specifically (e.g. Greenwood: no smoking/burning where tobacco smoking is prohibited). Do not conflate the constitutional public-use ban with tobacco smoke-free law.
  • Tribal lands / tribal sources: NO official tribal cannabis code, compact, or tribal-lands rule for Missouri was located in this pass, and Missouri has no federally recognised tribal cannabis programme identified in sources opened. Unavailable — if the parent requires a tribal subsection, it needs a separate official-source search (tribal government sites, NCAI/BIA records), not inference from other states' compacts.
  • County/city variation reminder: St. Louis City vs St. Louis County, Kansas City MO vs KS, Springfield MO vs IL/OH, Columbia MO vs SC, Independence MO vs KS, St. Joseph MO vs MI, O'Fallon MO vs IL — every local rule cited in Parts 11–12 must be checked against the correct jurisdiction. No local rule in this draft is stated for the wrong-state namesake.

Conflicting figures — how they were handled (auditable rule)

Where official sources gave different figures for the same thing, this file picked the most authoritative and most recent official figure, stated which source was chosen, and listed the other figures here and at point of use — never silently. Examples: CPPC July 2026 study figures over the February 2026 tracker (licences); monthly-sum sales over rounded press totals (with the gap stated); DCR fee-schedule $28.92 over a secondary $25 (patient fee); truly-agreed $500 over introduced-bill $2,500 (§195.819); Greenway/MJBizDaily-corroborated May 2026 medical $10.83m over a single secondary table cell $10.083m. Where no figure was authoritative enough to choose (microbusiness cumulative revocations; current patient count; Des Peres ban outcome), no figure was chosen and the item is left Verify.

What Run 2 resolved (Run 1 Verify items closed, or materially narrowed)

Run 1 open questionRun 2 findingStatus
Does Missouri run its own USDA-approved hemp plan, or USDA-direct licensing?Missouri's state plan (approved Sept 2020, amended May 2021) was discontinued 31 Dec 2022; from 1 Jan 2023 producers licence directly through the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program. Confirmed by MU Extension, the MDA sampler page (all MDA sampler certifications expired 31 Dec 2022), and the USDA AMS plan list (Missouri = “USDA Hemp Producer License”, updated 17 Feb 2026). The MDA registration PDF dated 9 Sept 2022 is stale and must not be presented as a current list.Resolved — Run 1 confirmed
Are there any federally recognised tribes / tribal operations in Missouri?None. No federally recognised tribe headquartered in Missouri, no reservation or trust land in Missouri, and no tribal cannabis/hemp operation identified (BIA recognised-tribes list; State Historical Society of Missouri). State-recognition claims (e.g. Northern Cherokee Nation of the Old Louisiana Territory) were examined and not adopted — no Missouri statutory recognition process, and HB 1742 (2012) did not become law.Resolved as a verified negative
Does Missouri have a per se THC driving limit?No. §577.010 DWI requires operating “while in an intoxicated condition”; §577.012's 0.08% rule is alcohol-only. Enforcement is observation / FST / DRE / toxicology. No other state's ng/mL figure was imported.Resolved

Because comprehensive/medical/microbusiness applications were closed at 30 Oct 2026 (Run 1 Part 4), conversion realistically means acquiring an existing licence/business or waiting for a future round (microbusiness Round 3 ~77 from Dec 2026). Flat “felons can't work” statements must carry this qualification, and the position after 28 Mar 2027 is Verify.Narrowed, expiry Verify

Method caveats for Run 2 (read before relying on Run 2 citations)

The Parts 7–8 and Parts 9–10 workers reported that direct page fetches failed in their runs (transient worker errors, not content denials) and that some official citations therefore rest on search-return text from official domains, with a direct re-open listed as the follow-up. Parts 11–12 likewise mark several items as secondary-summary only (notably 100-1.200 scope and 100-1.090 retention). Those items are flagged Verify at point of use in Parts 7–12 and are not upgraded here. Before republication, re-open each official URL cited in Parts 7–12 — especially HB 2641 on revisor.mo.gov, DCR guidance of 23 Oct 2026, the USDA AMS plan list, and the SOS ballot pages — and re-check the DCR dashboards in a live browser, since text fetch cannot extract their rows.

Recommended follow-ups, in priority order

  1. Live-browser check of the DCR licensee, sales, transfer, business-change, commencement and item-approval dashboards; record the export/download position and the current patient, licence and sales totals on the check date.
  2. Revisor.mo.gov pinpoints: Article XIV §§1.7(6) and 1.7(15), §2.6(8) (280E subtraction after Schedule III), §§195.740–195.773 status, §§579.040 / 579.076, and any §610.141 auto-expungement text.
  3. OSCA / Missouri courts: current expungement total and backlog, on a dated basis.
  4. HB 2641 docket, MoHemp v. Kehoe, No. 2:26-cv-04170 (W.D. Mo.): motion to dismiss / injunction status — an injunction would rewrite Part 7 §7.5 and Part 1 §5.
  5. SOS: Grand Totals PDFs for 2018 and 2022 ballot measures, and the certified 2026 ballot list (re-checked after 3 Nov 2026 only as a dated update, without rewriting the 30 Oct 2026 history in Parts 0–6).
  6. USDA AMS / HeMP: current Missouri producer count, 2025 acreage, and any fee or form change; MDA: confirm no revived state hemp registration.
  7. ATF / Form 4473: current wording and any post-Schedule III guidance on §922(g)(3) for medical patients — the highest-risk unresolved consumer question in Part 12.
  8. DCR: agent-ID permanent rule after the 28 Mar 2027 emergency-amendment expiry; 100-1.200 research-facility final text and fees; official licensed-lab list.
